The young man might have stayed in the bank and succeeded to his aunt’s thousand a year, and everybody would have called him a very nice young fellow﻿—“clever, too.” But he had deliberately chosen, as Edward Dixon had said, to chuck his chances away for the sake of literature; piety and a sense of the main chance had alike pointed the way to a delicate course of wheedling, to a little harmless practicing on Miss Spurry’s infirmities, to frequent compliances of a soothing nature, and the “young ass” had been blind to the direction of one and the other. It seemed almost right that the vicar should moralize, that Edward Dixon should sneer, and that Mr. Gervase should grow purple with contempt. Men, Lucian thought, were like judges, who may pity the criminal in their hearts, but are forced to vindicate the outraged majesty of the law by a severe sentence. He felt the same considerations applied to his own case; he knew that his father should have had more money, that his clothes should be newer and of a better cut, that he should have gone to the university and made good friends. If such had been his fortune he could have looked his fellow-men proudly in the face, upright and unashamed. Having put on the whole armor of a first-rate West End tailor, with money in his purse, having taken anxious thought for the morrow, and having some useful friends and good prospects; in such a case he might have held his head high in a gentlemanly and Christian community. As it was he had usually avoided the reproachful glance of his fellows, feeling that he deserved their condemnation. But he had cherished for a long time his romantic sentimentalities about women; literary conventions borrowed from the minor poets and pseudo-medievalists, or so he thought afterwards. But, fresh from school, wearied a little with the perpetual society of barbarian though worthy boys, he had in his soul a charming image of womanhood, before which he worshipped with mingled passion and devotion. It was a nude figure, perhaps, but the shining arms were to be wound about the neck of a vanquished knight; there was rest for the head of a wounded lover; the hands were stretched forth to do works of pity, and the smiling lips were to murmur not love alone, but consolation in defeat. Here was the refuge for a broken heart; here the scorn of men would but make tenderness increase; here was all pity and all charity with loving-kindness. It was a delightful picture, conceived in the “come rest on this bosom,” and “a ministering angel thou” manner, with touches of allurement that made devotion all the sweeter. He soon found that he had idealized a little; in the affair of young Bennett, while the men were contemptuous the women were virulent. He had been rather fond of Agatha Gervase, and she, so other ladies said, had “set her cap” at him. Now, when he rebelled, and lost the goodwill of his aunt, dear Miss Spurry, Agatha insulted him with all conceivable rapidity. “After all, Mr. Bennett,” she said, “you will be nothing better than a beggar; now, will you? You mustn’t think me cruel, but I can’t help speaking the truth. *Write books!*” Her expression filled up the incomplete sentence; she waggled with indignant emotion. These passages came to Lucian’s ears, and indeed the Gervases boasted of “how well poor Agatha had behaved.”

“Never mind, Gathy,” old Gervase had observed. “If the impudent young puppy comes here again, we’ll see what Thomas can do with the horsewhip.”

“Poor dear child,” Mrs. Gervase added in telling the tale, “and she was so fond of him too. But of course it couldn’t go on after his shameful behavior.”

===Chinese===

迪克森先生（Mr. Dixon）身为教职人员，从高处着眼看待这个问题，只觉得整件事可叹可悯；但众口一词的意见是：贝内特是个不可救药的年轻疯子。老格瓦斯先生（Mr. Gervase）一听人提起这名字便气得脸色发紫，迪克森家的少爷小姐们则把这段历险当作绝妙的笑料。

「我一向认为他是头讨厌的小蠢驴，」爱德华·迪克森（Edward Dixon）说，「可我没料到他会把自己的前程那样扔掉。竟然说受不了银行！我倒希望他受得了面包和白水。据我所知，那班舞文弄墨的家伙捞到的就只有这个——丁尼生（Tennyson）、马克·吐温（Mark Twain）那一路人物除外。」

卢西安自然同情那不幸的贝内特，但这样的裁决终究只是人之常情。那年轻人本可以留在银行里，日后继承他姨妈一年一千镑的进项，那样人人都会称他一声很好的年轻人——「而且还聪明」。可他偏偏处心积虑地选择了——正如爱德华·迪克森所说——为了文学把前程统统扔掉；孝心与趋利之心原本都同样指给他一条路：一套细腻的哄诱功夫，在斯珀里小姐的软耳根上略施无害的手腕，频频献上顺耳的殷勤；而这头「小蠢驴」对二者的指引竟一概视而不见。于是教区牧师要发一番道德感慨，爱德华·迪克森要讪笑，格瓦斯先生要鄙夷得脸色发紫，便几乎是天经地义的了。男人，卢西安想，就像法官：心里尽可以怜悯罪犯，却不得不以重判来维护被冒犯的法律尊严。他觉得同样的考量也适用于他自己的处境；他知道父亲本该更有钱些，知道自己的衣裳本该更新、剪裁更好些，知道自己本该进大学、交些得力的朋友。倘若命运如此，他便能昂首直面他的同类，挺直而无愧。穿上伦敦西区一流裁缝缝制的全副甲胄，钱袋里有的是钱，为明天忧虑周到，再有几个有用的朋友与光明的前程——若是那样，他原可以在绅士的、基督徒的社群里高昂起头。而实际情况是，他素来躲开同伴们责备的目光，自觉理当受他们的判词。只是关于女人，他久久怀抱着自己那套浪漫的感伤情致；那是从小诗人和伪中世纪主义者那里借来的文学成规——至少他后来如此想。然而，刚出校门，被那群虽属正派却终究野蛮的男孩们没完没了的相伴弄得微微厌倦之后，他灵魂里确曾供着一帧妩媚的女性形象，他以交织的激情与虔敬在她面前礼拜。那或是一具裸身的形相，但那莹洁的双臂，原是为了环住被征服的骑士的颈项；负伤的恋人可以把头颅安放其上；那双手伸展出来，是为了行怜悯的善工；那含笑的唇，要低语的不只是爱情，还有败绩中的抚慰。这里是碎裂之心的避难所；在这里，男人的讥诮只会叫温存愈发滋长；这里有全部的怜悯、全部的仁爱与慈惠。这是一幅叫人心醉的图画，以「来吧，在这怀抱里安息」和「你是一位服侍人的天使」的路数孕育而成，又点染上几分魅惑，教虔诚愈发甘美。他很快便发现自己有点理想化了：在年轻贝内特那桩事里，男人们不过是轻蔑，女人们却狠毒。贝内特原对阿加莎·格瓦斯（Agatha Gervase）颇有几分好感，而她——照别的太太们的说法——也曾一门心思「打他的主意」。及至他忤逆了姨妈、失了亲爱的斯珀里小姐的欢心，阿加莎便以一切想得到的速度对他施以侮辱。「说到底，贝内特先生，」她说，「你充其量不过是个乞丐罢了；不是吗？你可别以为我狠心，可我忍不住要说实话。*去写书！*」她的表情补足了那句未说完的话；她义愤得浑身直颤。这些话传到了卢西安耳朵里，而格瓦斯一家还真以「可怜的阿加莎处置得多么得体」自诩。

「别往心里去，加蒂（Gathy），」老格瓦斯曾这样说。「那厚脸皮的小畜生要是再敢上门，咱们倒要看看托马斯能用马鞭把他抽成什么样。」

「可怜的宝贝孩子，」格瓦斯太太（Mrs. Gervase）转述这段故事时还补上一句，「她先前又是那么喜欢他。可他行为那么不体面，这事当然不能再继续下去了。」

===Original===

But Lucian was troubled; he sought vainly for the ideal womanly, the tender note of “come rest on this bosom.” Ministering angels, he felt convinced, do not rub red pepper and sulfuric acid into the wounds of suffering mortals.

===Chinese===

可卢西安苦恼了；他徒劳地寻索那理想的女性温柔，那声「来吧，在这怀抱里安息」的柔音。他深信不疑：服侍人的天使，总不至于把红辣椒和硫酸揉进受苦凡人的伤口。

===Original===

Then there was the case of Mr. Vaughan, a squire in the neighborhood, at whose board all the aristocracy of Caermaen had feasted for years. Mr. Vaughan had a first-rate cook, and his cellar was rare, and he was never so happy as when he shared his good things with his friends. His mother kept his house, and they delighted all the girls with frequent dances, while the men sighed over the amazing champagne. Investments proved disastrous, and Mr. Vaughan had to sell the grey manor-house by the river. He and his mother took a little modern stucco villa in Caermaen, wishing to be near their dear friends. But the men were “very sorry; rough on you, Vaughan. Always thought those Patagonians were risky, but you wouldn’t hear of it. Hope we shall see you before very long; you and Mrs. Vaughan must come to tea some day after Christmas.”

“Of course we are all very sorry for them,” said Henrietta Dixon. “No, we haven’t called on Mrs. Vaughan yet. They have no regular servant, you know; only a woman in the morning. I hear old mother Vaughan, as Edward will call her, does nearly everything. And their house is absurdly small; it’s little more than a cottage. One really can’t call it a gentleman’s house.”

Then Mr. Vaughan, his heart in the dust, went to the Gervases and tried to borrow five pounds of Mr. Gervase. He had to be ordered out of the house, and, as Edith Gervase said, it was all very painful; “he went out in such a funny way,” she added, “just like the dog when he’s had a whipping. Of course it’s sad, even if it is all his own fault, as everybody says, but he looked so ridiculous as he was going down the steps that I couldn’t help laughing.” Mr. Vaughan heard the ringing, youthful laughter as he crossed the lawn.

Young girls like Henrietta Dixon and Edith Gervase naturally viewed the Vaughans’ comical position with all the high spirits of their age, but the elder ladies could not look at matters in this frivolous light.

===Chinese===

此外还有沃恩先生（Mr. Vaughan）的事。他是左近的一位乡绅，多年来，凯尔梅恩（Caermaen）的全部上流人物都在他的餐桌上饮宴过。沃恩先生有一位第一流的厨子，他的酒窖罕有其匹，而把好东西同朋友们分享，正是他至高的快乐。他的母亲替他管家，母子俩频频开办舞会，令所有的姑娘们欢天喜地，男人们则对着那惊人的香槟赞叹不置。后来投资一败涂地，沃恩先生只得卖掉河畔那座灰色的庄园宅邸。他和母亲搬进凯尔梅恩一幢小小的拉毛水泥新式别墅，为的是离亲爱的朋友们近些。可男人们只会说：「太遗憾了；你这下可真不好受，沃恩。早就觉得那些巴塔哥尼亚人的买卖悬乎，可你偏听不进去。盼着不久还能常常见面；圣诞过后，你同沃恩太太（Mrs. Vaughan）务必挑个日子来吃茶。」

「我们当然都为他们难过，」亨利埃塔·迪克森（Henrietta Dixon）说。「不，我们还没有去拜访沃恩太太。你要知道，他们家连个正经仆人都没有；只有一个女人早间来帮半日工。我听说，老沃恩妈妈——爱德华就爱这么称呼她——差不多样样都亲手做。再说他们的房子小得可笑；比一间村舍大不了多少。实在没法管那叫绅士的宅邸。」

后来，沃恩先生心堕尘埃，走到格瓦斯家，想向格瓦斯先生借五英镑。人家只得下逐客令把他轰出门去；正如伊迪丝·格瓦斯（Edith Gervase）所说，那场面要多难受有多难受；「他出去的样子才滑稽呢，」她补上一句，「活像挨了鞭子的狗。当然，这是很不幸的，就算像大家说的那样全怪他自己，可他下台阶那模样实在太可笑了，我忍不住笑出了声。」沃恩先生穿过草坪时，听见了那串清亮的、年轻人的笑声。

像亨利埃塔·迪克森和伊迪丝·格瓦斯这样的年轻姑娘，自然是以她们芳龄的十足兴头来看待沃恩一家可笑的处境；但年长的太太们，却不能以这样轻浮的眼光看待此事。

===Original===

“Hush, dear, hush,” said Mrs. Gervase, “it’s all too shocking to be a laughing matter. Don’t you agree with me, Mrs. Dixon? The sinful extravagance that went on at Pentre always *frightened* me. You remember that ball they gave last year? Mr. Gervase assured me that the champagne must have cost *at least* a hundred and fifty shillings the dozen.”

“It’s dreadful, isn’t it,” said Mrs. Dixon, “when one thinks of how many poor people there are who would be thankful for a crust of bread?”

“Yes, Mrs. Dixon,” Agatha joined in, “and you know how absurdly the Vaughans spoilt the cottagers. Oh, it was really wicked; one would think Mr. Vaughan wished to make them above their station. Edith and I went for a walk one day nearly as far as Pentre, and we begged a glass of water of old Mrs. Jones who lives in that pretty cottage near the brook. She began praising the Vaughans in the most fulsome manner, and showed us some flannel things they had given her at Christmas. I assure you, my dear Mrs. Dixon, the flannel was the very best quality; no lady could wish for better. It couldn’t have cost less than half-a-crown a yard.”

“I know, my dear, I know. Mr. Dixon always said it couldn’t last. How often I have heard him say that the Vaughans were pauperizing all the common people about Pentre, and putting everyone else in a most unpleasant position. Even from a worldly point of view it was very poor taste on their part. So different from the *true* charity that Paul speaks of.”

“I only wish they had given away nothing worse than flannel,” said Miss Colley, a young lady of very strict views. “But I assure you there was a perfect orgy, I can call it nothing else, every Christmas. Great joints of prime beef, and barrels of strong beer, and snuff and tobacco distributed wholesale; as if the poor wanted to be encouraged in their disgusting habits. It was really impossible to go through the village for weeks after; the whole place was poisoned with the fumes of horrid tobacco pipes.”

“Well, we see how that sort of thing ends,” said Mrs. Dixon, summing up judicially. “We had intended to call, but I really think it would be impossible after what Mrs. Gervase has told us. The idea of Mr. Vaughan trying to sponge on poor Mr. Gervase in that shabby way! I think meanness of that kind is so hateful.”

===Chinese===

「嘘，亲爱的，嘘，」格瓦斯太太说，「这事实在太骇人了，绝不是可以拿来取笑的事。你不赞成我的话吗，迪克森太太？彭特雷（Pentre）那种种罪孽的奢靡，向来叫我*胆战心惊*。你记得他们去年开的那场舞会吗？格瓦斯先生向我担保，那香槟少说也得*一百五十先令*一打。」

「真是骇人听闻，不是吗，」迪克森太太（Mrs. Dixon）说，「一想到世上还有多少穷人，连一小块干面包皮都会感恩戴德呢？」

「是啊，迪克森太太，」阿加莎插进来说，「再说你知道沃恩家把那些村舍穷户惯成了什么样子，简直没了体统。哦，那真是作孽；人家还当沃恩先生存心要把他们抬到不配的身份上去呢。有一天伊迪丝跟我出去散步，差不多走到彭特雷，我们向住在溪边那座漂亮村舍里的老琼斯太太（Mrs. Jones）讨了一杯水。她便开始用最肉麻的腔调称颂沃恩一家，还给我们看了他们圣诞时节送她的几件法兰绒衣物。我向你担保，亲爱的迪克森太太，那法兰绒是顶好的货色；哪位太太也不能指望更好的了。少说也得半克朗一码。」

「我知道，亲爱的，我知道。迪克森先生就一直说这长不了。我不知听他讲过多少回：沃恩家把彭特雷一带的贫民全惯成了叫花子，还把旁人统统置于极不愉快的境地。就算从世俗的眼光看，他们的趣味也实在要不得。同保罗所说的*真*慈善，差得太远了。」

「我巴不得他们送的不过是法兰绒，再没有更糟的了，」科利小姐（Miss Colley）说——她是位见识极严正的小姐。「可我向你担保，每年圣诞那里都有一场十足的狂欢，我只能这么称呼它。大块大块的上等牛肉，一桶一桶的烈性啤酒，还有成批散发的鼻烟和烟丝；倒好像巴不得穷人们那些恶心的嗜好再受点鼓励似的。之后一连几个星期，那村子简直没法走；整个地方都被烟斗里可怕的烟气熏毒了。」

「好了，这一套的结局如何，我们都看见了，」迪克森太太以法官断案的姿态作了总结。「我们本来是打算去拜访的，可听了格瓦斯太太这番话，我当真觉得再去是不可能的了。沃恩先生竟想用那种寒碜手段揩格瓦斯先生的油，亏他想得出！我觉得那种小家子气的下作最叫人憎恶。」

===Original===

It was the practical side of all this that astonished Lucian. He saw that in reality there was no high-flown quixotism in a woman’s nature; the smooth arms, made he had thought for caressing, seemed muscular; the hands meant for the doing of works of pity in his system, appeared dexterous in the giving of “stingers,” as Barnes might say, and the smiling lips could sneer with great ease. Nor was he more fortunate in his personal experiences. As has been told, Mrs. Dixon spoke of him in connection with “judgments,” and the younger ladies did not exactly cultivate his acquaintance. Theoretically they “adored” books and thought poetry “too sweet,” but in practice they preferred talking about mares and fox-terriers and their neighbors.

They were nice girls enough, very like other young ladies in other country towns, content with the teaching of their parents, reading the Bible every morning in their bedrooms, and sitting every Sunday in church amongst the well-dressed “sheep” on the right hand. It was not their fault if they failed to satisfy the ideal of an enthusiastic dreamy boy, and indeed, they would have thought his feigned woman immodest, absurdly sentimental, a fright (“never wears stays, my dear”) and *horrid*.

At first he was a good deal grieved at the loss of that charming tender woman, the work of his brain. When the Miss Dixons went haughtily by with a scornful waggle, when the Miss Gervases passed in the wagonette laughing as the mud splashed him, the poor fellow would look up with a face of grief that must have been very comic; “like a dying duck,” as Edith Gervase said. Edith was really very pretty, and he would have liked to talk to her, even about fox-terriers, if she would have listened. One afternoon at the Dixons’ he really forced himself upon her, and with all the obtuseness of an enthusiastic boy tried to discuss the “Lotus Eaters” of Tennyson. It was too absurd. Captain Kempton was making signals to Edith all the time, and Lieutenant Gatwick had gone off in disgust, and he had promised to bring her a puppy “by Vick out of Wasp.” At last the poor girl could bear it no longer:

===Chinese===

这一切之中令卢西安惊愕的，是那实利的一面。他看明白了：女人的天性里其实没有什么高蹈的堂吉诃德气；那他本以为生来为了爱抚的光滑臂膀，看来筋腱虬结；那在他的体系里本该用来行怜悯善工的双手，递起「扎人的狠话」来倒是熟练无比——正如巴恩斯（Barnes）可能会说的——而那含笑的唇，讪笑起来毫不费力。他在亲身的经历里也不见得更走运。如前文所述，迪克森太太提起他时总要联系到「天谴」，年轻小姐们也并不怎么有心结纳他的交情。在理论上，她们「崇拜」书籍，认为诗歌「甜极了」；可实际上，她们更爱谈母马、猎狐㹴和左邻右舍。

她们都是够好的姑娘，同其他乡镇里的其他小姐毫无二致：安于父母的教诲，每天早晨在卧房里读一段圣经，每个礼拜天坐在教堂里衣着体面的「绵羊」中间，居右手的位置。她们满足不了一个热心而耽梦的少年的理想，这不是她们的过错；而且说实话，她们只会觉得他虚构的那位女人不成体统、伤感得可笑、是个怪胎（「从来不爱穿紧身褡，亲爱的」），而且*可怕极了*。

起初，痛失那位由他头脑孕育的、妩媚而温存的女人，他还着实悲伤了一阵。每当迪克森家的小姐们傲然走过、轻蔑地把身子一扭，每当格瓦斯家的小姐们坐着四轮马车经过、泥浆溅上他一身时还笑声朗朗，这可怜的家伙便仰起一张愁苦的脸——那模样想必非常滑稽；用伊迪丝·格瓦斯的话说，「像一只垂死的鸭子」。伊迪丝人其实生得很美，倘若她肯听，他也愿意同她谈谈，哪怕是谈猎狐㹴。一天下午在迪克森家，他当真硬凑到她跟前，带着热心少年全副的迟钝，试图同她讨论丁尼生的《食莲人》（“Lotus Eaters”）。那场面实在太荒唐了。肯普顿上尉（Captain Kempton）一直在向伊迪丝使眼色，加特威克中尉（Lieutenant Gatwick）早已愤然走开——而他还答应过要给她带一只小狗来，「维克（Vick）配黄蜂（Wasp）生的」。最后，那可怜的姑娘再也忍受不住了：

===Original===

“Yes, it’s very sweet,” she said at last. “When did you say you were going to London, Mr. Taylor?”

It was about the time that his disappointment became known to everybody, and the shot told. He gave her a piteous look and slunk off, “just like the dog when he’s had a whipping,” to use Edith’s own expression. Two or three lessons of this description produced their due effect; and when he saw a male Dixon or Gervase approaching him he bit his lip and summoned up his courage. But when he descried a “ministering angel” he made haste and hid behind a hedge or took to the woods. In course of time the desire to escape became an instinct, to be followed as a matter of course; in the same way he avoided the adders on the mountain. His old ideals were almost if not quite forgotten; he knew that the female of the *bête humaine*, like the adder, would in all probability sting, and he therefore shrank from its trail, but without any feeling of special resentment. The one had a poisoned tongue as the other had a poisoned fang, and it was well to leave them both alone. Then had come that sudden fury of rage against all humanity, as he went out of Caermaen carrying the book that had been stolen from him by the enterprising Beit. He shuddered as he thought of how nearly he had approached the verge of madness, when his eyes filled with blood and the earth seemed to burn with fire. He remembered how he had looked up to the horizon and the sky was blotched with scarlet; and the earth was deep red, with red woods and red fields. There was something of horror in the memory, and in the vision of that wild night walk through dim country, when every shadow seemed a symbol of some terrible impending doom. The murmur of the brook, the wind shrilling through the wood, the pale light flowing from the mouldered trunks, and the picture of his own figure fleeing and fleeting through the shades; all these seemed unhappy things that told a story in fatal hieroglyphics. And then the life and laws of the sunlight had passed away, and the resurrection and kingdom of the dead began. Though his limbs were weary, he had felt his muscles grow strong as steel; a woman, one of the hated race, was beside him in the darkness, and the wild beast woke within him, ravening for blood and brutal lust; all the raging desires of the dim race from which he came assailed his heart. The ghosts issued out from the weird wood and from the caves in the hills, besieging him, as he had imagined the spiritual legion besieging Caermaen, beckoning him to a hideous battle and a victory that he had never imagined in his wildest dreams. And then out of the darkness the kind voice spoke again, and the kind hand was stretched out to draw him up from the pit. It was sweet to think of that which he had found at last; the boy’s picture incarnate, all the passion and compassion of his longing, all the pity and love and consolation. She, that beautiful passionate woman offering up her beauty in sacrifice to him, she was worthy indeed of his worship. He remembered how his tears had fallen upon her breast, and how tenderly she had soothed him, whispering those wonderful unknown words that sang to his heart. And she had made herself defenseless before him, caressing and fondling the body that had been so despised. He exulted in the happy thought that he had knelt down on the ground before her, and had embraced her knees and worshipped. The woman’s body had become his religion; he lay awake at night looking into the darkness with hungry eyes; wishing for a miracle, that the appearance of the so-desired form might be shaped before him. And when he was alone in quiet places in the wood, he fell down again on his knees, and even on his face, stretching out vain hands in the air, as if they would feel her flesh. His father noticed in those days that the inner pocket of his coat was stuffed with papers; he would see Lucian walking up and down in a secret shady place at the bottom of the orchard, reading from his sheaf of manuscript, replacing the leaves, and again drawing them out. He would walk a few quick steps, and pause as if enraptured, gazing in the air as if he looked through the shadows of the world into some sphere of glory, feigned by his thought. Mr. Taylor was almost alarmed at the sight; he concluded of course that Lucian was writing a book. In the first place, there seemed something immodest in seeing the operation performed under one’s eyes; it was as if the “makeup” of a beautiful actress were done on the stage, in full audience; as if one saw the rounded calves fixed in position, the fleshings drawn on, the voluptuous outlines of the figure produced by means purely mechanical, blushes mantling from the paint-pot, and the golden tresses well secured by the wigmaker. Books, Mr. Taylor thought, should swim into one’s ken mysteriously; they should appear all printed and bound, without apparent genesis; just as children are suddenly told that they have a little sister, found by mamma in the garden. But Lucian was not only engaged in composition; he was plainly rapturous, enthusiastic; Mr. Taylor saw him throw up his hands, and bow his head with strange gesture. The parson began to fear that his son was like some of those mad Frenchmen of whom he had read, young fellows who had a sort of fury of literature, and gave their whole lives to it, spending days over a page, and years over a book, pursuing art as Englishmen pursue money, building up a romance as if it were a business. Now Mr. Taylor held firmly by the “walking-stick” theory; he believed that a man of letters should have a real profession, some solid employment in life. “Get something to do,” he would have liked to say, “and then you can write as much as you please. Look at Scott, look at Dickens and Trollope.” And then there was the social point of view; it might be right, or it might be wrong, but there could be no doubt that the literary man, as such, was not thought much of in English society. Mr. Taylor knew his Thackeray, and he remembered that old Major Pendennis, society personified, did not exactly boast of his nephew’s occupation. Even Warrington was rather ashamed to own his connection with journalism, and Pendennis himself laughed openly at his novel-writing as an agreeable way of making money, a useful appendage to the cultivation of dukes, his true business in life. This was the plain English view, and Mr. Taylor was no doubt right enough in thinking it good, practical common sense. Therefore when he saw Lucian loitering and sauntering, musing amorously over his manuscript, exhibiting manifest signs of that fine fury which Britons have ever found absurd, he felt grieved at heart, and more than ever sorry that he had not been able to send the boy to Oxford.

===Chinese===

「是啊，写得很美，」她终于开口道。「泰勒先生，您方才说，您几时动身去伦敦？」

那大约正是他的失意闹得无人不知的时节，这一箭正中要害。他哀怜地望了她一眼，悄悄溜走了——用伊迪丝自己的话说，「活像挨了鞭子的狗」。诸如此类的教训，两三课下来便产生了应有的效果；远远望见哪位迪克森家或格瓦斯家的男性朝他走来，他便咬住嘴唇，鼓起勇气。可一旦发现什么「服侍人的天使」，他便赶紧躲到树篱后面，或者一头钻进树林。天长日久，逃避的欲望成了一种本能，一种理所当然的惯例；正如他避让山上的蝰蛇一样。旧日的理想即使没有忘尽，也差不多忘光了；他知道，人类这种畜生（*bête humaine*）的雌性，同蝰蛇一样，十之八九是要螫人的，所以他见了她的踪迹便退避三舍，却并无特别的怨怼之心。一个有恶毒的舌头，正如另一个有恶毒的毒牙，两个都还是敬而远之为妙。再后来，便是那场对全人类的骤然暴怒——那天他夹着那部曾被胆识过人的贝特偷走的书，走出凯尔梅恩。想起自己曾何等逼近疯狂的边缘——那时他眼中充满血，大地仿佛燃着火——他不寒而栗。他记得自己怎样仰望天际，天空洇满猩红的斑块；大地是深红的，红的树林，红的田野。这记忆里有一种恐怖的东西，还有那荒夜穿行于朦胧乡野的幻象——每一道暗影都像是某种迫近的可怖厄运的符记。溪水的呜咽，穿林而过的风的尖啸，从朽坏的树干上流泻的幽光，还有他自己那穿行于重重暗影之间、奔逃而去的身影；这一切都像是不幸之物，以宿命的象形文字讲述着一个故事。随后，白昼的生命与法度逝去了，死者的复活与王国开始了。他四肢虽已困乏，却感到肌肉钢铁般硬挺起来；黑暗里，一个女人——那可憎种族中的一个——就在他身旁，他体内的野兽醒了，饕餮着血腥与粗暴的欲念；他身上涌来的，是他所自出的那个幽昧种族全部狂乱的渴念。鬼影从那片魔异的树林里、从山间的洞穴中涌出，围困住他——一如他曾想象灵界军团围困凯尔梅恩——召唤他去打一场狰狞的战役，去赢一场他在最狂乱的梦里也从未想象过的胜利。然后，黑暗中那温存的声音又开口了，那温存的手伸了出来，把他从深渊里拉起。想到他终于寻得的那个她，心头便觉甘甜：那是少年图画中的血肉之身，是他全部渴望的激情与怜悯，全部的慈心、爱与慰藉。她，那个美丽而炽烈的女子，把她的美当作祭献呈给他——她确实当得起他的礼拜。他记得自己的眼泪怎样落在她的胸上，她怎样温柔地抚慰他，用那些奇妙的、未知的、唱进他心里的话语低低絮语。她还在他面前解除了自己的防卫，爱抚着、温存着那具久遭鄙弃的身体。一想到自己曾跪倒在她面前的地上、抱住她的双膝礼拜，他便欣喜若狂。那女人的身体成了他的宗教；他夜里清醒地躺着，用饥饿的眼睛望进黑暗，祈盼一个奇迹，好让那具朝思暮想的形貌在他眼前成形。而当他独自在林中僻静之处时，他又跪下去，甚至整个人伏在地上，向着空中伸出徒劳的双手，仿佛要触摸她的肌肤。那些日子里，他父亲注意到他外衣的内袋总是塞满纸页；他会看见卢西安在果园深处一处隐秘的荫蔽地方来回踱步，捧读他那束手稿，把纸页放回去，又抽出来。他疾走几步，又恍然出神地驻足，凝望空中，仿佛要透过尘世的暗影，望进他思绪虚构出的某个荣光之境。泰勒先生（Mr. Taylor）见了这情形，几乎要惊慌起来；他当然断定，卢西安是在写书。首先，亲眼看着这道工序在眼前操作，总有些不体面；这就好比一个美女演员当着满场观众在台上化装；好比亲眼看见浑圆的小腿绑上衬垫，肉色的紧身衣一条条穿上，那具妖娆身段的曲线全凭纯然机械的手段做出，绯红的双颊从颜料罐里晕染而出，金色的发辫由制假发的师傅牢牢钉好。书籍，泰勒先生认为，应当神秘地游进人们的视野；它们露面时就该是印好装订好的全璧，看不出来历；正如孩子们忽然被告知，他们有了一个小妹妹，是妈妈在花园里捡到的。可卢西安不只是在写作；他分明是如痴如狂、神魂颠倒；泰勒先生看见他双手向天扬起，又以一种古怪的姿态垂下头颅。牧师开始担心，自己的儿子莫非像他读到过的那些法国狂人：那些年轻人对文学怀着一股癫狂，把一生一世都赔了进去，一页纸耗上几天，一本书拖上几年，追逐艺术如同英国人追逐金钱，营造一部传奇仿佛经营一桩买卖。而泰勒先生是坚决信奉「手杖论」的；他认为文人应当有一门真正的职业，一份人生里扎实的营生。「先找点事做，」他很想这样对儿子说，「然后你爱写多少写多少。看看司各特（Scott），看看狄更斯（Dickens）和特罗洛普（Trollope）。」此外还有社会这一层考虑；它或许对，或许不对，但毫无疑问，文人之为文人，在英国社交界是不怎么被看重的。泰勒先生熟读他的萨克雷（Thackeray）；他记得那位老潘丹尼斯少校（Major Pendennis）——社交界的化身——对侄子的营生可是绝口不夸的。连沃灵顿（Warrington）提起自己同新闻界的瓜葛都有几分羞于承认；潘丹尼斯本人则公然嘲笑自己写小说，说那不过是赚钱的雅道，是他真正的人生大业——结交公爵——的一件有用的附庸。这是明白晓畅的英国式见解，泰勒先生认定这是好端端的、实用的常识，多半也没错。因此，每当他看见卢西安闲逛游荡、对着手稿缠绵出神，显出那种种不列颠人历来觉得可笑的高贵癫狂的明证，他便打心底里难过，也更加懊悔没能把这孩子送进牛津。

===Original===

“B. N. C. would have knocked all this nonsense out of him,” he thought. “He would have taken a double First like my poor father and made something of a figure in the world. However, it can’t be helped.” The poor man sighed, and lit his pipe, and walked in another part of the garden.

But he was mistaken in his diagnosis of the symptoms. The book that Lucian had begun lay unheeded in the drawer; it was a secret work that he was engaged on, and the manuscripts that he took out of that inner pocket never left him day or night. He slept with them next to his heart, and he would kiss them when he was quite alone, and pay them such devotion as he would have paid to her whom they symbolized. He wrote on these leaves a wonderful ritual of praise and devotion; it was the liturgy of his religion. Again and again he copied and recopied this madness of a lover; dallying all days over the choice of a word, searching for more exquisite phrases. No common words, no such phrases as he might use in a tale would suffice; the sentences of worship must stir and be quickened, they must glow and burn, and be decked out as with rare work of jewelry. Every part of that holy and beautiful body must be adored; he sought for terms of extravagant praise, he bent his soul and mind low before her, licking the dust under her feet, abased and yet rejoicing as a Templar before the image of Baphomet. He exulted more especially in the knowledge that there was nothing of the conventional or common in his ecstasy; he was not the fervent, adoring lover of Tennyson’s poems, who loves with passion and yet with a proud respect, with the love always of a gentleman for a lady. Annie was not a lady; the Morgans had farmed their land for hundreds of years; they were what Miss Gervase and Miss Colley and the rest of them called common people. Tennyson’s noble gentlemen thought of their ladies with something of reticence: they imagined them dressed in flowing and courtly robes, walking with slow dignity; they dreamed of them as always stately, the future mistresses of their houses, mothers of their heirs. Such lovers bowed, but not too low, remembering their own honor, before those who were to be equal companions and friends as well as wives. It was not such conceptions as these that he embodied in the amazing emblems of his ritual; he was not, he told himself, a young officer, “something in the city,” or a rising barrister engaged to a Miss Dixon or a Miss Gervase. He had not thought of looking out for a nice little house in a good residential suburb where they would have pleasant society; there were to be no consultations about wallpapers, or jocose whispers from friends as to the necessity of having a room that would do for a nursery. No glad young thing had leant on his arm while they chose the suite in white enamel, and china for “our bedroom,” the modest salesman doing his best to spare their blushes. When Edith Gervase married she would get mamma to look out for two really good servants, “as we must begin quietly,” and mamma would make sure that the drains and everything were right. Then her “girl friends” would come on a certain solemn day to see all her “lovely things.” “Two dozen of everything!” “Look, Ethel, did you ever see such ducky frills?” “And that insertion, isn’t it quite too sweet?” “My dear Edith, you *are* a lucky girl.” “All the underlinen specially made by Madame Lulu!” “What delicious things!” “I hope he knows what a prize he is winning.” “Oh! do look at those lovely ribbon-bows!” “You darling, how happy you must be.” “Real Valenciennes!” Then a whisper in the lady’s ear, and her reply, “Oh, *don’t*, Nelly!” So they would chirp over their treasures, as in Rabelais they chirped over their cups; and everything would be done in due order till the wedding-day, when mamma, who had strained her sinews and the commandments to bring the match about, would weep and look indignantly at the unhappy bridegroom. “I *hope* you’ll be kind to her, Robert.” Then in a rapid whisper to the bride: “Mind, you *insist* on Wyman’s flushing the drains when you come back; servants are so careless and dirty too. Don’t let him go about by himself in Paris. Men are so *queer*, one never knows. You *have* got the pills?” And aloud, after these *secreta*, “God bless you, my dear; goodbye! *cluck*, *cluck*, goodbye!”

===Chinese===

「要是进了 B. N. C.（牛津布雷齐诺斯学院），这一套胡思乱想早给敲打干净了，」他想。「他会像我那可怜的父亲一样拿下双重甲等，在世上挣出个名头。不过，事已至此，无可奈何了。」这可怜的人叹了口气，点上烟斗，到花园另一头散步去了。

可他对症候的诊断错了。卢西安新动笔的那本书正躺在抽屉里无人理会；他埋头从事的，是一项隐秘的工程，而他从内袋里取出的那些手稿，日夜不曾离身。他睡觉时把它们贴着心口，独处无人的时候还要亲吻它们，向它们奉献那种敬意——正如奉献给它们所象征的那个她。他在这些纸页上写下了一套奇妙的颂赞与奉献的仪轨；那是他宗教的礼仪书。他一遍又一遍地誊写、再誊写这情人的癫狂；整日价在一个字的斟酌上流连，搜求更加精微的措辞。寻常的字眼，他在小说里或许会用的那些句子，都不足够；礼拜的文句必须跃动、必须振奋，必须灼灼发光、熊熊燃烧，还须用珍奇的珠宝工艺装点起来。那圣洁而美丽的身体的每一部分都必须受到礼赞；他搜求铺张扬厉的颂词，把灵魂与心智在她面前深深俯伏，舔舐她足下的尘土，卑屈而又狂喜，像圣殿骑士礼拜巴弗灭（Baphomet）的偶像。尤其令他狂喜的是，他知道自己的迷醉里没有一丝寻常或凡俗之物；他不是丁尼生诗中那种炽热崇拜的恋人——怀着激情，却又怀着骄矜的敬重，怀着的永远是绅士对淑女之爱。安妮不是什么淑女；摩根家（the Morgans）在那片土地上耕作了数百年；她们正是格瓦斯小姐、科利小姐以及那一路人所称的平民。丁尼生笔下高贵的绅士们想起他们的心上人时，总带着几分含蓄：他们想象她们身披飘逸而雍容的长袍，迈着端凝的缓步；他们梦想她们永远庄严华贵，是将来宅邸的女主人，是他们子嗣的母亲。这样的恋人也要躬身行礼，却不肯躬得太低，因为记得自家的体面——他们俯身面对的，是将来要做妻子的、也是平权的伴侣与朋友的人。他在自己仪轨那些惊人的徽记里所寄托的，绝非这类构想；他对自己说，他不是什么青年军官，不是「城里有点营生的」先生，也不是同迪克森小姐或格瓦斯小姐订了婚、前程看好的出庭律师。他从没想过要在体面的近郊住宅区觅一所小巧玲珑的好宅子，好让他们享有愉快的社交；不会有关于墙纸的商议，也不会有朋友们打趣的耳语，说务必备下一间将来用得上的育儿室。也从不曾有过哪个欢天喜地的小美人挽着他的手臂，挑选白珐琅的成套家具，挑「我们卧房」用的瓷器，而拘谨的店员在一旁竭力顾全他们的羞红。伊迪丝·格瓦斯出嫁的时候，会请妈妈代为物色两个真正的好仆人——「咱们开头总得从简」——妈妈还会把排水沟和一切设施都查验妥帖。然后，在某个隆重的日子，她的「闺中密友」们会来看她全部的「可爱物件」。「每样都打了两打！」「看哪，埃塞尔（Ethel），你见过这么俏的花褶边吗？」「还有那嵌花布条，是不是甜得不得了？」「亲爱的伊迪丝，你可*真*是个幸运的姑娘。」「贴身内衣全是露露夫人（Madame Lulu）特制的！」「多叫人心醉的东西啊！」「但愿他知道他赢得的是怎样一件珍宝。」「哦！快看那些可爱的缎带蝴蝶结！」「你这小宝贝，你该多幸福啊。」「真正的瓦朗谢讷（Valenciennes）花边！」随后是附在小姐耳畔的一句耳语，以及她的回答：「哎呀，*别说了*，内莉（Nelly）！」她们便是这样对着这些珍宝叽叽喳喳，恰如拉伯雷（Rabelais）笔下的人们对着酒杯叽叽喳喳；万事都按部就班地办妥，直到婚期来临——到那一天，为促成这门亲事绷紧了筋络、也拉扯了诫命的妈妈，会洒下眼泪，愤愤地瞪视那不幸的新郎。「我*希望*你好好待她，罗伯特（Robert）。」随即凑近新娘飞快地耳语：「记着，回来时你*务必*坚持让怀曼（Wyman）家的人来冲排水沟；仆人们粗心，还邋遢。别放他一个人在巴黎乱跑。男人*靠不住*，谁说得准呢。药丸你*带上了*吧？」这一番私房话（*secreta*）过后，才扬声说道：「上帝保佑你，亲爱的；再见啦！*啧*，*啧*，再见啦！」

===Original===

There were stranger things written in the manuscript pages that Lucian cherished, sentences that burnt and glowed like “coals of fire which hath a most vehement flame.” There were phrases that stung and tingled as he wrote them, and sonorous words poured out in ecstasy and rapture, as in some of the old litanies. He hugged the thought that a great part of what he had invented was in the true sense of the word occult: page after page might have been read aloud to the uninitiated without betraying the inner meaning. He dreamed night and day over these symbols, he copied and recopied the manuscript nine times before he wrote it out fairly in a little book which he made himself of a skin of creamy vellum. In his mania for acquirements that should be entirely useless he had gained some skill in illumination, or limning as he preferred to call it, always choosing the obscurer word as the obscurer arts. First he set himself to the severe practice of the text; he spent many hours and days of toil in struggling to fashion the serried columns of black letter, writing and rewriting till he could shape the massive character with firm true hand. He cut his quills with the patience of a monk in the scriptorium, shaving and altering the nib, lightening and increasing the pressure and flexibility of the points, till the pen satisfied him, and gave a stroke both broad and even. Then he made experiments in inks, searching for some medium that would rival the glossy black letter of the old manuscripts; and not till he could produce a fair page of text did he turn to the more entrancing labour of the capitals and borders and ornaments. He mused long over the Lombardic letters, as glorious in their way as a cathedral, and trained his hand to execute the bold and flowing lines; and then there was the art of the border, blossoming in fretted splendor all about the page. His cousin, Miss Deacon, called it all a great waste of time, and his father thought he would have done much better in trying to improve his ordinary handwriting, which was both ugly and illegible. Indeed, there seemed but a poor demand for the limner’s art. He sent some specimens of his skill to an “artistic firm” in London; a verse of the “Maud,” curiously emblazoned, and a Latin hymn with the notes pricked on a red stave. The firm wrote civilly, telling him that his work, though good, was not what they wanted, and enclosing an illuminated text. “We have great demand for this sort of thing,” they concluded, “and if you care to attempt something in this style we should be pleased to look at it.” The said text was “Thou, God, seest me.” The letter was of a degraded form, bearing much the same relation to the true character as a “churchwarden gothic” building does to Canterbury Cathedral; the colours were varied. The initial was pale gold, the *h* pink, the *o* black, the *u* blue, and the first letter was somehow connected with a bird’s nest containing the young of the pigeon, who were waited on by the female bird.

“What a pretty text,” said Miss Deacon. “I should like to nail it up in my room. Why don’t you try to do something like that, Lucian? You might make something by it.”

“I sent them these,” said Lucian, “but they don’t like them much.”

===Chinese===

在卢西安珍藏的这些手稿纸页上，还写着更为古怪的东西：有些句子灼灼燃烧，像「至猛烈焰之火炭」。有些词句落笔时便蜇人、便震颤，铿锵的字眼在心醉神迷中倾泻而出，宛如古时的几篇连祷文。他抱定一个念头，暗自得意：他所杜撰的东西，大半在真正的意义上是隐秘的（occult）：一页接一页，尽可以读给未入会的人听，而不泄露其中内义。他日夜梦想着这些符记，把手稿誊了又誊，前后誊过九遍，才以工整的字迹写进一本小书——那书是他亲手用一张乳白的犊皮纸（vellum）装成的。出于对全然无用之技艺的癖好，他在泥金饰彩之术（illumination）上颇下过一番功夫——他更愿意称之为「细描」（limning），正如在诸艺之中他总拣更冷僻的一样，用词也总拣更冷僻的。他先从严峻的正文学起；为习成黑体字（black letter）那密密的行列，他熬掉了许多时日，写了又写，直到能以坚定真确的手写出那端凝的字体。他像缮写室里的修士一样耐心地削着鹅毛笔，修削笔尖、改造笔锋，调试笔端的压力与弹力，直到那支笔令他满意，落笔的笔画既阔且匀。随后他又在墨水上做种种试验，寻求一种足以匹敌古手稿那乌亮墨色的介质；直到能写出一页像样的正文，他才转向大写字母、边框与纹饰那更令人心醉的劳作。他对伦巴第体字母冥想良久——那种字母自有一种大教堂般的辉煌——训练手腕写出遒劲流走的线条；然后便是边框的艺术，让雕琢的华彩绕着书页四围绽开。他的表姐迪肯小姐（Miss Deacon）说这一切全是天大的浪费时间，他父亲则认为，他还不如去练练日常的笔迹，那字既丑又没人认得。事实上，细描这门艺术，市场需求实在可怜。他把自己几件拿手的样品寄给伦敦一家「艺术商行」：一节《莫德》（“Maud”）的诗句，饰彩得古意盎然，还有一首拉丁文圣歌，音符点在一行红色的谱线上。商行客客气气地回信说，他的作品虽好，却不是他们想要的，随信附来一页饰彩经文。「这一类的东西我们需求很大，」信末写道，「您若愿意照这个样式试作一件，我们将乐于过目。」那节经文是「神啊，你鉴察我」。字母是一种堕落的形式，与真正字体的关系，恰如「教堂执事式哥特」建筑之于坎特伯雷大教堂（Canterbury Cathedral）；色彩则五花八门。首字母是淡金色，*h* 是粉色，*o* 是黑色，*u* 是蓝色，而那个首字母不知怎么又同一只鸟巢连在一起，巢里有几只雏鸽，由母鸽伺候着。

「多好看的经文啊，」迪肯小姐说。「我真想把它钉在我房间里。卢西安，你何不照这个样子也做一件呢？说不定还能靠它挣点什么呢。」

「我寄给他们的就是这两件，」卢西安说，「可他们不太喜欢。」

===Original===

“My dear boy! I should think not! Like them! What were you thinking of to draw those queer stiff flowers all round the border? Roses? They don’t look like roses at all events. Where do you get such ideas from?”

“But the design is appropriate; look at the words.”

“My dear Lucian, I can’t read the words; it’s such a queer old-fashioned writing. Look how plain that text is; one can see what it’s about. And this other one; I can’t make it out at all.”

“It’s a Latin hymn.”

“A Latin hymn? Is it a Protestant hymn? I may be old-fashioned, but *Hymns Ancient and Modern* is quite good enough for me. This is the music, I suppose? But, my dear boy, there are only four lines, and who ever heard of notes shaped like that: you have made some square and some diamond-shape? Why didn’t you look in your poor mother’s old music? It’s in the ottoman in the drawing-room. I could have shown you how to make the notes; there are crotchets, you know, and quavers.”

Miss Deacon laid down the illuminated *Urbs Beata* in despair; she felt convinced that her cousin was “next door to an idiot.”

===Chinese===

「我亲爱的孩子！我说呢，他们当然不喜欢！还喜欢呢！你脑子里想什么，竟在边框四周画那些古里古气的僵花？玫瑰？反正怎么看也不像玫瑰。你这些念头是从哪儿来的？」

「可这纹样是切题的；您看看这些词句。」

「亲爱的卢西安，这些词句我根本认不得；这字体古怪得太旧派了。看看那张经文多么明白；一眼就看得出讲的是什么。再说另外这件；我压根儿看不懂。」

「那是一首拉丁文圣歌。」

「拉丁文圣歌？是新教的圣歌吗？我兴许是老派，可《古今圣诗》（*Hymns Ancient and Modern*）对我已经够好的了。这是乐谱，我猜？可是，我亲爱的孩子，总共只有四条线呀，谁听说过那样形状的音符：你把一些写成方的，一些写成菱形？你怎么不去翻翻你可怜的母亲留下的旧乐谱？就在客厅那张软垫箱凳里。我本可以教你怎么画音符；有四分音符，你知道，还有八分音符。」

迪肯小姐绝望地放下了那页饰彩的《蒙福之城》（*Urbs Beata*）；她深信不疑，她这位表弟「离白痴只有一门之隔」。

===Original===

And he went out into the garden and raged behind a hedge. He broke two flowerpots and hit an apple-tree very hard with his stick, and then, feeling more calm, wondered what was the use in trying to do anything. He would not have put the thought into words, but in his heart he was aggrieved that his cousin liked the pigeons and the text, and did not like his emblematical roses and the Latin hymn. He knew he had taken great pains over the work, and that it was well done, and being still a young man he expected praise. He found that in this hard world there was a lack of appreciation; a critical spirit seemed abroad. If he could have been scientifically observed as he writhed and smarted under the strictures of “the old fool,” as he rudely called his cousin, the spectacle would have been extremely diverting. Little boys sometimes enjoy a very similar entertainment; either with their tiny fingers or with mamma’s nail scissors they gradually deprive a fly of its wings and legs. The odd gyrations and queer thin buzzings of the creature as it spins comically round and round never fail to provide a fund of harmless amusement. Lucian, indeed, fancied himself a very ill-used individual; but he should have tried to imitate the nervous organization of the flies, which, as mamma says, “can’t really feel.”

===Chinese===

于是他走进花园，在树篱后面大发雷霆。他摔碎了两只花盆，又用手杖狠狠抽了一顿苹果树，随后心平气和了些，寻思起做任何事究竟还有什么用处。这念头他不会说出口，可他心里着实委屈：表姐喜欢那些鸽子、喜欢那页经文，却不喜欢他那些象征的玫瑰与拉丁圣歌。他知道自己在这活计上煞费了苦心，知道它做得很好，而他终究年轻，还指望着夸赞。他发现，在这个冷酷的世界上，赏识是何等匮乏；一股苛评之风似乎四处弥漫。倘若他在「老蠢货」——他对表姐的粗鲁称呼——的那些针砭下辗转哀号时，能被人以科学的方式观察一番，那景象必定妙趣横生。小男孩们有时就享受着如出一辙的娱乐：或用小小的手指，或用妈妈的指甲剪，一点一点褫夺一只苍蝇的翅膀和腿。那小东西滑稽地打着旋儿转个不停，作出种种古怪的回旋、发出细细的嗡嗡声，永远能提供一宗无害的笑料。卢西安确实自视为一个饱受虐待的人；可他真该学学苍蝇的神经构造才是——用妈妈们的话说，它们「其实并不真的知道疼」。

===Original===

But now, as he prepared the vellum leaves, he remembered his art with joy; he had not laboured to do beautiful work in vain. He read over his manuscript once more, and thought of the designing of the pages. He made sketches on furtive sheets of paper, and hunted up books in his father’s library for suggestions. There were books about architecture, and medieval iron work, and brasses which contributed hints for adornment; and not content with mere pictures he sought in the woods and hedges, scanning the strange forms of trees, and the poisonous growth of great water-plants, and the parasite twining of honeysuckle and briony. In one of these rambles he discovered a red earth which he made into a pigment, and he found in the unctuous juice of a certain fern an ingredient which he thought made his black ink still more glossy. His book was written all in symbols, and in the same spirit of symbolism he decorated it, causing wonderful foliage to creep about the text, and showing the blossom of certain mystical flowers, with emblems of strange creatures, caught and bound in rose thickets. All was dedicated to love and a lover’s madness, and there were songs in it which haunted him with their lilt and refrain. When the book was finished it replaced the loose leaves as his constant companion by day and night. Three times a day he repeated his ritual to himself, seeking out the loneliest places in the woods, or going up to his room; and from the fixed intentness and rapture of his gaze, the father thought him still severely employed in the questionable process of composition. At night he contrived to wake for his strange courtship; and he had a peculiar ceremony when he got up in the dark and lit his candle. From a steep and wild hillside, not far from the house, he had cut from time to time five large boughs of spiked and prickly gorse. He had brought them into the house, one by one, and had hidden them in the big box that stood beside his bed. Often he woke up weeping and murmuring to himself the words of one of his songs, and then when he had lit the candle, he would draw out the gorse-boughs, and place them on the floor, and taking off his nightgown, gently lay himself down on the bed of thorns and spines. Lying on his face, with the candle and the book before him, he would softly and tenderly repeat the praises of his dear, dear Annie, and as he turned over page after page, and saw the raised gold of the majuscules glow and flame in the candlelight, he pressed the thorns into his flesh. At such moments he tasted in all its acute savour the joy of physical pain; and after two or three experiences of such delights he altered his book, making a curious sign in vermilion on the margin of the passages where he was to inflict on himself this sweet torture. Never did he fail to wake at the appointed hour, a strong effort of will broke through all the heaviness of sleep, and he would rise up, joyful though weeping, and reverently set his thorny bed upon the floor, offering his pain with his praise. When he had whispered the last word, and had risen from the ground, his body would be all freckled with drops of blood; he used to view the marks with pride. Here and there a spine would be left deep in the flesh, and he would pull these out roughly, tearing through the skin. On some nights when he had pressed with more fervor on the thorns his thighs would stream with blood, red beads standing out on the flesh, and trickling down to his feet. He had some difficulty in washing away the bloodstains so as not to leave any traces to attract the attention of the servant; and after a time he returned no more to his bed when his duty had been accomplished. For a coverlet he had a dark rug, a good deal worn, and in this he would wrap his naked bleeding body, and lie down on the hard floor, well content to add an aching rest to the account of his pleasures. He was covered with scars, and those that healed during the day were torn open afresh at night; the pale olive skin was red with the angry marks of blood, and the graceful form of the young man appeared like the body of a tortured martyr. He grew thinner and thinner every day, for he ate but little; the skin was stretched on the bones of his face, and the black eyes burnt in dark purple hollows. His relations noticed that he was not looking well.

===Chinese===

可如今，备制犊皮纸页之际，他怀着喜悦想起了自己的手艺；美的劳作不曾枉费。他把手稿通读了一遍，构思起书页的图样。他在偷偷摸摸的纸片上画草图，到父亲的书房里翻检书籍以供取法。那里有讲建筑的、讲中世纪铁艺的、讲黄铜纪念牌的书，都为装饰提供了暗示；他还不满足于单纯的图画，又到树林和树篱间寻访，细察树木的奇形，大水生植物有毒的滋长，还有忍冬与泻根寄生的缠绕。在一次这样的漫游中，他发现了一种红土，可以制成颜料；又在某种蕨类油润的汁液里找到一味配料，他相信它能使他的黑墨更加乌亮。他的书通体以符写成，他也以同样的象征精神装饰它：让奇妙的枝叶在字里行间蔓生，让某些神秘之花绽放，还有种种怪异生物的徽记，被捉住、被缚在玫瑰丛中。一切皆奉献给爱情与情人的癫狂；书里有几支歌，那调子与叠句萦绕在他心头不去。书成之后，它便取代散页，成为他日夜不离的伴侣。每日三次，他默诵他的仪轨：或寻到林中最僻静的处所，或登上自己的房间；从他那凝定专注、心醉神迷的目光看来，父亲还以为他仍在那可疑的写作工序里苦苦用功。夜里，他为这场奇异的求爱设法醒来；黑暗中起身、点上蜡烛之后，他有一套独特的仪式。在离宅子不远一面陡峭荒野的山坡上，他陆续砍下过五大枝多刺尖利的荆豆。他把它们一枝一枝带回屋里，藏进床边立着的那口大箱子。他常常在哭泣中醒来，喃喃自语着歌中的词句；点上蜡烛之后，他便抽出荆豆枝，铺在地上，脱去睡袍，轻轻把自己安放到那张荆棘与尖刺的床上。他伏卧着，蜡烛与书册摆在面前，柔声温存地诵着对亲爱的、亲爱的安妮的赞辞；一页页翻过去，看着大写字母上凸起的金色在烛光里灼灼生焰，他便把棘刺压进自己的皮肉。在这样的时刻，他体味着肉体剧痛那全部尖锐的滋味；两三度领受这般极乐之后，他改定了他的书，在那些将要对自己施加这甜蜜苦刑的段落边上，用朱砂画了一个古怪的记号。约定的时辰，他从未误过醒来；意志的强力冲破睡眠的一切沉滞，他便起身——含泪而欢欣——恭恭敬敬地把荆棘之床铺在地上，把他的疼痛同他的颂赞一同献上。低语完最后一个字、从地上起来时，他周身已斑斑点点尽是血珠；他惯于怀着骄傲检视这些印记。这里那里，会有棘刺深深断在皮肉里，他便把它们粗暴地拔出来，撕开皮肉。有些夜里，他压在荆棘上格外狂热，大腿上便血流如注，血珠颗颗立在皮肉上，一路淌到脚边。血渍颇费清洗，为的是不留痕迹、惹起仆人的注意；过了一些时候，功课本毕之后，他便不再回到床上去。他有一床深色的旧毯子做被盖，便用它裹住赤裸而流血的身子，躺倒在坚硬的地板上，心满意足地在这份欢愉的账上再添一笔酸痛的安息。他遍体鳞伤，白日里愈合的，夜里又重新绽开；苍白的橄榄色皮肤上满是血的红肿印记，那青年颀长优美的身形，俨然一具受难的殉道者的躯体。他一天比一天消瘦，因为吃得极少；面颊的皮肤紧绷在骨上，黑黑的眼睛在深紫的眼窝里燃烧。亲眷们都察觉，他气色不佳。

===Original===

“Now, Lucian, it’s perfect madness of you to go on like this,” said Miss Deacon, one morning at breakfast. “Look how your hand shakes; some people would say that you have been taking brandy. And all that you want is a little medicine, and yet you won’t be advised. You know it’s not my fault; I have asked you to try Dr. Jelly’s Cooling Powders again and again.”

He remembered the forcible exhibition of the powders when he was a boy, and felt thankful that those days were over. He only grinned at his cousin and swallowed a great cup of strong tea to steady his nerves, which were shaky enough. Mrs. Dixon saw him one day in Caermaen; it was very hot, and he had been walking rather fast. The scars on his body burnt and tingled, and he tottered as he raised his hat to the vicar’s wife. She decided without further investigation that he must have been drinking in public-houses.

“It seems a mercy that poor Mrs. Taylor was taken,” she said to her husband. “She has certainly been spared a great deal. That wretched young man passed me this afternoon; he was quite intoxicated.”

“How very said,” said Mr. Dixon. “A little port, my dear?”

“Thank you, Merivale, I will have another glass of sherry. Dr. Burrows is always scolding me and saying I *must* take something to keep up my energy, and this sherry is so weak.”

===Chinese===

「我说，卢西安，你这样熬下去纯粹是发疯，」一天早晨吃早饭时，迪肯小姐说。「瞧你的手抖成什么样了；换了别人，要说你一直在喝白兰地呢。你缺的不过是一点药，可你就是不肯听劝。你知道这不怪我；我一遍又一遍请你试试杰利医生的清凉散（Dr. Jelly's Cooling Powders）。」

他还记得小时候被强行灌服那种药散的场面，庆幸那些日子总算过去了。他只朝表姐咧嘴一笑，灌下一大杯浓茶，好稳住自己摇摇欲坠的神经。一天，迪克森太太在凯尔梅恩看见他；那天很热，他又走得相当急。身上的疤痕灼烧刺痛，他向牧师太太举帽致意时，身子晃了一晃。她未经进一步查证便断定，他准是上酒馆喝多了。

「可怜的泰勒太太得以蒙召，倒像是蒙了恩典，」她对丈夫说。「她确确实实省去了多少磨难。今天下午那个不争气的年轻人从我身边走过；他分明喝得酩酊大醉。」

「真是令人难过啊，」迪克森先生说。「来一点波特酒吗，亲爱的？」

「谢谢你，梅里维尔（Merivale），我再来一杯雪利酒。伯罗斯医生（Dr. Burrows）总骂我，说我*务必*喝点什么来提住元气，可这雪利酒淡得很。」

===Original===

The Dixons were not teetotalers. They regretted it deeply, and blamed the doctor, who “insisted on some stimulant.” However, there was some consolation in trying to convert the parish to total abstinence, or, as they curiously called it, temperance. Old women were warned of the sin of taking a glass of beer for supper; aged labourers were urged to try Cork-ho, the new temperance drink; an uncouth beverage, styled coffee, was dispensed at the reading-room. Mr. Dixon preached an eloquent “temperance” sermon, soon after the above conversation, taking as his text: “Beware of the leaven of the Pharisees.” In his discourse he showed that fermented liquor and leaven had much in common, that beer was at the present day “put away” during Passover by the strict Jews; and in a moving peroration he urged his dear brethren, “and more especially those amongst us who are poor in this world’s goods,” to beware indeed of that evil leaven which was sapping the manhood of our nation. Mrs. Dixon cried after church:

“Oh, Merivale, what a beautiful sermon! How earnest you were. I hope it will do good.”

Mr. Dixon swallowed his port with great decorum, but his wife fuddled herself every evening with cheap sherry. She was quite unaware of the fact, and sometimes wondered in a dim way why she always had to scold the children after dinner. And so strange things sometimes happened in the nursery, and now and then the children looked queerly at one another after a red-faced woman had gone out, panting.

Lucian knew nothing of his accuser’s trials, but he was not long in hearing of his own intoxication. The next time he went down to Caermaen he was hailed by the doctor.

===Chinese===

迪克森夫妇并不是绝对戒酒主义者。他们为此深感遗憾，并把责任推给医生，因为是医生「坚持要来点刺激性饮料」。不过，设法劝化整个教区绝对戒酒——或照他们古怪的说法，「节制」——总算是一种安慰。老太婆们被警告，晚饭喝一杯啤酒是有罪的；老迈的劳工们被敦促去试试科克霍（Cork-ho），一种新出的节制饮料；阅览室里则施舍着一种号称咖啡的粗劣饮料。上述谈话过后不久，迪克森先生便宣讲了一篇雄辩的「节制」布道，经文取的是：「你们要防备法利赛人的酵。」在讲道中他论证了发酵酒与酵有诸多共通之处，论证了时至今日，严谨的犹太人在逾越节期间仍要「屏除」啤酒；而在动人的结语里，他恳请亲爱的弟兄们——「尤其是我们中间在这世上的财物上贫穷的人」——务要警惕那正在蛀蚀我们民族男子气概的邪恶之酵。做完礼拜，迪克森太太哭了：

「哦，梅里维尔，多么美好的布道！你是多么恳切啊。但愿它能结出善果。」

迪克森先生喝他的波特酒喝得极有威仪，他的太太却每天晚上用廉价雪利把自己灌得醺醺然。她对此浑然不觉，有时还隐隐纳闷，为什么吃完晚饭她总得把孩子骂一顿。于是育儿室里有时会发生些怪事；间或，在一个红脸膛的女人喘着粗气走出去之后，孩子们会面面相觑，神色古怪。

卢西安对指控者的种种苦处一无所知，但没过多久，便听说自己喝醉了。下一次进城到凯尔梅恩，医生远远招呼了他。

===Original===

“Been drinking again today?”

“No,” said Lucian in a puzzled voice. “What do you mean?”

“Oh, well, if you haven’t, that’s all right, as you’ll be able to take a drop with me. Come along in?”

Over the whisky and pipes Lucian heard of the evil rumors affecting his character.

“Mrs. Dixon assured me you were staggering from one side of the street to the other. You quite frightened her, she said. Then she asked me if I recommended her to take one or two ounces of spirit at bedtime for the palpitation; and of course I told her two would be better. I have my living to make here, you know. And upon my word, I think she wants it; she’s always gurgling inside like a waterworks. I wonder how old Dixon can stand it.”

“I like ‘ounces of spirit,’ ” said Lucian. “That’s taking it medicinally, I suppose. I’ve often heard of ladies who have to ‘take it medicinally’; and that’s how it’s done?”

“That’s it. ‘Dr. Burrows won’t *listen to me*’: ‘I tell him how I dislike the taste of spirits, but he says they are absolutely *necessary* for my constitution’: ‘my medical man *insists* on something at bedtime’; that’s the style.”

Lucian laughed gently; all these people had become indifferent to him; he could no longer feel savage indignation at their little hypocrisies and malignancies. Their voices uttering calumny, and morality, and futility had become like the thin shrill angry note of a gnat on a summer evening; he had his own thoughts and his own life, and he passed on without heeding.

===Chinese===

「今天又喝酒了？」

「没有啊，」卢西安一脸茫然地说。「您这话什么意思？」

「哦，好吧，既然没喝，那正好，你还能陪我喝上一盅。进来坐坐？」

就着威士忌和烟斗，卢西安听到了那些败坏他名声的恶毒谣传。

「迪克森太太向我担保，说你在街上从这一边晃到那一边。她说你把她吓得不轻。接着她又问我，她心悸，睡前服一盎司还是两盎司烈酒为好；我当然告诉她两盎司更好。你知道，我在这儿还要挣饭吃呢。而且说句良心话，我看她也真离不了这个；她肚子里老是咕嘟咕嘟的，活像一座自来水厂。真奇怪老迪克森怎么受得了。」

「我喜欢『盎司烈酒』这个说法，」卢西安说。「照我猜，这就是『当药喝』了。我常听说有些太太非得『当药喝』不可；原来就是这么个喝法？」

「正是如此。『伯罗斯医生根本*不肯听我的*』；『我告诉他我多么讨厌酒味，可他说对我的体质*绝对必不可少*』；『我的医生*坚持*要我睡前用一点』；就是这一套。」

卢西安轻轻笑了；这些人对他来说已无关紧要；对他们那点小小的虚伪与恶毒，他再也生不出野蛮的义愤。他们的声音——诽谤、道德与无聊的声音——已变得像夏夜蚊蚋那细而尖的怒鸣；他自有自己的思绪、自己的生活，径自走了过去，毫不在意。

===Original===

“You come down to Caermaen pretty often, don’t you?” said the doctor. “I’ve seen you two or three times in the last fortnight.”

“Yes, I enjoy the walk.”

“Well, look me up whenever you like, you know. I am often in just at this time, and a chat with a human being isn’t bad, now and then. It’s a change for me; I’m often afraid I shall lose my patients.”

The doctor had the weakness of these terrible puns, dragged headlong into the conversation. He sometimes exhibited them before Mrs. Gervase, who would smile in a faint and dignified manner, and say:

“Ah, I see. Very amusing indeed. We had an old coachmen once who was very clever, I believe, at that sort of thing, but Mr. Gervase was obliged to send him away, the laughter of the other domestics was so very boisterous.”

Lucian laughed, not boisterously, but good-humouredly, at the doctor’s joke. He liked Burrows, feeling that he was a man and not an automatic gabbling machine.

“You look a little pulled down,” said the doctor, when Lucian rose to go. “No, you don’t want my medicine. Plenty of beef and beer will do you more good than drugs. I daresay it’s the hot weather that has thinned you a bit. Oh, you’ll be all right again in a month.”

===Chinese===

「你常到凯尔梅恩来，是吧？」医生说。「这两个星期我见过你两三回了。」

「是的，我喜欢这段路。」

「那好，你什么时候想来就来找我。这个时辰我多半在家；偶尔同一个活人聊聊也不坏。对我来说算是换换空气；我时常担心，我会把我的病人统统失掉——连耐性也一并失掉。」

这位医生脱不出爱说这类可怕双关语的毛病，总是不由分说地把它们硬拖进谈话。他有时在格瓦斯太太面前展览这些妙语，她便以淡漠而端庄的仪态微微一笑，说道：

「啊，我明白了。确实非常有趣。我们从前有个老马车夫，我相信，那一类的玩意儿他很在行；可格瓦斯先生不得不把他辞掉，因为别的仆人们笑得实在太放肆了。」

听了医生的双关语，卢西安笑了——不是放声大笑，而是善意地笑。他喜欢伯罗斯，觉得他是个人，不是一台自动呱噪的机器。

「你气色有点委顿，」卢西安起身告辞时，医生说。「不，你用不着我的药。多多的牛肉和啤酒，比药对你更有好处。我敢说，是天热把你熬瘦了一点。哦，不出一个月，你就又会好起来的。」

===Original===

As Lucian strolled out of the town on his way home, he passed a small crowd of urchins assembled at the corner of an orchard. They were enjoying themselves immensely. The “healthy” boy, the same whom he had seen some weeks ago operating on a cat, seemed to have recognized his selfishness in keeping his amusements to himself. He had found a poor lost puppy, a little creature with bright pitiful eyes, almost human in their fond, friendly gaze. It was not a well-bred little dog; it was certainly not that famous puppy “by Vick out of Wasp”; it had rough hair and a foolish long tail which it wagged beseechingly, at once deprecating severity and asking kindness. The poor animal had evidently been used to gentle treatment; it would look up in a boy’s face, and give a leap, fawning on him, and then bark in a small doubtful voice, and cower a moment on the ground, astonished perhaps at the strangeness, the bustle and animation. The boys were beside themselves with eagerness; there was quite a babble of voices, arguing, discussing, suggesting. Each one had a plan of his own which he brought before the leader, a stout and sturdy youth.

“Drown him! What be you thinkin’ of, mun?” he was saying. “ ’Tain’t no sport at all. You shut your mouth, *gwaes*. Be you goin’ to ask your mother for the boiling-water? Iss, Bob Williams, I do know all that: but where be you a-going to get the fire from? Be quiet, mun, can’t you? Thomas Trevor, be this dog yourn or mine? Now, look you, if you don’t all of you shut your bloody mouths, I’ll take the dog ’ome and keep him. There now!”

He was a born leader of men. A singular depression and lowness of spirit showed itself on the boys’ faces. They recognized that the threat might very possibly be executed, and their countenances were at once composed to humble attention. The puppy was still cowering on the ground in the midst of them: one or two tried to relieve the tension of their feelings by kicking him in the belly with their hobnail boots. It cried out with the pain and writhed a little, but the poor little beast did not attempt to bite or even snarl. It looked up with those beseeching friendly eyes at its persecutors, and fawned on them again, and tried to wag its tail and be merry, pretending to play with a straw on the road, hoping perhaps to win a little favor in that way.

===Chinese===

卢西安出城走上归途，路过果园一角聚着的一小群顽童。他们正玩得兴高采烈。那个「健康的」男孩——就是几星期前他见过的、给一只猫动手术的那个——似乎已经认识到，把乐趣独享未免自私。他捡到了一只可怜的走失小狗，一个小东西，一双明亮而哀婉的眼睛，那亲热友爱的凝望里几乎带着人味。这不是一只血统高贵的小狗；它当然不是那只有名的、「维克配黄蜂生的」小狗；它毛色粗劣，拖着一条傻气的长尾巴，哀求地摇着，既告饶求免严厉，又讨要一点善意。这可怜的牲灵显然过惯了温存的日子；它会仰头望一个男孩的脸，蹦一下，向他献媚，然后用细小而不安的声音叫一声，往地上缩伏片刻——也许是被这份陌生、这阵忙乱与骚动惊呆了。男孩们急不可耐，全然忘形；人声嘈嘈，有争执的，有议论的，有出主意的。每人都有一套自己的方案，捧到首领面前去——那是个矮壮敦实的少年。

「淹死他！你想什么呢，咹？」他正说着。「那根本算不上玩意儿。闭上你的嘴，*gwaes*〔土话，「家伙」〕。你打算找你娘要开水去？是哩，鲍勃·威廉斯（Bob Williams），那我都晓得：可你上哪儿弄火去？都消停点儿，行不？托马斯·特雷弗（Thomas Trevor），这狗是你的还是我的？都听好了，你们这帮家伙要还都不闭上你们那臭嘴，我就把狗拎回家自己养了。怎么样！」

他是天生的众人之首。男孩们脸上顿时现出一阵异样的沮丧与低沉。他们听得出，这威胁很可能说到做到，脸上的神色立刻收拢成谦卑的恭听。小狗还缩伏在他们中间的地上：一两个人抬起钉着平头钉的靴子往它肚子上踹，好缓解心头情绪的紧张。它痛得叫出声来，身子扭了扭，可这可怜的小畜生并不试图咬人，连龇牙都不曾。它用那双哀求而友好的眼睛仰望着折磨它的人们，又向他们献起媚来，还试着摇摇尾巴、打起精神，装作去玩路上一根稻草，或许指望这样讨得一点垂怜。

===Original===

The leader saw the moment for his masterstroke. He slowly drew a piece of rope from his pocket.

“What do you say to that, mun? Now, Thomas Trevor! We’ll hang him over that there bough. Will that suit you, Bobby Williams?”

There was a great shriek of approval and delight. All was again bustle and animation. “I’ll tie it round his neck?” “Get out, mun, you don’t know how it be done.” “Iss, I do, Charley.” “Now, let me, *gwaes*, now do let me.” “You be sure he won’t bite?” “He bain’t mad, be he?” “Suppose we were to tie up his mouth first?”

The puppy still fawned and curried favor, and wagged that sorry tail, and lay down crouching on one side on the ground, sad and sorry in his heart, but still with a little gleam of hope; for now and again he tried to play, and put up his face, praying with those fond, friendly eyes. And then at last his gambols and poor efforts for mercy ceased, and he lifted up his wretched voice in one long dismal whine of despair. But he licked the hand of the boy that tied the noose.

He was slowly and gently swung into the air as Lucian went by unheeded; he struggled, and his legs twisted and writhed. The “healthy” boy pulled the rope, and his friends danced and shouted with glee. As Lucian turned the corner, the poor dangling body was swinging to and fro, the puppy was dying, but he still kicked a little.

===Chinese===

首领看准了施展绝招的时机。他从口袋里慢慢抽出一条绳子。

「你们看这个怎么样，咹？来吧，托马斯·特雷弗！咱们把他吊到那根树杈上。这合你的意吗，博比·威廉斯？」

响起一片赞成与狂喜的尖叫。一切又忙碌喧腾起来。「我来把绳子套上他脖子？」「去你的，咹，你根本不会弄。」「是哩，我会，查利（Charley）。」「让我来，*gwaes*，好歹让我来一回。」「你担保他不咬人？」「他没疯，是吧？」「要不咱们先把他的嘴捆上？」

小狗还在献媚讨好，摇着那条可怜的尾巴，侧着身子缩卧在地上，心里悲悲切切，却还存着一线微光似的希望；因为它时不时还想玩耍一阵，仰起脸来，用那双亲热而友好的眼睛祈告着。可到后来，它的雀跃与可怜的求饶终于停了，它扬起凄惨的声音，发出一声悠长的、绝望的哀鸣。可是，它还是舔了舔那个打绳套的男孩的手。

卢西安走过时并未留意，就在那时，它被慢慢悠悠、轻轻巧巧地荡上了半空；它挣扎着，四条腿扭动抽搐。那个「健康的」男孩拽紧绳子，他的伙伴们手舞足蹈，欢叫连连。卢西安转过街角时，那可怜的、悬垂的小小躯体还在晃来晃去；小狗快要死了，可它还在轻轻地蹬踹。

===Original===

Lucian went on his way hastily, and shuddering with disgust. The young of the human creature were really too horrible; they defiled the earth, and made existence unpleasant, as the pulpy growth of a noxious and obscene fungus spoils an agreeable walk. The sight of those malignant little animals with mouths that uttered cruelty and filth, with hands dexterous in torture, and feet swift to run all evil errands, had given him a shock and broken up the world of strange thoughts in which he had been dwelling. Yet it was no good being angry with them: it was their nature to be very loathsome. Only he wished they would go about their hideous amusements in their own back gardens where nobody could see them at work; it was too bad that he should be interrupted and offended in a quiet country road. He tried to put the incident out of his mind, as if the whole thing had been a disagreeable story, and the visions amongst which he wished to move were beginning to return, when he was again rudely disturbed. A little girl, a pretty child of eight or nine, was coming along the lane to meet him. She was crying bitterly and looking to left and right, and calling out some word all the time.

“Jack, Jack, Jack! Little Jackie! Jack!”

Then she burst into tears afresh, and peered into the hedge, and tried to peep through a gate into a field.

“Jackie, Jackie, Jackie!”

She came up to Lucian, sobbing as if her heart would break, and dropped him an old-fashioned curtsy.

“Oh, please sir, have you seen my little Jackie?”

“What do you mean?” said Lucian. “What is it you’ve lost?”

“A little dog, please sir. A little terrier dog with white hair. Father gave me him a month ago, and said I might keep him. Someone did leave the garden gate open this afternoon, and he must ’a got away, sir, and I was so fond of him sir, he was so playful and loving, and I be afraid he be lost.”

===Chinese===

卢西安匆匆赶路，厌恶得直打寒噤。人类这种生灵的幼崽，实在太过骇人；他们玷污大地，把生存弄得不堪——正如一朵毒性而下流的菌类那软糊糊的滋长，毁掉一段惬意的散步。看见那群恶毒的小动物——嘴里吐着残忍与污秽，双手精于施刑，两脚奔走于一切邪恶的差使——他受了震动，栖居其中的那个奇思异想的世界也被击碎了。然而同他们动怒毫无用处：生得可憎本是他们的天性。他只盼他们到自家的后花园里去进行那些可怖的玩乐，别让任何人瞧见他们作业；在一条安静的乡间小路上被打搅、被冒犯，未免欺人太甚。他竭力把这段事从脑子里赶出去，只当整件事是个不愉快的故事；他愿意徜徉其中的那些幻象正开始归来，却猛然又被粗暴地惊扰了。一个小姑娘，八九岁模样的俊俏孩子，正沿小巷迎面走来。她哭得好不伤心，左顾右盼，口里一声声唤着什么。

「杰克，杰克，杰克！小杰基！杰克！」

接着她重新放声大哭起来，往树篱里细看，又想从一扇园门的缝隙里窥看里面的田地。

「杰基，杰基，杰基！」

她走到卢西安跟前，抽抽噎噎，哭得心都要碎了，向他行了一个老派的屈膝礼。

「哦，求求您，先生，您见过我的小杰基吗？」

「你是什么意思？」卢西安说。「你丢了什么？」

「一只小狗，求求您，先生。一只白色毛的小㹴犬。爸爸一个月前把他给我的，说我可以养他。今天下午不知是谁没关花园的门，他准是跑出去了，先生，俺可喜欢他了，先生，他又爱玩又亲人，俺怕他是走丢了。」

===Original===

She began to call again, without waiting for an answer.

“Jack, Jack, Jack!”

“I’m afraid some boys have got your little dog,” said Lucian. “They’ve killed him. You’d better go back home.”

He went on, walking as fast as he could in his endeavor to get beyond the noise of the child’s crying. It distressed him, and he wished to think of other things. He stamped his foot angrily on the ground as he recalled the annoyances of the afternoon, and longed for some hermitage on the mountains, far above the stench and the sound of humanity.

===Chinese===

她不等回答，又唤了起来。

「杰克，杰克，杰克！」

「恐怕是几个男孩捉住了你的小狗，」卢西安说。「他们把他弄死了。你还是回家去吧。」

他继续前行，走得越快越好，只求把孩子的哭声甩在身后。那哭声折磨着他，他只想些别的事情。回想起下午种种恼人的事，他愤愤地跺了跺脚，渴望在高山之巅觅得一间隐庐，远远高踞于人类的秽臭与喧嚣之上。

===Original===

A little farther, and he came to Croeswen, where the road branched off to right and left. There was a triangular plot of grass between the two roads; there the cross had once stood, “the goodly and famous roode” of the old local chronicle. The words echoed in Lucian’s ears as he went by on the right hand. “There were five steps that did go up to the first pace, and seven steps to the second pace, all of clene hewn ashler. And all above it was most curiously and gloriously wrought with thorowgh carved work; in the highest place was the Holy Roode with Christ upon the Cross having Marie on the one syde and John on the other. And below were six splendent and glisteringe archaungels that bore up the roode, and beneath them in their stories were the most fair and noble images of the xii Apostles and of divers other Saints and Martirs. And in the lowest storie there was a marvelous imagerie of divers Beasts, such as oxen and horses and swine, and little dogs and peacocks, all done in the finest and most curious wise, so that they all seemed as they were caught in a Wood of Thorns, the which is their torment of this life. And here once in the year was a marvelous solemn service, when the parson of Caermaen came out with the singers and all the people, singing the psalm *Benedicite omnia opera* as they passed along the road in their procession. And when they stood at the roode the priest did there his service, making certain prayers for the beasts, and then he went up to the first pace and preached a sermon to the people, showing them that as our Lord Jhu dyed upon the Tree of his deare mercy for us, so we too owe mercy to the beasts his Creatures, for that they are all his poor lieges and silly servants. And that like as the Holy Aungells do their suit to him on high, and the Blessed xii Apostles and the Martirs, and all the Blissful Saints served him aforetime on earth and now praise him in heaven, so also do the beasts serve him, though they be in torment of life and below men. For their spirit goeth downward, as Holy Writ teacheth us.”

===Chinese===

再往前走一程，他便到了克罗斯温（Croeswen），大路在此左右分岔。两条路之间夹着一块三角形的草地；十字架曾经立在那里——便是那部古老地方编年史所称的「美好而驰名的十字架」。卢西安打右手经过时，那些词句在他耳中回响。「昔有五级台阶，通至第一层台；又有七级，通至第二层台；皆以琢治洁净之方石为之。其上通体皆施镂刻之工，奇巧而辉煌；至高处为神圣十字架，基督钉于架上，马利亚立于一旁，约翰立于另一旁。其下有六位光辉炳耀之天使长，擎举十字之架；再下各层，雕十二使徒及诸圣徒、诸殉道者之像，皆至美至贵。至低一层，则有百兽之奇图：牛马与豕，小犬与孔雀，皆以至精至巧之法为之，观之俨然尽陷于荆棘之林——是即彼辈此生之苦刑也。岁一度，于此行至隆至肃之仪：凯尔梅恩之牧师率歌者与众民出，行于道中，一路唱《万物颂》（*Benedicite omnia opera*）之诗。既至十字架前，司铎乃行其仪，为百兽祈祷；遂登第一层台，向众民宣讲，晓谕之曰：我主耶稣以其慈怀死于木上，为我等故；则我等于彼之受造物百兽，亦当施以怜悯，盖彼皆其贫弱之藩属、无知无识之仆役也。又曰：诸圣天使于高天事奉之，蒙福之十二使徒与诸殉道者、诸福圣，昔在世上事奉之，今在天上颂赞之；百兽亦如是事奉之——虽身处生之刑苦，位居人下。盖兽之灵下坠入地，圣书明训如此。」

===Original===

It was a quaint old record, a curious relic of what the modern inhabitants of Caermaen called the Dark Ages. A few of the stones that had formed the base of the cross still remained in position, grey with age, blotched with black lichen and green moss. The remainder of the famous rood had been used to mend the roads, to build pigsties and domestic offices; it had turned Protestant, in fact. Indeed, if it had remained, the parson of Caermaen would have had no time for the service; the coffee-stall, the Portuguese Missions, the Society for the Conversion of the Jews, and important social duties took up all his leisure. Besides, he thought the whole ceremony unscriptural.

Lucian passed on his way, wondering at the strange contrasts of the Middle Ages. How was it that people who could devise so beautiful a service believed in witchcraft, demoniacal possession and obsession, in the incubus and succubus, and in the Sabbath and in many other horrible absurdities? It seemed astonishing that anybody could even pretend to credit such monstrous tales, but there could be no doubt that the dread of old women who rode on broomsticks and liked black cats was once a very genuine terror.

A cold wind blew up from the river at sunset, and the scars on his body began to burn and tingle. The pain recalled his ritual to him, and he began to recite it as he walked along. He had cut a branch of thorn from the hedge and placed it next to his skin, pressing the spikes into the flesh with his hand till the warm blood ran down. He felt it was an exquisite and sweet observance for her sake; and then he thought of the secret golden palace he was building for her, the rare and wonderful city rising in his imagination. As the solemn night began to close about the earth, and the last glimmer of the sun faded from the hills, he gave himself anew to the woman, his body and his mind, all that he was, and all that he had.

===Chinese===

这是一份古怪的老记录，一件奇特的遗物，出自凯尔梅恩当今居民口中的「黑暗时代」。当年承托十字架底座的石头，还有几块留在原处，苍老而灰暗，斑驳着黑色的地衣与绿色的苔藓。那座驰名十字架的其余部分，则被用来修补道路、搭建猪圈与杂用屋；事实上，它已皈依了新教。说真的，倘若它还在，凯尔梅恩的牧师也无暇主持那套仪式；咖啡摊、葡萄牙传道会（the Portuguese Missions）、犹太人皈依协会（the Society for the Conversion of the Jews），以及种种重大的社交义务，占尽了他的闲暇。再说，他觉得整套仪式也不合圣经。

卢西安继续赶路，暗自惊异于中世纪种种奇异的反差。竟能构想出如此美好之仪式的人们，怎么会又相信巫术，相信魔鬼附身与魇缠，相信男梦魔（incubus）与女梦魔（succubus），相信巫魔夜会（the Sabbath），以及许多别的可怕的荒唐事？任何人哪怕只是假装信一信这等怪诞不经的故事，看来都令人骇怪；然而毫无疑问，对骑扫帚、爱黑猫的老太婆的恐惧，曾是一种极其真切的恐怖。

日暮时分，一阵冷风从河上吹来，他身上的疤痕开始灼烧刺痛。疼痛把他的仪轨唤回心头，他一边走，一边诵念起来。他从树篱上砍了一枝荆棘，贴肉放着，用手把棘刺压进皮肉，直到温热的血淌下来。他觉得，这是为她奉行的一桩精微而甘美的功课；随后他又想起他正为她营造的那座隐秘的黄金宫殿，那座在他想象中冉冉升起的珍异而奇妙的城市。庄严的夜开始四合于大地，落日最后一抹微光从山峦间消隐，他把自己重新奉献给那个女人——他的身体与他的心智，他的一切所是，与他的一切所有。

## IV / 第四章

===Original===

In the course of the week Lucian again visited Caermaen. He wished to view the amphitheatre more precisely, to note the exact position of the ancient walls, to gaze up the valley from certain points within the town, to imprint minutely and clearly on his mind the surge of the hills about the city, and the dark tapestry of the hanging woods. And he lingered in the museum where the relics of the Roman occupation had been stored; he was interested in the fragments of tessellated floors, in the glowing gold of drinking cups, the curious beads of fused and coloured glass, the carved amber-work, the scent-flagons that still retained the memory of unctuous odours, the necklaces, brooches, hairpins of gold and silver, and other intimate objects which had once belonged to Roman ladies. One of the glass flagons, buried in damp earth for many hundred years, had gathered in its dark grave all the splendors of the light, and now shone like an opal with a moonlight glamour and gleams of gold and pale sunset green, and imperial purple. Then there were the wine jars of red earthenware, the memorial stones from graves, and the heads of broken gods, with fragments of occult things used in the secret rites of Mithras. Lucian read on the labels where all these objects were found: in the churchyard, beneath the turf of the meadow, and in the old cemetery near the forest; and whenever it was possible he would make his way to the spot of discovery, and imagine the long darkness that had hidden gold and stone and amber. All these investigations were necessary for the scheme he had in view, so he became for some time quite a familiar figure in the dusty deserted streets and in the meadows by the river. His continual visits to Caermaen were a tortuous puzzle to the inhabitants, who flew to their windows at the sound of a step on the uneven pavements. They were at a loss in their conjectures; his motive for coming down three times a week must of course be bad, but it seemed undiscoverable. And Lucian on his side was at first a good deal put out by occasional encounters with members of the Gervase or Dixon or Colley tribes; he had often to stop and exchange a few conventional expressions, and such meetings, casual as they were, annoyed and distracted him. He was no longer infuriated or wounded by sneers of contempt or by the cackling laughter of the young people when they passed him on the road (his hat was a shocking one and his untidiness terrible), but such incidents were unpleasant just as the smell of a drain was unpleasant, and threw the strange mechanism of his thoughts out of fear for the time. Then he had been disgusted by the affair of the boys and the little dog; the loathsomeness of it had quite broken up his fancies. He had read books of modern occultism, and remembered some of the experiments described. The adept, it was alleged, could transfer the sense of consciousness from his brain to the foot or hand, he could annihilate the world around him and pass into another sphere. Lucian wondered whether he could not perform some such operation for his own benefit. Human beings were constantly annoying him and getting in his way, was it not possible to annihilate the race, or at all events to reduce them to wholly insignificant forms? A certain process suggested itself to his mind, a work partly mental and partly physical, and after two or three experiments he found to his astonishment and delight that it was successful. Here, he thought, he had discovered one of the secrets of true magic; this was the key to the symbolic transmutations of the Eastern tales. The adept could, in truth, change those who were obnoxious to him into harmless and unimportant shapes, not as in the letter of the old stories, by transforming the enemy, but by transforming himself. The magician puts men below him by going up higher, as one looks down on a mountain city from a loftier crag. The stones on the road and such petty obstacles do not trouble the wise man on the great journey, and so Lucian, when obliged to stop and converse with his fellow-creatures, to listen to their poor pretences and inanities, was no more inconvenienced than when he had to climb an awkward stile in the course of a walk. As for the more unpleasant manifestations of humanity; after all they no longer concerned him. Men intent on the great purpose did not suffer the current of their thoughts to be broken by the buzzing of a fly caught in a spider’s web, so why should he be perturbed by the misery of a puppy in the hands of village boys? The fly, no doubt, endured its tortures; lying helpless and bound in those slimy bands, it cried out in its thin voice when the claws of the horrible monster fastened on it; but its dying agonies had never vexed the reverie of a lover. Lucian saw no reason why the boys should offend him more than the spider, or why he should pity the dog more than he pitied the fly. The talk of the men and women might be wearisome and inept and often malignant; but he could not imagine an alchemist at the moment of success, a general in the hour of victory, or a financier with a gigantic scheme of swindling well on the market being annoyed by the buzz of insects. The spider is, no doubt, a very terrible brute with a hideous mouth and hairy tiger-like claws when seen through the microscope; but Lucian had taken away the microscope from his eyes. He could now walk the streets of Caermaen confident and secure, without any dread of interruption, for at a moment’s notice the transformation could be effected. Once Dr. Burrows caught him and made him promise to attend a bazaar that was to be held in aid of the Hungarian Protestants; Lucian assented the more willingly as he wished to pay a visit to certain curious mounds on a hill a little way out of the town, and he calculated on slinking off from the bazaar early in the afternoon. Lord Beamys was visiting Sir Vivian Ponsonby, a local magnate, and had kindly promised to drive over and declare the bazaar open. It was a solemn moment when the carriage drew up and the great man alighted. He was rather an evil-looking old nobleman, but the clergy and gentry, their wives and sons and daughters welcomed him with great and unctuous joy. Conversations were broken off in mid-sentence, slow people gaped, not realizing why their friends had so suddenly left them, the Meyricks came up hot and perspiring in fear lest they should be too late, Miss Colley, a yellow virgin of austere regard, smiled largely, Mrs. Dixon beckoned wildly with her parasol to the “girls” who were idly strolling in a distant part of the field, and the archdeacon ran at full speed. The air grew dark with bows, and resonant with the genial laugh of the archdeacon, the cackle of the younger ladies, and the shrill parrot-like voices of the matrons; those smiled who had never smiled before, and on some maiden faces there hovered that look of adoring ecstasy with which the old maidens graced their angels. Then, when all the due rites had been performed, the company turned and began to walk towards the booths of their small Vanity Fair. Lord Beamys led the way with Mrs. Gervase, Mrs. Dixon followed with Sir Vivian Ponsonby, and the multitudes that followed cried, saying, “What a dear old man!”﻿—“Isn’t it *kind* of him to come all this way?”﻿—“What a sweet expression, isn’t it?”﻿—“I think he’s an old love”﻿—“One of the good old sort”﻿—“Real English nobleman”﻿—“Oh most correct, I assure you; if a girl gets into trouble, notice to quit at once”﻿—“Always stands by the Church”﻿—“Twenty livings in his gift”﻿—“Voted for the Public Worship Regulation Act”﻿—“Ten thousand acres strictly preserved.” The old lord was leering pleasantly and muttering to himself: “Some fine gals here. Like the looks of that filly with the pink hat. Ought to see more of her. She’d give Lotty points.”

===Chinese===

一周之内，卢西安（Lucian）又一次造访了凯尔梅恩（Caermaen）。他想更精确地察看那座圆形剧场，记下古墙的确切位置，从镇内若干地点仰望山谷，把群山环城奔涌的起伏、悬林如深色织锦的景象，纤毫毕现地烙进脑海。他还在博物馆里流连，那里收藏着罗马占领时期的遗物；他感兴趣的是镶嵌地面的残片，是饮杯灼灼的黄金、熔铸彩色玻璃的奇趣珠子、雕琢的琥珀器件、犹自残留着脂膏香气记忆的香水瓶，还有金银的项链、胸针、发簪，以及其他曾属于罗马贵妇的贴身之物。其中一只玻璃瓶，在潮湿的泥土里埋了几百年，已在自己幽暗的墓穴中聚敛了光的全部华彩，如今像一颗蛋白石般莹莹生辉，带着月光的迷魅，闪着金色、淡淡落日绿与帝王紫的光泽。还有红陶的酒坛、墓中的纪念石碑、断残的神像头颅，以及密特拉（Mithras）秘仪所用秘物的残件。卢西安在标签上读到这些器物各自的出土地点：教堂墓园里、草甸的草皮下、森林近旁的古墓地里；只要有可能，他总要亲赴发现之地，想象那把黄金、石头与琥珀掩藏起来的漫漫黑暗。这一切查考，于他心中的构想都是必要的，于是有一段日子，他成了尘封空寂的街巷与河畔草甸间一个相当眼熟的身影。他频频到访凯尔梅恩，叫镇民们好生费解；一听得不平的人行道上传来脚步声，他们便扑到窗前张望。他们的揣度全然不得要领；他一星期下来三趟，动机必然不端，可这动机偏偏无从查究。而卢西安这一边，起初也颇受偶与格瓦斯（Gervase）、迪克森（Dixon）、科利（Colley）几家部落中人相遇的搅扰；他常常不得不停下脚来寒暄几句客套，这类相遇虽说偶然，却叫他心烦意乱。年轻人路上与他错身而过时轻蔑的讥笑与嘎嘎的笑声（他的帽子糟糕透顶，邋遢也骇人听闻），已不再叫他怒火中烧或刺痛于心，但这类事情终究令人生厌，正如阴沟的气味令人生厌，一时之间，把他思绪那部奇特的机器搅出了常轨。再说，男孩们与那只小狗的事件也叫他作呕；那件事的可憎，把他的种种遐想击得七零八落。他读过现代通灵术的书，还记得书中描述的几种实验。据说，术士能把意识之感从头脑转移到脚或手，能湮灭周遭的世界，进入另一个界域。卢西安寻思，自己能否也做一番这样的操作，以资己用。人类无时无刻不烦扰他、碍他的事；把这整个族类湮灭掉，或者至少把他们缩减成全然无足轻重的形骸，岂非可能？他心里浮现出一种特定的法程，一件半属心智、半属形骸的功课；两三次实验之后，他又惊又喜地发现，这法子竟是灵验的。他想，在此他发现了真魔法的一桩秘密；这便是东方故事里那些象征变形的钥匙。术士确实能把可厌之人变成无害而卑微的形状——不是如旧故事的字面那样，去变形仇敌，而是变形他自己。魔术师把自己升得更高，从而把众人踩在脚下，正如人从更高的危崖俯瞰一座山城。路上的石块与这等细碎的障碍，烦扰不了踏上伟大旅程的智者；同样，卢西安每每当不得不驻足与同类寒暄、听他们那可怜的虚饰与空谈时，也不再比散步途中翻越一道别扭的栅栏踏阶更觉不便。至于人性中那些更不愉快的显形，毕竟已与他无干。专注于伟大目标的人，不会让思绪的水流被一只落网苍蝇的嗡嗡搅断；那么，一只小狗崽落在村童手中的苦难，又何必搅动他的心？那苍蝇无疑忍受着它的酷刑；无助地被缚在那些黏滑的束缚里，当那可怕怪物的利爪攫住它时，它便以细弱的声音哀鸣；可它垂死的痛楚，从不曾搅扰过一位恋人的遐思。卢西安看不出，男孩们凭什么比蜘蛛更冒犯他，也看不出他凭什么怜悯那只狗，胜过怜悯那只苍蝇。男女们的言谈或许乏味、失当，且常常恶毒；可他无法想象，一位功成在即的炼金术士、一位得胜时刻的将军，或一位巨额骗局已在市场上顺利推展的金融家，会被昆虫的嗡嗡搅扰。蜘蛛透过显微镜看去，无疑是一头非常可怕的畜生，生着丑陋的嘴和毛茸茸虎爪般的螯肢；可卢西安已把显微镜从眼前拿开了。如今他行走在凯尔梅恩的街巷，自信而安稳，全不惧怕滋扰；因为只消片刻之意，变形便可完成。有一回，伯罗斯医生（Dr. Burrows）逮住了他，逼他答应出席一场为援助匈牙利新教徒（the Hungarian Protestants）而办的义卖会；卢西安应承得格外爽快，因为他正想去造访镇外不远处一座山上的几座古怪土墩，盘算着下午一过半就从义卖会上溜之大吉。比米斯勋爵（Lord Beamys）正客居在本地巨绅维维安·庞森比爵士（Sir Vivian Ponsonby）府上，好心地许诺驱车前来，宣布义卖会开幕。马车驶停、大人物跨下车来的那一刻，是何等庄严的时刻。他是位相貌颇为凶恶的老贵族，可教职人员与缙绅们，连同他们的妻室儿女，油光光地欢天喜地迎接他。谈话半句半句地中断，迟钝的人张着嘴发怔，不明白朋友们为何突然撇下他们；梅里克一家（the Meyricks）热汗涔涔地赶来，唯恐迟了一步；科利小姐（Miss Colley），那位神情严峻的黄脸老处女，绽开大大的笑容；迪克森太太（Mrs. Dixon）挥着阳伞，狂乱地向远处场地上闲逛的「姑娘们」打手势；会吏长撒腿飞奔。鞠躬作揖使得空气都暗了下来，会吏长亲热的笑声、年轻女士们的嘎嘎声、太太们鹦鹉般的尖利嗓音，激荡其间；从来不笑的人笑了，一些少女的脸上，则萦绕起老小姐们敬奉天使时那种崇拜的狂喜神情。随后，一切应行的礼数行毕，众人转过身，朝着他们那小小名利场（Vanity Fair）的货棚走去。比米斯勋爵由格瓦斯太太（Mrs. Gervase）陪同领行，迪克森太太与维维安·庞森比爵士随在其后，跟上来的人群嚷嚷着：「多可爱的老头儿啊！」——「大老远赶来，他多*仁慈*啊！」——「多甜美的表情，是不是？」——「我看他是位老宝贝儿」——「老派好人里的一个」——「真正的英国贵族」——「哦，我向你担保，规矩最严不过；哪个姑娘惹了麻烦，立刻下逐客令」——「永远站在教会一边」——「他手里握着二十处圣俸」——「投票赞成《公共礼拜规制法》」——「一万英亩禁猎森严」。老勋爵乐滋滋地斜睨着眼，喃喃自语：「这儿有几个俊妞儿。瞧上戴粉帽子那匹小母马了。该多见见她。她准能胜洛蒂几分。」

===Original===

The pomp swept slowly across the grass: the archdeacon had got hold of Mr. Dixon, and they were discussing the misdeeds of some clergyman in the rural deanery.

“I can scarce credit it,” said Mr. Dixon.

“Oh, I assure you, there can be no doubt. We have witnesses. There can be no question that there was a procession at Llanfihangel on the Sunday before Easter; the choir and minister went round the church, carrying palm branches in their hands.”

===Chinese===

这支盛大的行列缓缓扫过草坪：会吏长已经攥住了迪克森先生（Mr. Dixon），两人正议论着乡区总铎区里某位教士的劣迹。

「我简直难以置信。」迪克森先生说。

「哦，我向你担保，这是毋庸置疑的。我们有证人。复活节前的星期日，兰菲汉格尔（Llanfihangel）确实举行了一场游行，这毫无疑问；唱诗班和牧师手里拿着棕榈枝，绕着教堂走了一圈。」

===Original===

“Very shocking.”

“It has distressed the bishop. Martin is a hardworking man enough, and all that, but those sort of things can’t be tolerated. The bishop told me that he had set his face against processions.”

“Quite right: the bishop is perfectly right. Processions are unscriptural.”

===Chinese===

「骇人听闻。」

「这事让主教痛心。马丁（Martin）倒是个够勤勉的人，如此等等，可这类事情不能容忍。主教告诉我，他对游行是坚决抵制到底的。」

「完全正确：主教完全正确。游行是不合圣经的。」

===Original===

“It’s the thin end of the wedge, you know, Dixon.”

“Exactly. I have always resisted anything of the kind here.”

“Right. *Principiis obsta*, you know. Martin is so *imprudent*. There’s a *way* of doing things.”

===Chinese===

「这可是那楔子的薄尖儿，你知道的，迪克森。」

「正是。在这里，这类事情我从来都是抵制的。」

「对。*Principiis obsta*——防微杜渐嘛，你知道。马丁就是这么*不知检点*。做事总有个*章法*。」

===Original===

The “scriptural” procession led by Lord Beamys broke up when the stalls were reached, and gathered round the nobleman as he declared the bazaar open.

Lucian was sitting on a garden-seat, a little distance off, looking dreamily before him. And all that he saw was a swarm of flies clustering and buzzing about a lump of tainted meat that lay on the grass. The spectacle in no way interrupted the harmony of his thoughts, and soon after the opening of the bazaar he went quietly away, walking across the fields in the direction of the ancient mounds he desired to inspect.

===Chinese===

比米斯勋爵引领的这支「合乎圣经」的游行队伍，行到货摊前便散了开来，聚拢到贵族周围，听他宣布义卖会开幕。

卢西安坐在不远处的一张花园长椅上，恍惚地望着前方。而他眼里所见的，无非是一群苍蝇，围着一团弃在草上的腐肉攒聚嗡鸣。这景象丝毫没有打断他思绪的和谐；义卖会开幕后不久，他便悄悄走开了，穿过田野，朝他想去察看的那些古土墩而去。

===Original===

All these journeys of his to Caermaen and its neighborhood had a peculiar object; he was gradually leveling to the dust the squalid kraals of modern times, and rebuilding the splendid and golden city of Siluria. All this mystic town was for the delight of his sweetheart and himself; for her the wonderful villas, the shady courts, the magic of tessellated pavements, and the hangings of rich stuffs with their intricate and glowing patterns. Lucian wandered all day through the shining streets, taking shelter sometimes in the gardens beneath the dense and gloomy ilex trees, and listening to the plash and trickle of the fountains. Sometimes he would look out of a window and watch the crowd and colour of the marketplace, and now and again a ship came up the river bringing exquisite silks and the merchandise of unknown lands in the Far East. He had made a curious and accurate map of the town he proposed to inhabit, in which every villa was set down and named. He drew his lines to scale with the gravity of a surveyor, and studied the plan till he was able to find his way from house to house on the darkest summer night. On the southern slopes about the town there were vineyards, always under a glowing sun, and sometimes he ventured to the furthest ridge of the forest, where the wild people still lingered, that he might catch the golden gleam of the city far away, as the light quivered and scintillated on the glittering tiles. And there were gardens outside the city gates where strange and brilliant flowers grew, filling the hot air with their odour, and scenting the breeze that blew along the streets. The dull modern life was far away, and people who saw him at this period wondered what was amiss; the abstraction of his glance was obvious, even to eyes not over-sharp. But men and women had lost all their power of annoyance and vexation; they could no longer even interrupt his thought for a moment. He could listen to Mr. Dixon with apparent attention, while he was in reality enraptured by the entreating music of the double flute, played by a girl in the garden of Avallaunius, for that was the name he had taken. Mr. Dixon was innocently discoursing archeology, giving a brief résumé of the view expressed by Mr. Wyndham at the last meeting of the antiquarian society.

===Chinese===

他这一趟趟凯尔梅恩及四乡之行，都有着一个独特的目的：他在渐渐把现代那些污陋的棚栏村落（kraals）夷为尘土，重建西卢里亚（Siluria）那座辉煌的金城。这整座神秘之城，是为着他与恋人的欢愉而设；给她的是那些奇妙的府邸、荫凉的庭院、镶嵌地面的魔法，还有那些织着繁复灼丽花纹的华贵帷幕。卢西安整日徜徉在明亮的街巷间，有时到浓荫幽暗的冬青栎下的花园里避日，听喷泉的泼洒与涓滴。有时他从一扇窗口眺望集市的人潮与色彩，不时有一艘船溯河而上，载来精美的丝绸与远东未知之邦的货品。他为他打算栖居的这座城镇绘了一幅精细而准确的地图，每一座府邸都标注在案、一一命名。他以测量师的严谨按比例绘制线条，把图纸研习到在最黑的夏夜也能挨家挨户寻到路的境地。城四周的南坡上是葡萄园，永远沐着一轮灼灼的太阳；有时他壮着胆子走到森林最远的山脊——野民们仍盘桓之地——好远远望见那座城金色的微光，看光在灿亮的屋瓦上颤动闪耀。城门外还有花园，奇丽的花朵盛开其间，把热烘烘的空气注满芬芳，让吹过街巷的微风也带着香气。沉闷的现代生活远在天边；这个时期见过他的人都纳闷他哪里不对劲；他眼神的恍惚有目共睹，连不太尖利的眼睛也看得出。但男男女女已失去了全部搅扰与烦他的能力；他们连打断他片刻的思绪也再办不到了。他能一脸专注地听迪克森先生讲话，实则早已心醉神迷于一支双管笛恳求般的乐声——吹笛的是阿瓦隆尼乌斯（Avallaunius）花园里的一个少女；阿瓦隆尼乌斯，正是他给自己取的名字。迪克森先生则无知无觉地大谈考古学，把温德姆先生（Mr. Wyndham）在上次古物学会会议上发表的观点简述了一遍。

===Original===

“There can be no doubt that the temple of Diana stood there in pagan times,” he concluded, and Lucian assented to the opinion, and asked a few questions which seemed pertinent enough. But all the time the flute notes were sounding in his ears, and the ilex threw a purple shadow on the white pavement before his villa.
