A boy came forward from the garden; he had been walking amongst the vines and plucking the ripe grapes, and the juice had trickled down over his breast. Standing beside the girl, unashamed in the sunlight, he began to sing one of Sappho’s love songs. His voice was as full and rich as a woman’s, but purged of all emotion; he was an instrument of music in the flesh. Lucian looked at him steadily; the white perfect body shone against the roses and the blue of the sky, clear and gleaming as marble in the glare of the sun. The words he sang burned and flamed with passion, and he was as unconscious of their meaning as the twin pipes of the flute. And the girl was smiling. The vicar shook hands and went on, well pleased with his remarks on the temple of Diana, and also with Lucian’s polite interest.

===Chinese===

「毫无疑问，异教时代狄安娜（Diana）的神庙就立在那儿。」他如此作结。卢西安赞同了这个见解，还提了几个看起来相当切题的问题。可自始至终，笛音都在他耳中萦绕，冬青栎在他府邸前白色的镶嵌地面上投着紫色的影。一个男孩从花园里走上前来；他方才在葡萄藤间行走，摘食成熟的葡萄，果汁顺着他胸口淌下。他站在少女身旁，在阳光里毫无羞赧，唱起萨福（Sappho）的一支情歌。他的嗓音如女人般圆润丰润，却涤尽了一切情感；他是一件血肉做的乐器。卢西安定定地望着他；那白皙完美的身体，映衬着玫瑰与蓝天，在灼灼烈日下莹洁生光，如大理石一般。他唱的词句燃烧着、腾着情焰，而他对其意义浑然不觉，一如那双管笛的笛管本身。少女则微笑着。堂区牧师握了握手，继续前行，对自己关于狄安娜神庙的议论颇为满意，对卢西安礼貌的兴味也颇为满意。

===Original===

“He is by no means wanting in intelligence,” he said to his family. “A little curious in manner, perhaps, but not stupid.”

“Oh, papa,” said Henrietta, “don’t you think he is rather silly? He can’t talk about anything﻿—anything interesting, I mean. And he pretends to know a lot about books, but I heard him say the other day he had never read *The Prince of the House of David* or *Ben-Hur*. Fancy!”

===Chinese===

「他绝不算缺乏才智，」他对家人说。「举止也许有点古怪，可并不愚蠢。」

「哦，爸爸，」亨利埃塔（Henrietta）说，「你不觉得他相当傻吗？他什么也谈不来——我是说，什么有趣的事都谈不来。他还装作满腹诗书，可前几天我听见他说，他从来没读过*《大卫家的王子》*（The Prince of the House of David）和*《宾虚》*（Ben-Hur）。你想想！」

===Original===

The vicar had not interrupted Lucian. The sun still beat upon the roses, and a little breeze bore the scent of them to his nostrils together with the smell of grapes and vine-leaves. He had become curious in sensation, and as he leant back upon the cushions covered with glistening yellow silk, he was trying to analyze a strange ingredient in the perfume of the air. He had penetrated far beyond the crude distinctions of modern times, beyond the rough: “there’s a smell of roses,” “there must be sweetbriar somewhere.” Modern perceptions of odour were, he knew, far below those of the savage in delicacy. The degraded black fellow of Australia could distinguish odours in a way that made the consumer of “damper” stare in amazement, but the savage’s sensations were all strictly utilitarian. To Lucian as he sat in the cool porch, his feet on the marble, the air came laden with scents as subtly and wonderfully interwoven and contrasted as the harmonica of a great master. The stained marble of the pavement gave a cool reminiscence of the Italian mountain, the bloodred roses palpitating in the sunlight sent out an odour mystical as passion itself, and there was the hint of inebriation in the perfume of the trellised vines. Besides these, the girl’s desire and the unripe innocence of the boy were as distinct as benzoin and myrrh, both delicious and exquisite, and exhaled as freely as the scent of the roses. But there was another element that puzzled him, an aromatic suggestion of the forest. He understood it at last; it was the vapor of the great red pines that grew beyond the garden; their spicy needles were burning in the sun, and the smell was as fragrant as the fume of incense blown from far. The soft entreaty of the flute and the swelling rapture of the boy’s voice beat on the air together, and Lucian wondered whether there were in the nature of things any true distinction between the impressions of sound and scent and colour. The violent blue of the sky, the song, and the odours seemed rather varied symbols of one mystery than distinct entities. He could almost imagine that the boy’s innocence was indeed a perfume, and that the palpitating roses had become a sonorous chant.

===Chinese===

牧师并没有打断卢西安。太阳依旧炙烤着玫瑰，一缕微风把玫瑰的香气送入他鼻中，还夹着葡萄与葡萄叶的气息。他已变得贪求感官的新奇；他斜倚在闪亮黄丝绸的软垫上，正试图解析空气芬芳里一种奇异的成分。他早已穿透了现代那些粗陋的区分，越过了那粗糙的一套：「这是玫瑰的香味」「附近准是有野蔷薇」。他知道，现代人嗅觉的精微，远在野蛮人之下。澳洲那种堕落的黑人汉子分辨起气味来，能叫吃「丹帕饼」（damper）的人瞠目结舌；可野蛮人的种种感觉，全然囿于实用。而卢西安坐在荫凉的门廊里，双足踏着大理石，空气向他送来的诸般香气，如一位大师谱写的和声一般，微妙奇幻地交织着、对映着。地面斑彩的大理石，凉沁沁地令人忆起意大利的群山；阳光下悸动的血红玫瑰，吐出一缕如激情本身一般神秘的芬芳；棚架上的葡萄藤，其香气里含着一丝醉意。除此之外，少女的欲望与男孩未熟的纯真，如安息香与没药一般判然可辨，一样甘美精致，一样如玫瑰的香气般自由吐纳。可还有一个成分令他困惑，一种森林的芳香暗示。他终于明白了；那是园外大红松树蒸腾的气息；它们辛辣的松针正在阳光下燃烧，那气味如远方吹来的馨香炉烟一般芬芳。笛子柔婉的恳求与男孩歌声高涨的狂喜一同在空气中搏动，卢西安寻思着，在万物的本性里，声、香、色的印象之间，究竟有没有真正的分别。天空烈烈的蓝、歌声与诸般香气，与其说是各自独立的实体，不如说是一个奥秘的不同象征。他几乎可以想象，男孩的纯真当真是一种芬芳，而悸动的玫瑰已化作一段铿然的歌吟。

===Original===

In the curious silence which followed the last notes, when the boy and girl had passed under the purple ilex shadow, he fell into a reverie. The fancy that sensations are symbols and not realities hovered in his mind, and led him to speculate as to whether they could not actually be transmuted one into another. It was possible, he thought, that a whole continent of knowledge had been undiscovered; the energies of men having been expended in unimportant and foolish directions. Modern ingenuity had been employed on such trifles as locomotive engines, electric cables, and cantilever bridges; on elaborate devices for bringing uninteresting people nearer together; the ancients had been almost as foolish, because they had mistaken the symbol for the thing signified. It was not the material banquet which really mattered, but the thought of it; it was almost as futile to eat and take emetics and eat again as to invent telephones and high-pressure boilers. As for some other ancient methods of enjoying life, one might as well set oneself to improve calico printing at once.

“Only in the garden of Avallaunius,” said Lucian to himself, “is the true and exquisite science to be found.”

He could imagine a man who was able to live in one sense while he pleased; to whom, for example, every impression of touch, taste, hearing, or seeing should be translated into odour; who at the desired kiss should be ravished with the scent of dark violets, to whom music should be the perfume of a rose-garden at dawn.

===Chinese===

末音落下之后那奇异的寂静里，男孩与少女已走进紫色的冬青栎影下，他坠入了遐思。感觉是象征而非实在——这个念头萦绕在他心头，引他揣想：它们究竟能不能真的彼此转化。他想，或许有一整块知识的大陆始终未被发现；而世人的精力都耗费在了无关紧要的愚蠢方向上。现代的机巧都用在火车头、电缆、悬臂桥这等琐物上；用在让乏味之人彼此靠近的精巧装置上；古人也几乎同样愚蠢，因为他们把象征错当成了所指之物。真正要紧的并不是那物质的盛宴，而是对盛宴的想望；吃了又服吐药再吃，与发明电话和高压锅炉，几乎同样徒劳。至于古人享受生活的另外一些法子，人还不如立时立志去改良印花布呢。

「唯有在阿瓦隆尼乌斯的花园里，」卢西安对自己说，「才能寻见那真正而精妙的学问。」

他能想象这样一个人：能随心所欲地活在单一的感官里；譬如说，触、味、听、视的每一种印象，都给他译成了气味；盼望的吻落下时，他因深色紫罗兰的芬芳而魂销；而音乐于他，是黎明时分玫瑰园的馥郁。

===Original===

When, now and again, he voluntarily resumed the experience of common life, it was that he might return with greater delight to the garden in the city of refuge. In the actual world the talk was of Nonconformists, the lodger franchise, and the Stock Exchange; people were constantly reading newspapers, drinking Australian Burgundy, and doing other things equally absurd. They either looked shocked when the fine art of pleasure was mentioned, or confused it with going to musical comedies, drinking bad whisky, and keeping late hours in disreputable and vulgar company. He found to his amusement that the profligate were by many degrees duller than the pious, but that the most tedious of all were the persons who preached promiscuity, and called their system of “pigging” the “New Morality.”

He went back to the city lovingly, because it was built and adorned for his love. As the metaphysicians insist on the consciousness of the ego as the implied basis of all thought, so he knew that it was she in whom he had found himself, and through whom and for whom all the true life existed. He felt that Annie had taught him the rare magic which had created the garden of Avallaunius. It was for her that he sought strange secrets and tried to penetrate the mysteries of sensation, for he could only give her wonderful thoughts and a wonderful life, and a poor body stained with the scars of his worship.

===Chinese===

他不时自愿重回寻常生活的经验里去，为的正是带着更大的欢愉，回到那避难之城里的花园。在现实世界里，人们谈的是非国教徒、房客选举权和证券交易所；人们无休无止地读报纸、喝澳洲勃艮第，做着其他同样荒唐的事。一提起享乐的雅艺，他们或者面露骇色，或者把它同看音乐喜剧、喝劣质威士忌、与声名狼藉的粗鄙伙伴厮混到深夜混为一谈。他好笑地发现，放荡之徒比虔敬之徒无趣出许多倍，而最乏味的，还是那些鼓吹滥交、把他们那套「猪圈式混居」唤作「新道德」的人。

他深情地回到那座城，因为它是为他的爱而筑、为他的爱而妆点的。正如形而上学家们坚称，自我意识是一切思想不言而喻的根基；他也同样知道：正是她，使他寻见了自己；正是靠着她、为了她，一切真实的生活才得以存在。他感到，是安妮（Annie）教会了他那创造了阿瓦隆尼乌斯花园的稀世魔法。正是为了她，他才追寻奇秘，试图洞穿感官的奥秘；因为他能献给她的，唯有奇妙的思想与奇妙的生活，还有一具可怜的身体，带着他崇拜留下的斑斑伤痕。

===Original===

It was with this object, that of making the offering of himself a worthy one, that he continually searched for new and exquisite experiences. He made lovers come before him and confess their secrets; he pried into the inmost mysteries of innocence and shame, noting how passion and reluctance strive together for the mastery. In the amphitheatre he sometimes witnessed strange entertainments in which such tales as *Daphnis and Chloe* and *The Golden Ass* were performed before him. These shows were always given at nighttime; a circle of torchbearers surrounded the stage in the center, and above, all the tiers of seats were dark. He would look up at the soft blue of the summer sky, and at the vast dim mountain hovering like a cloud in the west, and then at the scene illumined by a flaring light, and contrasted with violent shadows. The subdued mutter of conversation in a strange language rising from bench after bench, swift hissing whispers of explanation, now and then a shout or a cry as the interest deepened, the restless tossing of the people as the end drew near, an arm lifted, a cloak thrown back, the sudden blaze of a torch lighting up purple or white or the gleam of gold in the black serried ranks; these were impressions that seemed always amazing. And above, the dusky light of the stars, around, the sweet-scented meadows, and the twinkle of lamps from the still city, the cry of the sentries about the walls, the wash of the tide filling the river, and the salt savour of the sea. With such a scenic ornament he saw the tale of Apuleius represented, heard the names of Fotis and Byrrhaena and Lucius proclaimed, and the deep intonation of such sentences as *Ecce Veneris hortator et armiger Liber advenit ultro*. The tale went on through all its marvelous adventures, and Lucian left the amphitheatre and walked beside the river where he could hear indistinctly the noise of voices and the singing Latin, and note how the rumor of the stage mingled with the murmur of the shuddering reeds and the cool lapping of the tide. Then came the farewell of the cantor, the thunder of applause, the crash of cymbals, the calling of the flutes, and the surge of the wind in the great dark wood.

===Chinese===

正是为了这个——把自己这份祭品献得相称——他才不断追寻崭新而精妙的经验。他召情人们到面前吐露他们的秘密；他刺探纯真与羞耻最深处的奥秘，留意激情与抗拒如何争夺着主宰权。在圆形剧场里，他有时观看奇异的演出，诸如*《达夫尼斯与克洛厄》*（Daphnis and Chloe）与*《金驴记》*（The Golden Ass）这样的故事，就在他眼前搬演。这些演出总在夜间举行；一圈执炬者环围着居中的戏台，上方，一排排坐席全黑着。他仰望夏夜柔和的蓝天，望西天如云悬停的莽莽苍苍的大山，再看被熊熊火光照亮、又被浓烈阴影反衬的戏景。一层又一层坐席上升起陌生语言的低低絮语，急促嘶嘶的解说耳语，情节愈紧处时而爆出的呼喝与惊叫，结局将近时人群不安的骚动，一条手臂扬起，一领斗篷甩开，一支火把骤然喷出的光焰，照亮黑压压密匝匝人丛里的紫袍、白袍或金光——这些印象，回回都令人惊叹。头顶，是星星暗淡的光；四野，是芬芳的草甸，静谧城中灯火的明灭，城墙上周遭哨兵的呼号，涨满河床的潮水的荡漾，还有海水的咸腥。以这样一派景致为饰，他看着阿普列乌斯（Apuleius）的故事搬演，听着福提斯（Fotis）、比尔赫纳（Byrrhaena）与卢基乌斯（Lucius）的名字被高声唱出，听着诸如 *Ecce Veneris hortator et armiger Liber advenit ultro*——「看哪，维纳斯的怂恿者与执械侍从利柏尔，翩然自来」——这样句子的沉沉诵调。故事历尽万般奇遇，一路演下去；卢西安走出圆形剧场，沿河漫步，隐约听得人声与拉丁语的歌唱，留意舞台的喧声如何与颤瑟芦苇的絮语、潮水清凉的舐岸声交融在一起。随后是领唱者的道别，掌声如雷，铙钹轰然，笛声唤鸣，还有大风掠过黑沉沉大森林的呼啸。

===Original===

At other times it was his chief pleasure to spend a whole day in a vineyard planted on the steep slope beyond the bridge. A grey stone seat had been placed beneath a shady laurel, and here he often sat without motion or gesture for many hours. Below him the tawny river swept round the town in a half circle; he could see the swirl of the yellow water, its eddies and miniature whirlpools, as the tide poured up from the south. And beyond the river the strong circuit of the walls, and within, the city glittered like a charming piece of mosaic. He freed himself from the obtuse modern view of towns as places where human beings live and make money and rejoice or suffer, for from the standpoint of the moment such facts were wholly impertinent. He knew perfectly well that for his present purpose the tawny sheen and shimmer of the tide was the only fact of importance about the river, and so he regarded the city as a curious work in jewelry. Its radiant marble porticoes, the white walls of the villas, a dome of burning copper, the flash and scintillation of tiled roofs, the quiet red of brickwork, dark groves of ilex, and cypress, and laurel, glowing rose-gardens, and here and there the silver of a fountain, seemed arranged and contrasted with a wonderful art, and the town appeared a delicious ornament, every cube of colour owing its place to the thought and inspiration of the artificer. Lucian, as he gazed from his arbour amongst the trellised vines, lost none of the subtle pleasures of the sight; noting every nuance of colour, he let his eyes dwell for a moment on the scarlet flash of poppies, and then on a glazed roof which in the glance of the sun seemed to spout white fire. A square of vines was like some rare green stone; the grapes were massed so richly amongst the vivid leaves, that even from far off there was a sense of irregular flecks and stains of purple running through the green. The laurel garths were like cool jade; the gardens, where red, yellow, blue and white gleamed together in a mist of heat, had the radiance of opal; the river was a band of dull gold. On every side, as if to enhance the preciousness of the city, the woods hung dark on the hills; above, the sky was violet, specked with minute feathery clouds, white as snowflakes. It reminded him of a beautiful bowl in his villa; the ground was of that same brilliant blue, and the artist had fused into the work, when it was hot, particles of pure white glass.

===Chinese===

另一些时候，他最大的乐事便是到桥对面陡坡上的葡萄园里消磨一整天。一株荫凉的月桂树下安着一张灰石座位，他常常在这里一坐几个钟头，不动不言。脚下，黄褐色的河水绕着城扫出半圆；潮水从南面涌上来时，他看得见黄水的漩卷、涡流与一个个小小的漩涡。河那边是城墙坚实的环围，墙内，城池闪烁，如一件迷人的镶嵌工艺品。他把自己从现代那种迟钝的城镇观里解放出来——那种把城镇当作人类居住、赚钱、欢乐或受苦之处的看法；从当下这个立足点看，这类事实全然文不对题。他一清二楚，就他此刻的目的而言，潮水黄褐色的光影波颤，才是这条河唯一要紧的事实；于是他把这座城看作一件奇巧的珠宝工艺。大理石门廊的熠熠光华，府邸的白墙，一座赤铜灼灼的穹顶，瓦顶的闪光与辉映，砖工沉静的红，冬青栎、柏树与月桂的幽暗树丛，灼灼燃烧的玫瑰园，间或一注喷泉的银亮——这一切仿佛以神奇的艺术安排着、对映着，全城便如一件美妙的饰物，每一方色块的位置，都归功于匠人的巧思与灵感。卢西安坐在棚架葡萄藤间的凉棚里眺望，这景致的种种微妙乐趣他一毫不失；他留意着色彩的每一重层次，让目光在罂粟猩红的闪光上流连片刻，又移向一面上了釉的屋顶，它在太阳的扫视下仿佛喷着白火。一方葡萄园如一块珍稀的绿宝石；葡萄在鲜亮的叶间结得那样繁密，远远望去，便觉有一片片不规则的紫色斑纹在绿中流淌。月桂园圃如凉沁的碧玉；座座花园里，红黄蓝白在暑雾中一同灼烁，有蛋白石的辉光；河是一条暗金的带子。四面八方，森林黑沉沉地悬在山峦上，仿佛要衬出这城的贵重；头顶，天是堇色的，点缀着细碎如羽的白云，白得像雪花。这让他想起府邸里一只美丽的碗；碗底正是那同一种明艳的蓝，匠人趁它炽热时，把纯白的玻璃细粒熔进了器身。

===Original===

For Lucian this was a spectacle that enchanted many hours; leaning on one hand, he would gaze at the city glowing in the sunlight till the purple shadows grew down the slopes and the long melodious trumpet sounded for the evening watch. Then, as he strolled beneath the trellises, he would see all the radiant facets glimmering out, and the city faded into haze, a white wall shining here and there, and the gardens veiled in a dim glow of colour. On such an evening he would go home with the sense that he had truly lived a day, having received for many hours the most acute impressions of beautiful colour.

===Chinese===

对卢西安来说，这是一场令人心醉多个时辰的景观；他一手支颐，凝望那在阳光中灼灼放光的城，直到紫色的影子顺坡长下来，直到悠长而旋律悠扬的号角为晚间守夜吹响。随后，他在棚架下漫步，看着所有的璀璨光面一一闪现，城池褪入薄霭，一堵白墙这里那里地闪亮，花园罩上一层朦胧的色晕。这样的傍晚回到家里，他怀着一种真切地活过了一天的感觉——多少个钟头里，他领受着美色最锐利的印象。

===Original===

Often he spent the night in the cool court of his villa, lying amidst soft cushions heaped upon the marble bench. A lamp stood on the table at his elbow, its light making the water in the cistern twinkle. There was no sound in the court except the soft continual plashing of the fountain. Throughout these still hours he would meditate, and he became more than ever convinced that man could, if he pleased, become lord of his own sensations. This, surely, was the true meaning concealed under the beautiful symbolism of alchemy. Some years before he had read many of the wonderful alchemical books of the later Middle Ages, and had suspected that something other than the turning of lead into gold was intended. This impression was deepened when he looked into *Lumen de Lumine* by Vaughan, the brother of the Silurist, and he had long puzzled himself in the endeavor to find a reasonable interpretation of the hermetic mystery, and of the red powder, “glistening and glorious in the sun.” And the solution shone out at last, bright and amazing, as he lay quiet in the court of Avallaunius. He knew that he himself had solved the riddle, that he held in his hand the powder of projection, the philosopher’s stone transmuting all it touched to fine gold; the gold of exquisite impressions. He understood now something of the alchemical symbolism; the crucible and the furnace, the “Green Dragon,” and the “Son Blessed of the Fire” had, he saw, a peculiar meaning. He understood, too, why the uninitiated were warned of the terror and danger through which they must pass; and the vehemence with which the adepts disclaimed all desire for material riches no longer struck him as singular. The wise man does not endure the torture of the furnace in order that he may be able to compete with operators in pork and company promoters; neither a steam yacht, nor a grouse-moor, nor three liveried footmen would add at all to his gratifications. Again Lucian said to himself:

===Chinese===

他常常在府邸荫凉的庭院里过夜，躺卧在大理石长椅上堆叠的软垫之间。一盏灯立在手边的桌上，灯光映得蓄水池里的水微微闪动。庭院里万籁俱寂，只有喷泉柔波不息的泼洒。在这岑寂的时辰里他冥想，愈发确信：人只要愿意，便能做自己感觉的主人。这，想必才是炼金术那一套美丽象征之下藏着的真义。几年前他读过许多中世纪晚期奇妙的炼金术书籍，早已疑心其中所指的，并非只是铅化为金。当他细读「西卢尔人」沃恩（Vaughan）的兄弟所著的*《光中之光》*（Lumen de Lumine）时，这印象又深了一层；他久久苦思，力图为这赫尔墨斯的秘奥、为那「在太阳下熠熠生辉、荣光焕然」的红粉，寻一个合理的解释。而当他静静躺在阿瓦隆尼乌斯的庭院里时，谜底终于亮了出来，明澈而惊人。他知道，他自己已经解开了这谜；他手里握着点金粉，握着那把它所触的一切都点化成纯金的哲人石——精妙印象的黄金。如今他懂得了炼金象征的一二分深意；坩埚与炉膛，「绿龙」与「受火祝福之子」，他看出，都各有其殊义。他也懂得了，为什么入道未深者总被警告将经历恐怖与凶险；而术士们那样激烈地撇清对物质财富的一切贪求，也不复令他诧异。智者忍受炉火之刑，不是为了能同做猪肉生意的和发起股份公司的一争高下；汽艇也好，松鸡猎沼也好，三名号衣仆从也好，都给他欢愉添不了分毫。卢西安又对自己说：

===Original===

“Only in the court of Avallaunius is the true science of the exquisite to be found.”

He saw the true gold into which the beggarly matter of existence may be transmuted by spagyric art; a succession of delicious moments, all the rare flavors of life concentrated, purged of their lees, and preserved in a beautiful vessel. The moonlight fell green on the fountain and on the curious pavements, and in the long sweet silence of the night he lay still and felt that thought itself was an acute pleasure, to be expressed perhaps in terms of odour or colour by the true artist.

And he gave himself other and even stranger gratifications. Outside the city walls, between the baths and the amphitheatre, was a tavern, a place where wonderful people met to drink wonderful wine. There he saw priests of Mithras and Isis and of more occult rites from the East, men who wore robes of bright colours, and grotesque ornaments, symbolizing secret things. They spoke amongst themselves in a rich jargon of coloured words, full of hidden meanings and the sense of matters unintelligible to the uninitiated, alluding to what was concealed beneath roses, and calling each other by strange names. And there were actors who gave the shows in the amphitheatre, officers of the legion who had served in wild places, singers, and dancing girls, and heroes of strange adventure.

===Chinese===

「唯有在阿瓦隆尼乌斯的庭院，才能寻见精妙之学的真髓。」

他看见了真金——存在这贫乏的质料，凭斯帕吉里克秘术（spagyric art）便可点化成的真金：一连串甘美的时刻，生活全部的珍稀滋味浓缩于此，滤净了渣滓，封存在一只美丽的器皿里。月光泛着绿意，落在喷泉上，落在奇妙的镶嵌地面上；在夜漫长甜美的寂静里，他静静躺着，感到思想本身就是一种锐利的欢愉，或许可由真正的艺术家，用气味或颜色抒写出来。

他还给自己另一些更为古怪的享乐。城墙外，浴场与圆形剧场之间，有一家酒馆，一个奇妙的人们相聚喝奇妙的酒的地方。在那里，他见到密特拉与伊西斯（Isis）的祭司，还有来自东方更幽秘仪式的祭司；这些人穿着色彩明艳的长袍，佩着古怪的饰物，象征着隐秘的事物。他们彼此交谈，用一种辞藻斑斓的丰富行话，满是暗藏的意义，满是未入道者无从索解的事理，暗指着玫瑰之下所藏的东西，以古怪的名字相称。还有在圆形剧场演戏的伶人、在蛮荒之地服役过的军团军官、歌者、舞姬，和种种奇遇中的英雄。

===Original===

The walls of the tavern were covered with pictures painted in violent hues; blues and reds and greens jarring against one another and lighting up the gloom of the place. The stone benches were always crowded, the sunlight came in through the door in a long bright beam, casting a dancing shadow of vine leaves on the further wall. There a painter had made a joyous figure of the young Bacchus driving the leopards before him with his ivy-staff, and the quivering shadow seemed a part of the picture. The room was cool and dark and cavernous, but the scent and heat of the summer gushed in through the open door. There was ever a full sound, with noise and vehemence, there, and the rolling music of the Latin tongue never ceased.

“The wine of the siege, the wine that we saved,” cried one.

“Look for the jar marked Faunus; you will be glad.”

“Bring me the wine of the Owl’s Face.”

“Let us have the wine of Saturn’s Bridge.”

===Chinese===

酒馆的墙上覆满浓烈色调的绘画；蓝、红、绿互相冲撞着，照亮了这里的幽暗。石凳上永远拥挤不堪，阳光从门口投进一道长长的亮光柱，在对面的墙上投下藤叶舞动的影子。那里，一位画师画下一个欢腾的形象：年轻的巴克斯（Bacchus）挥着常春藤杖，驱赶着豹子走在前头，而颤动的影子仿佛成了画的一部分。屋子荫凉、幽暗，如洞穴一般，夏天的香气与热气却从敞开的门口涌进来。那里永远满是喧声，带着噪嚷与激烈，拉丁语铿锵的音乐永不停歇。

「围城时的酒，我们省下来的酒，」有人喊道。

「找那口法乌努斯（Faunus）标记的酒坛；你们会欢喜的。」

「给我拿鸮面酒来。」

「咱们喝萨图恩之桥酒吧。」

===Original===

The boys who served brought the wine in dull red jars that struck a charming note against their white robes. They poured out the violet and purple and golden wine with calm sweet faces as if they were assisting in the mysteries, without any sign that they heard the strange words that flashed from side to side. The cups were all of glass; some were of deep green, of the colour of the sea near the land, flawed and specked with the bubbles of the furnace. Others were of brilliant scarlet, streaked with irregular bands of white, and having the appearance of white globules in the moulded stem. There were cups of dark glowing blue, deeper and more shining than the blue of the sky, and running through the substance of the glass were veins of rich gamboge yellow, twining from the brim to the foot. Some cups were of a troubled and clotted red, with alternating blotches of dark and light, some were variegated with white and yellow stains, some wore a film of rainbow colours, some glittered, shot with gold threads through the clear crystal, some were as if sapphires hung suspended in running water, some sparkled with the glint of stars, some were black and golden like tortoiseshell.

A strange feature was the constant and fluttering motion of hands and arms. Gesture made a constant commentary on speech; white fingers, whiter arms, and sleeves of all colours, hovered restlessly, appeared and disappeared with an effect of threads crossing and re-crossing on the loom. And the odour of the place was both curious and memorable; something of the damp cold breath of the cave meeting the hot blast of summer, the strangely mingled aromas of rare wines as they fell plashing and ringing into the cups, the drugged vapor of the East that the priests of Mithras and Isis bore from their steaming temples; these were always strong and dominant. And the women were scented, sometimes with unctuous and overpowering perfumes, and to the artist the experiences of those present were hinted in subtle and delicate nuances of odour.

They drank their wine and caressed all day in the tavern. The women threw their round white arms about their lover’s necks, they intoxicated them with the scent of their hair, the priests muttered their fantastic jargon of Theurgy. And through the sonorous clash of voices there always seemed the ring of the cry:

===Chinese===

侍酒的童子们把酒盛在暗红的坛子里端来，在他们的白袍映衬下敲出一记迷人的音符。他们神色沉静甜美地斟着紫罗兰色、紫色和金色的酒，仿佛在襄助秘仪，四下里飞来飞去的古怪字眼，他们听了没有半点痕迹。杯子全是玻璃的；有的是深绿色，近陆海水的颜色，带着炉火的气泡留下的瑕疵与斑点。有的是艳丽的猩红，夹着一道道不规则的白纹，模铸的杯脚里嵌着白色的小珠泡。有幽幽灼灼深蓝的杯子，比天空的蓝更深、更亮，玻璃的肌理里贯穿着浓郁的藤黄色脉，从杯口一直缠绕到杯脚。有的杯子是浑浊凝滞的红，深一块浅一块地斑驳；有的杂着白与黄的渍痕；有的罩着一层彩虹色的薄膜；有的闪闪发光，清澈的水晶里穿织着金丝；有的仿佛悬在流水中的蓝宝石；有的闪着星星的微芒；有的黑金相间，如龟甲一般。

一个奇特的景象，是手与臂永不停歇的翻飞。手势给言谈作着连绵的注解；白皙的手指、更白的手臂、五颜六色的衣袖，无休无止地盘旋，忽隐忽现，宛如织机上经纬交叠往复的丝线。这地方的气味也奇特而难忘：洞穴阴冷潮湿的气息，与盛夏的热风相迎；名贵美酒落进杯中时叮咚溅响、奇妙交融的芬芳；密特拉与伊西斯的祭司们从热气蒸腾的神庙里携来的、东方的迷醉烟雾——这些气味永远浓烈而强势。女人们也熏着香，有时是油润逼人的浓香；而在艺术家闻来，在场者各自的阅历，都由气味精微的层次暗示了出来。

他们在酒馆里整日饮酒、爱抚。女人们把浑圆白皙的手臂绕上情人的脖颈，用发间的香气把他们灌醉；祭司们喃喃诵着他们通神术（Theurgy）的奇诡行话。而穿过人声的铿锵喧豗，永远仿佛回响着那一声呼喊：

===Original===

“Look for the jar marked Faunus; you will be glad.”

Outside, the vine tendrils shook on the white walls glaring in the sunshine; the breeze swept up from the yellow river, pungent with the salt sea savour.

===Chinese===

「找那口法乌努斯标记的酒坛；你们会欢喜的。」

外面，葡萄卷须在白墙上颤动，墙面在烈日下白得晃眼；微风从黄色的河上卷上来，带着海水的咸腥。

===Original===

These tavern scenes were often the subject of Lucian’s meditation as he sat amongst the cushions on the marble seat. The rich sound of the voices impressed him above all things, and he saw that words have a far higher reason than the utilitarian office of imparting a man’s thought. The common notion that language and linked words are important only as a means of expression he found a little ridiculous; as if electricity were to be studied solely with the view of “wiring” to people, and all its other properties left unexplored, neglected. Language, he understood, was chiefly important for the beauty of its sounds, by its possession of words resonant, glorious to the ear, by its capacity, when exquisitely arranged, of suggesting wonderful and indefinable impressions, perhaps more ravishing and farther removed from the domain of strict thought than the impressions excited by music itself. Here lay hidden the secret of the sensuous art of literature; it was the secret of suggestion, the art of causing delicious sensation by the use of words. In a way, therefore, literature was independent of thought; the mere English listener, if he had an ear attuned, could recognize the beauty of a splendid Latin phrase.

===Chinese===

这些酒馆场景，常常是卢西安倚在大理石长椅软垫间冥想的题目。人声的丰润音响给他的印象压倒一切；他看出，词语有着远比传递思想这一实用职分更高的理由。那种认为语言与连贯的词句只作为表达手段才要紧的通行观念，他觉得有几分可笑；仿佛研究电，只是为了给人「发电报」，其余的性质一概不去探索、弃置不顾。他懂得了，语言之要紧，首先在其声音之美，在它拥有那些铿锵入耳、煌煌悦耳的词，在它一经精妙排布、便能唤起奇妙而无可名状的印象的能力——那印象或许比音乐本身激起的印象更加销魂，离严格思想的疆域也更远。感官的文学艺术，其秘密就藏在这里；那是暗示的秘密，是用词语唤起甘美感觉的艺术。因此，在某种意义上，文学独立于思想；一个只懂英语的听者，只要耳朵调准了，也能辨出一句辉煌拉丁语的妙处。

===Original===

Here was the explanation of the magic of “Lycidas.” From the standpoint of the formal understanding it was an affected lament over some wholly uninteresting and unimportant Mr. King; it was full of nonsense about “shepherds” and “flocks” and “muses” and such stale stock of poetry; the introduction of St. Peter on a stage thronged with nymphs and river gods was blasphemous, absurd, and, in the worst taste; there were touches of greasy Puritanism, the twang of the conventicle was only too apparent. And “Lycidas” was probably the most perfect piece of pure literature in existence; because every word and phrase and line were sonorous, ringing and echoing with music.

===Chinese===

这里正是《利西达斯》魔力的解释。从形式理智的立足点看，它不过是对某位全然乏味无谓的金先生（Mr. King）的一篇矫饰挽歌；它满篇是「牧人」「羊群」「缪斯」之类的胡话，尽是诗歌的陈货旧栈；让圣彼得（St. Peter）登上一座挤满宁芙与河神的水榭，是渎神、荒唐，而且趣味恶俗到了极处；其中还有几抹油乎乎的清教主义，非国教礼拜聚会的鼻音腔调再明显不过。而《利西达斯》却多半是存世纯粹文学中最完美的一篇；因为它的每一个词、每一个词组、每一行，都铿锵作响，回荡着音乐。

===Original===

“Literature,” he re-enunciated in his mind, “is the sensuous art of causing exquisite impressions by means of words.”

And yet there was something more; besides the logical thought, which was often a hindrance, a troublesome though inseparable accident, besides the sensation, always a pleasure and a delight, besides these there were the indefinable inexpressible images which all fine literature summons to the mind. As the chemist in his experiments is sometimes astonished to find unknown, unexpected elements in the crucible or the receiver, as the world of material things is considered by some a thin veil of the immaterial universe, so he who reads wonderful prose or verse is conscious of suggestions that cannot be put into words, which do not rise from the logical sense, which are rather parallel to than connected with the sensuous delight. The world so disclosed is rather the world of dreams, rather the world in which children sometimes live, instantly appearing, and instantly vanishing away, a world beyond all expression or analysis, neither of the intellect nor of the senses. He called these fancies of his “Meditations of a Tavern,” and was amused to think that a theory of letters should have risen from the eloquent noise that rang all day about the violet and golden wine.

===Chinese===

「文学，」他在心里重新申明，「是用词语唤起精妙印象的感官艺术。」

然而还有更多的东西；在逻辑思想——它常常是一种妨碍，一桩麻烦而不可分割的意外——之外，在感觉——它永远是一种愉悦与欢喜——之外，还有那些一切美好文学召入心头的、无可名状不可言传的形象。正如化学家在实验中，有时惊愕地在坩埚或接收器里发现未知的、出乎意料的元素；正如物质世界被有些人视为非物质宇宙的一层薄纱；读绝妙散文或诗歌的人，也意识到种种无法诉诸言辞的暗示，它们并非从逻辑意义中升起，与其说与感官的愉悦相连，不如说与之并行。由此显豁出来的世界，毋宁说是梦的世界，毋宁说是孩子们有时栖居的那个世界，倏然而现，倏然而逝，一个超出一切表达与分析的世界，既不属于智性，也不属于感官。他把他这些玄想唤作《酒馆冥想录》（Meditations of a Tavern），想到一套文学理论竟自紫与金色的美酒旁整日萦回的雄辩喧声中升起，不禁莞尔。

===Original===

“Let us seek for more exquisite things,” said Lucian to himself. He could almost imagine the magic transmutation of the senses accomplished, the strong sunlight was an odour in his nostrils; it poured down on the white marble and the palpitating roses like a flood. The sky was a glorious blue, making the heart joyous, and the eyes could rest in the dark green leaves and purple shadow of the ilex. The earth seemed to burn and leap beneath the sun, he fancied he could see the vine tendrils stir and quiver in the heat, and the faint fume of the scorching pine needles was blown across the gleaming garden to the seat beneath the porch. Wine was before him in a cup of carved amber; a wine of the colour of a dark rose, with a glint as of a star or of a jet of flame deep beneath the brim; and the cup was twined about with a delicate wreath of ivy. He was often loath to turn away from the still contemplation of such things, from the mere joy of the violent sun, and the responsive earth. He loved his garden and the view of the tessellated city from the vineyard on the hill, the strange clamor of the tavern, and white Fotis appearing on the torch-lit stage. And there were shops in the town in which he delighted, the shops of the perfume makers, and jewelers, and dealers in curious ware. He loved to see all things made for ladies’ use, to touch the gossamer silks that were to touch their bodies, to finger the beads of amber and the gold chains which would stir above their hearts, to handle the carved hairpins and brooches, to smell odours which were already dedicated to love.

===Chinese===

「让我们追寻更精妙的东西吧，」卢西安对自己说。他几乎能想象感官的魔法转化已然完成，炽烈的阳光成了他鼻中的一缕气味；它如洪流般倾泻在白色大理石与悸动的玫瑰上。天空是一片辉煌的蓝，叫人心生欢喜，眼睛可以栖歇在冬青栎深绿的叶与紫色的影里。大地仿佛在太阳下面燃烧、跃动；他恍惚看见葡萄卷须在暑热里搅动颤瑟，灼烫松针的淡淡青烟被吹过灼灼的花园，送到门廊下的座椅边。他面前的一只雕花琥珀杯里盛着酒；那酒是深玫瑰的颜色，杯口深处闪着一星光辉，如一颗星，或一注火焰；杯身上缠绕着一环精致的常春藤花饰。他常常舍不得从这些事物的静观中、从烈日与应和的大地这份单纯的欢喜中转开眼去。他爱他的花园，爱从山上葡萄园望见的镶嵌之城，爱酒馆奇异的喧腾，爱雪白的福提斯在火炬照亮的戏台上现身。城里还有些他心爱的铺子：制香匠的、珠宝匠的、卖奇巧货品的铺子。他爱看一切为女人们制作的物事，爱抚那些将贴上她们肌肤的薄纱丝绸，捻动那些将在她们心口荡漾的琥珀珠与金链，把玩雕花的发簪与胸针，嗅闻那些早已奉献给了爱的香气。

===Original===

But though these were sweet and delicious gratifications, he knew that there were more exquisite things of which he might be a spectator. He had seen the folly of regarding fine literature from the standpoint of the logical intellect, and he now began to question the wisdom of looking at life as if it were a moral representation. Literature, he knew, could not exist without some meaning, and considerations of right and wrong were to a certain extent inseparable from the conception of life, but to insist on ethics as the chief interest of the human pageant was surely absurd. One might as well read “Lycidas” for the sake of its denunciation of “our corrupted Clergy,” or Homer for “manners and customs.” An artist entranced by a beautiful landscape did not greatly concern himself with the geological formation of the hills, nor did the lover of a wild sea inquire as to the chemical analysis of the water. Lucian saw a coloured and complex life displayed before him, and he sat enraptured at the spectacle, not concerned to know whether actions were good or bad, but content if they were curious.

===Chinese===

然而尽管这些是甜美可口的享受，他知道还有更精妙的事情，可让他做个旁观者。他已见识过用逻辑智力的立足点去看待精美文学的愚蠢，如今又开始怀疑，把生活当作一场道德演出来看待，是否也算智慧。他知道文学离了意义便不能存在，是非的考量也在某种程度上与生活的概念不可分；可硬要把伦理当作人生这出大戏的头等旨趣，无疑是荒谬的。那还不如为了其中对「我们腐败的教士」的申斥去读《利西达斯》，或者为了「风俗制度」去读荷马（Homer）。一位陶醉于美丽风景的艺术家，不会太操心山峦的地质构造；狂野大海的恋人，也不会去打听海水的化学分析。卢西安看见一场彩色斑斓、繁复错综的生活在他面前铺展，他心醉神迷地坐在那里观看这出好戏，不关心行动是善是恶，只求它们奇诡好看。

===Original===

In this spirit he made a singular study of corruption. Beneath his feet, as he sat in the garden porch, was a block of marble through which there ran a scarlet stain. It began with a faint line, thin as a hair, and grew as it advanced, sending out offshoots to right and left, and broadening to a pool of brilliant red. There were strange lives into which he looked that were like the block of marble; women with grave sweet faces told him the astounding tale of their adventures, and how, they said, they had met the faun when they were little children. They told him how they had played and watched by the vines and the fountains, and dallied with the nymphs, and gazed at images reflected in the water pools, till the authentic face appeared from the wood. He heard others tell how they had loved the satyrs for many years before they knew their race; and there were strange stories of those who had longed to speak but knew not the word of the enigma, and searched in all strange paths and ways before they found it.

===Chinese===

他以这样的精神，对堕落做了一番奇特的研究。他坐在花园门廊里，脚下方有一块大理石，其中贯穿着一道猩红的纹。它起初是一条淡线，细如发丝，越行越深，向左右分出支脉，渐渐漫宽成一泊艳红。他窥看过的那些奇特生命，便如这块大理石；面容端凝甜美的妇人们，给他讲她们历险的惊人事，讲她们如何——她们说——在小小年纪就遇见了法翁（faun）。她们告诉他，她们如何在葡萄藤与泉水边嬉戏守望，与宁芙们调笑嬉闹，凝望水潭中倒映的影，直到那真正的面容从林间现身。他听另一些人讲，她们如何爱了萨提尔（satyr）许多年，才认清他们的族类；还有些奇异的故事，讲那些渴望开口却不识谜语字眼的人，如何在一切奇僻的路径上摸索，方才寻见它。

===Original===

He heard the history of the woman who fell in love with her slave-boy, and tempted him for three years in vain. He heard the tale from the woman’s full red lips, and watched her face, full of the ineffable sadness of lust, as she described her curious stratagems in mellow phrases. She was drinking a sweet yellow wine from a gold cup as she spoke, and the odour in her hair and the aroma of the precious wine seemed to mingle with the soft strange words that flowed like an unguent from a carven jar. She told how she bought the boy in the market of an Asian city, and had him carried to her house in the grove of fig-trees. “Then,” she went on, “he was led into my presence as I sat between the columns of my court. A blue veil was spread above to shut out the heat of the sun, and rather twilight than light shone on the painted walls, and the wonderful colours of the pavement, and the images of Love and the Mother of Love. The men who brought the boy gave him over to my girls, who undressed him before me, one drawing gently away his robe, another stroking his brown and flowing hair, another praising the whiteness of his limbs, and another caressing him, and speaking loving words in his ear. But the boy looked sullenly at them all, striking away their hands, and pouting with his lovely and splendid lips, and I saw a blush, like the rosy veil of dawn, reddening his body and his cheeks. Then I made them bathe him, and anoint him with scented oils from head to foot, till his limbs shone and glistened with the gentle and mellow glow of an ivory statue. Then I said: ‘You are bashful, because you shine alone amongst us all; see, we too will be your fellows.’ The girls began first of all, fondling and kissing one another, and doing for each other the offices of waiting-maids. They drew out the pins and loosened the bands of their hair, and I never knew before that they were so lovely. The soft and shining tresses flowed down, rippling like sea-waves; some had hair golden and radiant as this wine in my cup, the faces of others appeared amidst the blackness of ebony; there were locks that seemed of burnished and scintillating copper, some glowed with hair of tawny splendor, and others were crowned with the brightness of the sardonyx. Then, laughing, and without the appearance of shame, they unfastened the brooches and bands which sustained their robes, and so allowed silk and linen to flow swiftly to the stained floor, so that one would have said there was a sudden apparition of the fairest nymphs. With many festive and jocose words they began to incite each other to mirth, praising the beauties that shone on every side, and calling the boy by a girl’s name, they invited him to be their playmate. But he refused, shaking his head, and still standing dumbfounded and abashed, as if he saw a forbidden and terrible spectacle. Then I ordered the women to undo my hair and my clothes, making them caress me with the tenderness of the fondest lover, but without avail, for the foolish boy still scowled and pouted out his lips, stained with an imperial and glorious scarlet.”

===Chinese===

他听过那个爱上自家奴隶男孩的妇人的经历，她引诱了他三年，徒劳无功。这故事是从那妇人丰润的红唇间听来的；他望着她的脸，那脸上满是一种情欲的无可言说的悲凉，她一面用圆润的词句，描述着她一桩桩奇巧的计谋。她说话时正用一只金杯喝着甜黄酒，她发间的气息与美酒的芳香，仿佛同那些柔软的奇字句交融为一，如香膏从雕花的罐中缓缓流泻。她讲她如何在一座亚细亚城市的集市上买下这男孩，如何让人把他抬到她无花果树林中的宅邸。「于是，」她接着说，「他们把男孩领到我面前时，我正坐在庭院的柱廊之间。头顶张着一幅蓝纱，遮蔽太阳的炎热；照在彩绘的墙上、照在地面绚烂的色彩上、照在爱神与爱神之母的像上的，与其说是光，不如说是暮色。带来男孩的人们把他交给我的侍女们，她们当着我的面为他宽衣：一个轻轻褪下他的袍子，一个抚着他棕色飘逸的头发，一个夸赞他肢体的白皙，又一个爱抚着他，在他耳边说着温存话。可男孩阴沉地望着她们所有人，打开她们的手，用他那可爱而华美的嘴唇撅着嘴，我看见一抹红晕，如黎明的玫瑰色面纱，染红了他的身体和双颊。于是我吩咐她们为他沐浴，从头到脚涂上香膏，直到他的四肢莹莹发光，带着象牙雕像温润柔和的光泽。于是我说：『你害羞，是因为你独自一个在我们中间闪耀；看，我们也来做你的同伴。』侍女们先彼此爱抚亲吻起来，互相尽着贴身侍女的职分。她们拔下发簪，解开束发的带子，我从前竟不知道她们这样美。柔软光亮的长发倾泻而下，如海波般起伏涟漪；有的头发金黄流光，如我杯中的这酒；有的面庞在乌檀般的黑发间显现；有的发绺如擦亮的铜，灼灼生辉；有的顶着黄褐的华彩，另一些则冠以红缟玛瑙的明光。随后她们笑着，毫无羞色地解开承住衣袍的胸针与束带，任丝绸与麻衣倏然滑落到彩色的地面上，让人只道是一群最美宁芙的骤然显形。她们说了许多节庆的戏谑话，彼此逗弄取乐，夸赞着四下闪耀的美色，又用一个女孩的名字唤那男孩，邀他来做她们的游伴。可他摇头拒绝了，依旧怔忡羞赧地立着，仿佛看见了一场禁忌而可怕的景象。于是我命侍女们解开我的头发、褪去我的衣裳，让她们以最深情的恋人般的温存爱抚我——却终归徒劳，因为这痴愚的男孩依旧蹙着眉，撅着他那染着帝王般华贵猩红的嘴唇。」

===Original===

She poured out more of the topaz-coloured wine in her cup, and Lucian saw it glitter as it rose to the brim and mirrored the gleam of the lamps. The tale went on, recounting a hundred strange devices. The woman told how she had tempted the boy by idleness and ease, giving him long hours of sleep, and allowing him to recline all day on soft cushions, that swelled about him, enclosing his body. She tried the experiment of curious odours: causing him to smell always about him the oil of roses, and burning in his presence rare gums from the East. He was allured by soft dresses, being clothed in silks that caressed the skin with the sense of a fondling touch. Three times a day they spread before him a delicious banquet, full of savour and odour and colour; three times a day they endeavored to intoxicate him with delicate wine.

“And so,” the lady continued, “I spared nothing to catch him in the glistening nets of love; taking only sour and contemptuous glances in return. And at last in an incredible shape I won the victory, and then, having gained a green crown, fighting in agony against his green and crude immaturity, I devoted him to the theatre, where he amused the people by the splendor of his death.”

===Chinese===

她又往杯中斟了些黄玉色的酒，卢西安看着它升到杯沿时粼粼闪光，映出灯焰的微芒。故事继续着，数说出上百种奇异的手段。妇人讲她如何用安逸与闲适引诱那男孩，让他长睡，准他整日斜倚在软垫上，软垫在他四周鼓起，裹住他的身体。她试过奇异气味的实验：让他身边永远弥漫着玫瑰油的香，在他面前焚烧东方来的珍稀香脂。她还用柔软的衣裳诱他：给他穿上那以爱抚之感触拂肌肤的丝绸。他们一天三次在他面前摆开甘美的盛宴，满是滋味、香气与颜色；一天三次，竭力用精酿的美酒把他灌醉。

「就这样，」夫人继续道，「我不惜一切，要把他捕进爱那闪亮的罗网；换来的却只有酸溜溜的、轻蔑的一瞥。最后，以一个难以置信的形态，我赢得了胜利；随后，赢得了绿冠——在苦战中战胜他青涩粗粝的懵懂——我把他献给了剧场，他在那里以他死亡的光华娱悦了众人。」

===Original===

On another evening he heard the history of the man who dwelt alone, refusing all allurements, and was at last discovered to be the lover of a black statue. And there were tales of strange cruelties, of men taken by mountain robbers, and curiously maimed and disfigured, so that when they escaped and returned to the town, they were thought to be monsters and killed at their own doors. Lucian left no dark or secret nook of life unvisited; he sat down, as he said, at the banquet, resolved to taste all the savours, and to leave no flagon unvisited.

His relations grew seriously alarmed about him at this period. While he heard with some inner ear the suave and eloquent phrases of singular tales, and watched the lamplight in amber and purple wine, his father saw a lean pale boy, with black eyes that burnt in hollows, and sad and sunken cheeks.

===Chinese===

另一个晚上，他听了那个独居男人的经历：那人拒斥一切诱惑，最后却被发现是一尊黑色雕像的情人。还有种种奇酷的故事：有人被山中的盗匪掳去，被古怪地伤残毁容，待他们逃回城里，人们只当是妖怪，把他们杀死在自家门口。生命里没有一个黑暗或隐秘的角落是卢西安不曾踏访的；如他自己所说，他在盛宴上坐了下来，决意尝遍一切滋味，不放过任何一坛酒。

这个时期，他的亲人们当真为他惊慌起来。当他用内里的耳朵听着奇谈那些温婉雄辩的词句，望着灯光在琥珀色与紫色的酒中摇曳时，他父亲看见的，却是一个瘦削苍白的男孩，黑眼睛在深窝里燃烧，双颊悲戚凹陷。

===Original===

“You ought to try and eat more, Lucian,” said the parson; “and why don’t you have some beer?”

He was looking feebly at the roast mutton and sipping a little water; but he would not have eaten or drunk with more relish if the choicest meat and drink had been before him.

His bones seemed, as Miss Deacon said, to be growing through his skin; he had all the appearance of an ascetic whose body has been reduced to misery by long and grievous penance. People who chanced to see him could not help saying to one another: “How ill and wretched that Lucian Taylor looks!” They were of course quite unaware of the joy and luxury in which his real life was spent, and some of them began to pity him, and to speak to him kindly.

===Chinese===

「你该想法子多吃一点，卢西安，」牧师说；「还有，你怎么不喝点啤酒呢？」

他有气无力地望着烤羊肉，小口啜着一点水；可即便最上乘的酒肉摆在他面前，他也吃不出、喝不出更多的滋味来。

正如迪肯小姐（Miss Deacon）所说，他的骨头仿佛要顶破皮肤长出来；他浑身是一副苦行者的模样，那身体已被漫长而惨烈的苦修磨折到了不幸的境地。偶然见他的人免不得彼此说：「卢西安·泰勒那样子瞧着，病得多重、多憔悴啊！」他们自然全然不知，他真正的生活浸在怎样的欢愉与奢华里；有些人开始可怜他，对他说话也和气起来。

===Original===

It was too late for that. The friendly words had as much lost their meaning as the words of contempt. Edward Dixon hailed him cheerfully in the street one day:

“Come in to my den, won’t you, old fellow?” he said. “You won’t see the pater. I’ve managed to bag a bottle of his old port. I know you smoke like a furnace, and I’ve got some ripping cigars. You will come, won’t you! I can tell you the pater’s booze is first rate.”

===Chinese===

但那已经太迟了。友好的话语同轻蔑的话语一样，早已失去了意义。一天，爱德华·迪克森（Edward Dixon）在街上快活地招呼他：

「到我的小窝里来坐坐，好不好，老伙计？」他说。「不会撞见老爷子的。我设法顺来了他一瓶陈年波特酒。我知道你抽烟抽得像个火炉，我还搞到了几支顶呱呱的雪茄。你会来的，对吧！我告诉你，老爷子的酒可是一等一的好。」

===Original===

He gently declined and went on. Kindness and unkindness, pity and contempt had become for him mere phrases; he could not have distinguished one from the other. Hebrew and Chinese, Hungarian and Pushtu would be pretty much alike to an agricultural labourer; if he cared to listen he might detect some general differences in sound, but all four tongues would be equally devoid of significance.

To Lucian, entranced in the garden of Avallaunius, it seemed very strange that he had once been so ignorant of all the exquisite meanings of life. Now, beneath the violet sky, looking through the brilliant trellis of the vines, he saw the picture; before, he had gazed in sad astonishment at the squalid rag which was wrapped about it.

===Chinese===

他温言谢绝了，继续走他的路。善意与恶意、怜悯与轻蔑，于他都成了空言；他已分不出彼此。希伯来语与汉语、匈牙利语与普什图语，在一个庄稼汉听来也相差无几；他若肯听，或许能听出些大体的声调之别，可这四种语言同样全无意义。

对沉醉在阿瓦隆尼乌斯花园里的卢西安来说，他竟曾对生命全部精妙的意蕴那般无知，这事显得多么奇怪。如今，在堇色的天空下，透过葡萄藤绚烂的棚架，他看见的是画本身；从前，他只是悲哀而错愕地，凝望着裹在那画外面的污破布。

## V / 第五章

===Original===
And he was at last in the city of the unending murmuring streets, a part of the stirring shadow, of the amber-lighted gloom.

It seemed a long time since he had knelt before his sweetheart in the lane, the moonfire streaming upon them from the dark circle of the fort, the air and the light and his soul full of haunting, the touch of the unimaginable thrilling his heart; and now he sat in a terrible “bed-sitting-room” in a western suburb, confronted by a heap and litter of papers on the desk of a battered old bureau.

He had put his breakfast-tray out on the landing, and was thinking of the morning’s work, and of some very dubious pages that he had blackened the night before. But when he had lit his disreputable briar, he remembered there was an unopened letter waiting for him on the table; he had recognized the vague, staggering script of Miss Deacon, his cousin. There was not much news; his father was “just the same as usual,” there had been a good deal of rain, the farmers expected to make a lot of cider, and so forth. But at the close of the letter Miss Deacon became useful for reproof and admonition.

===Chinese===
他终于来到了这座低语街巷永无休止的城里，成了那涌动的暗影的一部分，成了那被琥珀色灯光照亮的幽冥的一部分。

仿佛已经隔了悠悠长日，自从他在小径上跪在恋人面前，堡垒的黑环里月华如流火倾泻在两人身上，空气、光与他的灵魂都盈满萦回不去之物，那不可思议者的一触令他的心震颤；而如今，他坐在西郊一间可怕的「卧室兼起居室」（bed-sitting-room）里，面对着一张破旧写字柜的桌面上堆积散乱的纸堆。

他已把早餐托盘放到楼梯平台上，正想着早晨的功课，想着昨夜涂黑的那几页颇为可疑的文字。可等他点起那只寒酸的石楠木烟斗，才记起桌上还有一封没拆的信在等着他；他认出了表姐迪肯小姐（Miss Deacon）那笔虚浮歪斜、摇摇欲坠的字迹。信里没什么新闻；他父亲「还是老样子」，雨水下了不少，农户们指望酿出许多苹果酒，诸如此类。可是到了信的末尾，迪肯小姐便派上了训诫规劝的用场。

===Original===
“I was at Caermaen on Tuesday,” she said, “and called on the Gervases and the Dixons. Mr. Gervase smiled when I told him you were a literary man, living in London, and said he was afraid you wouldn’t find it a very practical career. Mrs. Gervase was very proud of Henry’s success; he passed fifth for some examination, and will begin with nearly four hundred a year. I don’t wonder the Gervases are delighted. Then I went to the Dixons, and had tea. Mrs. Dixon wanted to know if you had published anything yet, and I said I thought not. She showed me a book everybody is talking about, called the *Dog and the Doctor*. She says it’s selling by thousands, and that one can’t take up a paper without seeing the author’s name. She told me to tell you that you ought to try to write something like it. Then Mr. Dixon came in from the study, and your name was mentioned again. He said he was afraid you had made rather a mistake in trying to take up literature as if it were a profession, and seemed to think that a place in a house of business would be more *suitable* and more practical. He pointed out that you had not had the advantages of a university training, and said that you would find men who had made good friends, and had the *tone* of the university, would be before you at every step. He said Edward was doing very well at Oxford. He writes to them that he knows several noblemen, and that young Philip Bullingham (son of Sir John Bullingham) is his most intimate friend; of course this is *very* satisfactory for the Dixons. I am afraid, my dear Lucian, you have rather overrated your powers. Wouldn’t it be better, even now, to look out for some *real work* to do, instead of wasting your time over those silly old books? I know quite well how the Gervases and the Dixons feel; they think idleness so injurious for a young man, and likely to lead to *bad habits*. You know, my dear Lucian, I am only writing like this because of my affection for you, so I am sure, my dear boy, you won’t be offended.”

===Chinese===
「星期二我到凯尔梅恩（Caermaen）去了，」她写道，「拜望了格瓦斯一家（the Gervases）和迪克森一家（the Dixons）。我告诉格瓦斯先生，说你是个文人，住在伦敦，他笑了笑，说恐怕你会发现这营生不大实际。格瓦斯太太正为亨利的出息得意呢；他考某项文凭考了第五名，起薪便将近四百镑一年。格瓦斯一家欢天喜地，我一点也不奇怪。随后我到迪克森家去，用了茶。迪克森太太问你可曾出版过什么，我说我想还没有。她拿给我看一本人人都在谈论的书，叫*《狗与医生》*（Dog and the Doctor）。她说这书成千上万地卖，还说随便拿起一份报纸，没有不见着作者名字的。她让我告诉你，你该想法子写一本那样的东西。接着迪克森先生从书房进来，你的名字又被提到了。他说，恐怕你把文学当作一门职业来操持，是相当失策的，并且似乎觉得，到一家商行里谋个位子会更*合适*些、更实际些。他指出，你未尝沾过大学教育的益处，还说你会发觉，那些交了好朋友、带着大学*气派*的人，步步都要走在你前头。他说爱德华在牛津很出息。他写信回家，说他结识了好几位贵族，还说小菲利普·布林厄姆（Philip Bullingham，约翰·布林厄姆爵士之子）是他最要好的朋友；这当然让迪克森一家*十分*称心。我亲爱的卢西安（Lucian），恐怕你是把自己的能耐估量得太高了。哪怕到如今，寻一份*正经活计*做做，岂不更好，何苦把光阴都虚掷在那些愚蠢的旧书上？格瓦斯家和迪克森家的心思，我揣摩得透透彻彻；他们觉得游手好闲对年轻人最有害处，容易沾上*坏习气*。你知道，亲爱的卢西安，我这样絮絮叨叨，全是因为疼你，所以我相信，亲爱的孩子，你一定不会见怪的。」

===Original===
Lucian pigeonholed the letter solemnly in the receptacle lettered “Barbarians.” He felt that he ought to ask himself some serious questions: “Why haven’t I passed fifth? why isn’t Philip (son of Sir John) my most intimate friend? why am I an idler, liable to fall into bad habits?” but he was eager to get to his work, a curious and intricate piece of analysis. So the battered bureau, the litter of papers, and the thick fume of his pipe, engulfed him and absorbed him for the rest of the morning. Outside were the dim October mists, the dreary and languid life of a side street, and beyond, on the main road, the hum and jangle of the gliding trains. But he heard none of the uneasy noises of the quarter, not even the shriek of the garden gates nor the yelp of the butcher on his round, for delight in his great task made him unconscious of the world outside.

===Chinese===
卢西安郑重其事地把信归进了标着「野蛮人」字样的那一格。他觉得，自己理当问上几个严肃的问题：「我为什么没考第五？为什么菲利普（约翰爵士之子）不是我最亲密的朋友？我为什么是个闲人，随时可能沾上坏习气？」可他一心只想着去做他的功课——一项古怪而繁复的分析。于是，破旧的写字柜、散乱的纸堆、烟斗的浓烟，便把他吞没、把他吸纳了整整一个上午。外面是十月朦胧的雾气，是小街小巷沉闷恹恹的生活，再往外，大马路上是电车滑行而过的嗡鸣与叮当。可这一带种种不安的声息他一概不闻，连花园铁门的尖叫、屠夫走街串巷的吆喝也听不见，因为对这项大工程的耽爱，使他浑然忘却了外面的世界。

===Original===
He had come by curious paths to this calm hermitage between Shepherd’s Bush and Acton Vale. The golden weeks of the summer passed on in their enchanted procession, and Annie had not returned, neither had she written. Lucian, on his side, sat apart, wondering why his longing for her were not sharper. As he though of his raptures he would smile faintly to himself, and wonder whether he had not lost the world and Annie with it. In the garden of Avallaunius his sense of external things had grown dim and indistinct; the actual, material life seemed every day to become a show, a fleeting of shadows across a great white light. At last the news came that Annie Morgan had been married from her sister’s house to a young farmer, to whom, it appeared, she had been long engaged, and Lucian was ashamed to find himself only conscious of amusement, mingled with gratitude. She had been the key that opened the shut palace, and he was now secure on the throne of ivory and gold. A few days after he had heard the news he repeated the adventure of his boyhood; for the second time he scaled the steep hillside, and penetrated the matted brake. He expected violent disillusion, but his feeling was rather astonishment at the activity of boyish imagination. There was no terror nor amazement now in the green bulwarks, and the stunted undergrowth did not seem in any way extraordinary. Yet he did not laugh at the memory of his sensations, he was not angry at the cheat. Certainly it had been all illusion, all the heats and chills of boyhood, its thoughts of terror were without significance. But he recognized that the illusions of the child only differed from those of the man in that they were more picturesque; belief in fairies and belief in the Stock Exchange as bestowers of happiness were equally vain, but the latter form of faith was ugly as well as inept. It was better, he knew, and wiser, to wish for a fairy coach than to cherish longings for a well-appointed brougham and liveried servants.

===Chinese===
他一路循着奇诡的路径，来到了谢泼德布什（Shepherd's Bush）与阿克顿谷（Acton Vale）之间这处静穆的隐居之所。夏日金黄的几个星期，排着中了魔法的行列一路行过，而安妮（Annie）没有回来，也没有来信。卢西安这一边，独自坐在一旁，纳闷自己对她的渴望为何竟不再更灼烈些。想起自己的种种狂喜，他会对自己淡淡一笑，怀疑自己是不是已经失去了这个世界，连安妮也一并失去了。在阿瓦隆尼乌斯（Avallaunius）的花园里，他对外物的感知已变得黯淡模糊；实际的、物质的生活，似乎一天天成了一场戏，成了影子掠过一片巨大白光的倏忽闪现。终于有消息传来，说安妮·摩根（Annie Morgan）已从她姐姐家出阁，嫁给了一个年轻农夫——看样子，她与他早有婚约；而卢西安羞愧地发现，自己心里竟只意识到一阵好笑，还掺着感激。她曾是开启那座封闭宫殿的钥匙，而如今他稳稳坐在象牙与黄金的王座上。听到消息几天之后，他重演了少年时代的冒险；他第二次攀上陡峭的山坡，钻进了那片交缠密结的灌木丛。他料想会有一场猛烈的幻灭，可他的心情，倒更近乎对少年想象力的活跃感到惊诧。如今那些绿色的壁垒里再没有恐怖，也没有惊异，矮缩的下木也丝毫不见古怪。然而他没有嘲笑记忆中当年的种种感受，也没有为那场骗局动气。那固然全是幻觉，全是童年的燥热与寒颤，那些关于恐怖的念头本无意义。但他认识到，孩子的幻觉与成人的幻觉，分别只在前者更有画意；相信仙子能赐福与相信证券交易所能赐福，同属虚妄，只是后一种信仰既荒谬又丑陋。盼一辆仙人的马车，他知道，比渴求一驾配备齐整的轿式马车和穿号衣的仆从，更好，也更明智。

===Original===
He turned his back on the green walls and the dark oaks without any feeling of regret or resentment. After a little while he began to think of his adventures with pleasure; the ladder by which he had mounted had disappeared, but he was safe on the height. By the chance fancy of a beautiful girl he had been redeemed from a world of misery and torture, the world of external things into which he had come a stranger, by which he had been tormented. He looked back at a kind of vision of himself seen as he was a year before, a pitiable creature burning and twisting on the hot coals of the pit, crying lamentably to the laughing bystanders for but one drop of cold water wherewith to cool his tongue. He confessed to himself, with some contempt, that he had been a social being, depending for his happiness on the goodwill of others; he had tried hard to write, chiefly, it was true, from love of the art, but a little from a social motive. He had imagined that a written book and the praise of responsible journals would ensure him the respect of the county people. It was a quaint idea, and he saw the lamentable fallacies naked; in the first place, a painstaking artist in words was not respected by the respectable; secondly, books should not be written with the object of gaining the goodwill of the landed and commercial interests; thirdly and chiefly, no man should in any way depend on another.

===Chinese===
他转身背对绿色的壁垒与幽暗的橡树，心里没有一丝憾恨或怨怼。过了不多时，他开始怀着愉悦回想自己这一场场奇遇；他攀援而上的那架梯子已经消失，而他安然立于高处。凭着一位美丽姑娘偶然的一段情思，他被从悲惨与酷刑的世界里赎了出来——那外物的世界，他本是作为异乡人闯入其中，又受尽它的荼毒。他回望一年前的自己，恍如看一场幻象：一个可怜的造物，在深渊炙热的炭火上焚烧扭动，向着围观的哄笑者凄声哀告，只讨一滴凉水，凉凉他的舌头。他带着几分鄙夷向自己招认：他曾经是个社会性的生物，幸福须仰仗他人的善意；他也曾发奋著述，诚然主要出于对这门艺术的热爱，却也有几分出于社会性的动机。他曾设想，写成一部书、博得几家正经报刊的称扬，便能保他赢得郡中人士的敬重。这是个古雅的念头，如今那可悲的种种谬误，他已看得赤裸裸：其一，苦心经营的文字艺术家，体面人是不敬重的；其二，书不该抱着博取地产与商业势力好意的目地去写；其三，也是最主要的，人无论如何不该依附于另一个人。

===Original===
From this utter darkness, from danger of madness, the ever dear and sweet Annie had rescued him. Very beautifully and fitly, as Lucian thought, she had done her work without any desire to benefit him, she had simply willed to gratify her own passion, and in doing this had handed to him the priceless secret. And he, on his side, had reversed the process; merely to make himself a splendid offering for the acceptance of his sweetheart, he had cast aside the vain world, and had found the truth, which now remained with him, precious and enduring.

And since the news of the marriage he found that his worship of her had by no means vanished; rather in his heart was the eternal treasure of a happy love, untarnished and spotless; it would be like a mirror of gold without alloy, bright and lustrous forever. For Lucian, it was no defect in the woman that she was desirous and faithless; he had not conceived an affection for certain moral or intellectual accidents, but for the very woman. Guided by the self-evident axiom that humanity is to be judged by literature, and not literature by humanity, he detected the analogy between “Lycidas” and Annie. Only the dullard would object to the nauseous cant of the one, or to the indiscretions of the other. A sober critic might say that the man who could generalize Herbert and Laud, Donne and Herrick, Sanderson and Juxon, Hammond and Lancelot Andrewes into “our corrupted Clergy” must be either an imbecile or a scoundrel, or probably both. The judgment would be perfectly true, but as a criticism of “Lycidas” it would be a piece of folly. In the case of the woman one could imagine the attitude of the conventional lover; of the chevalier who, with his tongue in his cheek, “reverences and respects” all women, and coming home early in the morning writes a leading article on St. English Girl. Lucian, on the other hand, felt profoundly grateful to the delicious Annie, because she had at precisely the right moment voluntarily removed her image from his way. He confessed to himself that, latterly, he had a little dreaded her return as an interruption; he had shivered at the thought that their relations would become what was so terribly called an “intrigue” or “affair.” There would be all the threadbare and common stratagems, the vulgarity of secret assignations, and an atmosphere suggesting the period of Mr. Thomas Moore and Lord Byron and “segars.” Lucian had been afraid of all this; he had feared lest love itself should destroy love.

===Chinese===
从这彻底的黑暗，从疯狂的危崖边缘，是永远亲爱甜美的安妮救出了他。而且，正如卢西安所想，她这件事做得美妙而得体：她全无益他之心，一心只想餍足自己的情热，而恰恰在这样做的时候，她把那无价的秘密交到了他手里。而他这一边，则把这个过程倒了过来；仅仅为了把自己妆成一宗辉煌的祭献，好献到恋人面前受纳，他抛开了虚荣的尘世，却找到了真理——如今这真理留驻在他身上，珍贵而恒久。

而自婚讯传来，他发现自己对她的崇拜并未消散；他心里所有的，毋宁说是一宗幸福之爱的永恒宝藏，无垢无瑕；它将像一面不掺合金的金镜，永远明亮生辉。在卢西安看来，这女子情热易变，并非她的缺憾；他倾心爱慕的，从来不是附着于她的若干道德或心智上的偶然品质，而是这女子本身。他本着那条不证自明的公理——应当以文学判断人性，而非以人性判断文学——识出了《利西达斯》（Lycidas）与安妮之间的类比。只有愚钝的人，才会去反感这一位的令人生厌的空言，或那一位的轻率失检。一位清醒的批评家或许会说：能把赫伯特（Herbert）与劳德（Laud）、多恩（Donne）与赫里克（Herrick）、桑德森（Sanderson）与朱克森（Juxon）、哈蒙德（Hammond）与兰斯洛特·安德鲁斯（Lancelot Andrewes）一锅烩作「我们堕落的教士」的人，不是白痴便是恶棍，多半两样兼是。这判断千真万确，但作为对《利西达斯》的批评，却是愚不可及的笑谈。至于这女子，人们不难想象那类俗套恋人的做派：那位眼含讥诮、「敬畏且尊重」一切女性的骑士，凌晨回到家中，提笔写上一篇论「圣英国少女」（St. English Girl）的社论。卢西安却相反，他对娇媚可人的安妮怀着深切的感激，因为她恰恰在最合宜的时刻，亲手把自己的影像从他的路上撤去了。他向自己招认，近来他已有点怕她回来搅扰；一想到两人的关系将沦为那被叫得如此可怕的「私情」或「韵事」，他便不寒而栗。那将少不了种种用得稀烂的庸常心机，幽期密会的秽俗，还有一股托马斯·穆尔（Thomas Moore）与拜伦勋爵和「雪茄」（segars）年代的气息。卢西安害怕这一切；他怕的是，爱本身会毁掉爱。

===Original===
He considered that now, freed from the torment of the body, leaving untasted the green water that makes thirst more burning, he was perfectly initiated in the true knowledge of the splendid and glorious love. There seemed to him a monstrous paradox in the assertion that there could be no true love without a corporal presence of the beloved; even the popular sayings of “Absence makes the heart grow fonder,” and “familiarity breeds contempt,” witnessed to the contrary. He thought, sighing, and with compassion, of the manner in which men are continually led astray by the cheat of the senses. In order that the unborn might still be added to the born, nature had inspired men with the wild delusion that the bodily companionship of the lover and the beloved was desirable above all things, and so, by the false show of pleasure, the human race was chained to vanity, and doomed to an eternal thirst for the nonexistent.

Again and again he gave thanks for his own escape; he had been set free from a life of vice and sin and folly, from all the dangers and illusions that are most dreaded by the wise. He laughed as he remembered what would be the common view of the situation. An ordinary lover would suffer all the sting of sorrow and contempt; there would be grief for a lost mistress, and rage at her faithlessness, and hate in the heart; one foolish passion driving on another, and driving the man to ruin. For what would be commonly called the real woman he now cared nothing; if he had heard that she had died in her farm in Utter Gwent, he would have experienced only a passing sorrow, such as he might feel at the death of anyone he had once known. But he did not think of the young farmer’s wife as the real Annie; he did not think of the frostbitten leaves in winter as the real rose. Indeed, the life of many reminded him of the flowers; perhaps more especially of those flowers which to all appearance are for many years but dull and dusty clumps of green, and suddenly, in one night, burst into the flame of blossom, and fill all the misty lawns with odour: till the morning. It was in that night that the flower lived, not through the long unprofitable years; and, in like manner, many human lives, he thought, were born in the evening and dead before the coming of day. But he had preserved the precious flower in all its glory, not suffering it to wither in the hard light, but keeping it in a secret place, where it could never be destroyed. Truly now, and for the first time, he possessed Annie, as a man possesses the gold which he has dug from the rock and purged of its baseness.

===Chinese===
他认为，如今自己既已从肉体的折磨中解脱，又没有去尝那令渴愈灼的绿水，便已圆满入了门，得着了辉煌荣耀之爱的真知识。有人说，没有所爱者的肉身在场，便没有真爱；在他看来，这论断是个荒怪的悖论；连「别久情愈深」「习近则生慢」这些俗谚，也证明着相反的道理。他叹息着，怀着悲悯，想着世人如何被感官的骗局一再引入歧途。为了让未生者继续添在已生者之后，自然赋予人一种疯狂的错觉，以为爱者与被爱者肉身的厮守贵于万事；于是，凭一场欢乐的假象，人类被锁链缚在虚荣之上，被判永远渴求那不存在之物。

他一次又一次为自己的逃脱称谢；他已获释脱身，离开了罪恶、罪愆与愚妄的生活，离开了智者最为畏惧的一切危险与幻象。想起世人对这处境会作何想，他笑了。一个寻常的恋人会受尽悲伤与轻蔑的螫刺；会为失去的情妇哀恸，为她的负心狂怒，心头怀恨；一种愚妄的情热驱赶着另一种，把人直驱到毁灭。对于世人通常称作真实女人的那一个，他如今已毫不在意；倘若听说她死在遥远格温特（Utter Gwent）腹地的农庄里，他也只会有一阵转瞬即逝的悲伤，如同听闻某个旧识之人的死讯。但他并不把那个年轻农妇当作真正的安妮；正如他并不把冬日里冻坏的枝叶当作真正的玫瑰。的确，许多人的一生使他想起花木；或许尤其像那一种花：多年间看去不过是一丛晦暗蒙尘的绿，却在忽然之间、一夜之内，迸放出花朵的火焰，把雾蒙蒙的草地尽数熏透了芬芳：直到清晨。花活在那一夜里，而不活在漫长的无益岁月里；同样地，他想，许多人的一生也在黄昏降生，不到白昼来临便已死去。而他已把那朵珍异的花保全于全盛的荣光之中，不容它在硬光下凋萎，而是把它藏在一处秘所，永不毁坏。千真万确，此刻，而且是头一回，他占有了安妮，如同人占有一块自岩中掘出、涤尽渣滓的黄金。

===Original===
He was musing over these things when a piece of news, very strange and unexpected, arrived at the rectory. A distant, almost a mythical relative, known from childhood as “Cousin Edward in the Isle of Wight,” had died, and by some strange freak had left Lucian two thousand pounds. It was a pleasure to give his father five hundred pounds, and the rector on his side forgot for a couple of days to lean his head on his hand. From the rest of the capital, which was well invested, Lucian found he would derive something between sixty and seventy pounds a year, and his old desires for literature and a refuge in the murmuring streets returned to him. He longed to be free from the incantations that surrounded him in the country, to work and live in a new atmosphere; and so, with many good wishes from his father, he came to the retreat in the waste places of London.

===Chinese===
他正这样冥想之际，一则离奇而全然出乎意料的消息传到了教区宅邸。一位疏远的、近乎神话般的亲戚——从小便以「怀特岛的爱德华表哥」（Cousin Edward in the Isle of Wight）闻名的那位——死了，又不知出于怎样古怪的异想，给卢西安留下两千镑。把五百镑送给父亲是一桩乐事，而牧师他老人家这一边，竟也有两天忘了把手支在头上。其余的资本投放得宜，卢西安算出每年可得益六十到七十镑之间，他旧日的心愿——文学，与低语街巷间的一处避难所——重新回到心头。他渴望摆脱乡间四面环绕他的咒语，渴望在一种新的气氛里工作、生活；于是，带着父亲的许多祝福，他来到了伦敦荒僻之地间的这处退隐之所。

===Original===
He was in high spirits when he found the square, clean room, horribly furnished, in the by-street that branched from the main road, and advanced in an unlovely sweep to the mud pits and the desolation that was neither town nor country. On every side monotonous grey streets, each house the replica of its neighbor, to the east an unexplored wilderness, north and west and south the brickfields and market-gardens, everywhere the ruins of the country, the tracks where sweet lanes had been, gangrened stumps of trees, the relics of hedges, here and there an oak stripped of its bark, white and haggard and leprous, like a corpse. And the air seemed always grey, and the smoke from the brickfields was grey.

At first he scarcely realized the quarter into which chance had led him. His only thought was of the great adventure of letters in which he proposed to engage, and his first glance round his “bed-sitting-room” showed him that there was no piece of furniture suitable for his purpose. The table, like the rest of the suite, was of bird’s-eye maple; but the maker seemed to have penetrated the druidic secret of the rocking-stone, the thing was in a state of unstable equilibrium perpetually. For some days he wandered through the streets, inspecting the secondhand furniture shops, and at last, in a forlorn byway, found an old Japanese bureau, dishonored and forlorn, standing amongst rusty bedsteads, sorry china, and all the refuse of homes dead and desolate. The bureau pleased him in spite of its grime and grease and dirt. Inlaid mother-of-pearl, the gleam of lacquer dragons in red gold, and hints of curious design shone through the film of neglect and ill-usage, and when the woman of the shop showed him the drawers and well and pigeonholes, he saw that it would be an apt instrument for his studies.

The bureau was carried to his room and replaced the “bird’s-eye” table under the gas-jet. As Lucian arranged what papers he had accumulated: the sketches of hopeless experiments, shreds and tatters of stories begun but never completed, outlines of plots, two or three notebooks scribbled through and through with impressions of the abandoned hills, he felt a thrill of exaltation at the prospect of work to be accomplished, of a new world all open before him.

===Chinese===
找到那间四四方方、干干净净的房间时，他兴致很高，虽然屋里的陈设可怕得很；那是大马路岔出的一条小街，一路不甚雅观地逶迤延展，直通到泥坑，通到那既非城市亦非乡野的荒凉地界。四面八方是单调的灰色街道，每一所房子都是隔壁那所的复制品；东面是一片未知的荒野，北、西、南三面是砖场与供应市场的菜园；到处是乡野的废墟——那些从前是可爱小径的道道痕迹，坏疽般的树桩，残败的树篱断片，间或一棵被剥了皮的橡树，惨白、憔悴、生着癞病，像一具尸体。而空气似乎永远是灰的，砖场里冒出的烟也是灰的。

起初他几乎不曾意识到机缘把他引到了怎样一个地段。他一心想的，只是他即将投身的那场文字的大冒险；朝「卧室兼起居室」里环顾第一眼，他便看出屋里没有一件家具合乎他的用途。那张桌子，同整套家具一样，是雀眼枫木的；但匠人似乎参透了德鲁伊摇石的秘密，那东西永远处在一种不稳定的平衡之中。有几天他在街巷间游荡，察看一间间旧家具铺，终于，在一条凄凉的僻路上，发现了一张旧式的日本写字柜，蒙尘落魄、遭尽冷落，立在生了锈的床架、粗陋的瓷器和一切死寂荒废之家的废物中间。尽管积垢、油腻、肮脏，这写字柜还是让他欢喜。嵌螺钿的纹样，红金色的漆龙隐隐生辉，还有种种古怪图案的暗示，都透过荒疏与糟践的浮膜莹莹放光；当铺子里的女人拉开抽屉、亮出暗屉和鸽笼格给他看时，他明白，这将是他学问生涯的一件利器。

写字柜搬进了他的房间，在煤气灯嘴下取代了那张「雀眼枫」桌子。卢西安把积攒下来的纸页一一归置——无望试验的稿样，开了头又从未完成的故事的残篇断简，情节的提纲，两三本写满了被抛诸脑后的群山印象的笔记本——他感到一阵激昂的震颤，为了那有待完成的工作，为了那个在他面前豁然洞开的新世界。

===Original===
He set out on the adventure with a fury of enthusiasm; his last thought at night when all the maze of streets was empty and silent was of the problem, and his dreams ran on phrases, and when he awoke in the morning he was eager to get back to his desk. He immersed himself in a minute, almost a microscopic analysis of fine literature. It was no longer enough, as in the old days, to feel the charm and incantation of a line or a word; he wished to penetrate the secret, to understand something of the wonderful suggestion, all apart from the sense, that seemed to him the differentia of literature, as distinguished from the long follies of “character-drawing,” “psychological analysis,” and all the stuff that went to make the three-volume novel of commerce.

He found himself curiously strengthened by the change from the hills to the streets. There could be no doubt, he thought, that living a lonely life, interested only in himself and his own thoughts, he had become in a measure inhuman. The form of external things, black depths in woods, pools in lonely places, those still valleys curtained by hills on every side, sounding always with the ripple of their brooks, had become to him an influence like that of a drug, giving a certain peculiar colour and outline to his thoughts. And from early boyhood there had been another strange flavor in his life, the dream of the old Roman world, those curious impressions that he had gathered from the white walls of Caermaen, and from the looming bastions of the fort. It was in reality the subconscious fancies of many years that had rebuilt the golden city, and had shown him the vine-trellis and the marbles and the sunlight in the garden of Avallaunius. And the rapture of love had made it all so vivid and warm with life, that even now, when he let his pen drop, the rich noise of the tavern and the chant of the theatre sounded above the murmur of the streets. Looking back, it was as much a part of his life as his schooldays, and the tessellated pavements were as real as the square of faded carpet beneath his feet.

===Chinese===
他怀着一团狂热的烈焰踏上了这场冒险；夜里，当迷宫般的街巷全都空寂无声，他睡前最后的念头是那个难题，他的梦沿着一个个短语流驶；早晨醒来，他便急急地回到书桌前。他沉浸到一种精细的、几乎是显微式的精美文学分析之中。像旧日那样，只感受一行字、一个词的魅力与咒语，已经不再够用；他要参透那秘密，要多少领会一点那奇妙的暗示——它全然在意义之外，在他看来正是文学的种差，有别于「性格刻画」「心理分析」的冗长愚行，有别于一切用来制造三卷本商业小说的货色。

他发现自己由于从群山到街巷的变迁，竟奇异地强壮了起来。毫无疑问，他想，过着孤独的生活，只关心自己与自己的思想，他在某种程度上已变得非人。外物的形貌——林中的黑色深处，僻静地方的水潭，那些四面被山峦掩遮如帷幔、永远回响着溪流涟漪的死寂山谷——对他已成了一种类似药物的影响，给他的思想染上某种特异的色彩与轮廓。而自童年初年起，他的生命里还有另一种奇异的滋味：古罗马世界的梦，那些他从凯尔梅恩白色城墙、从堡垒森然逼近的棱堡中拾取的古怪印象。实际上，正是多年潜意识的种种遐想，重建了那座黄金之城，向他展示了葡萄棚架、大理石与阿瓦隆尼乌斯花园里的阳光。而爱的狂喜又使这一切如此生动、如此温热地充盈着生命，以至于即使在如今，当他搁下笔，酒肆的喧闹与剧场的歌吟，仍会浮响在街市的低语之上。回望过去，那同他的学生时代一样，是他生命的一部分；那些镶嵌铺石的路，同他脚下那方褪色的地毯一样真实。

===Original===
But he felt that he had escaped. He could now survey those splendid and lovely visions from without, as if he read of opium dreams, and he no longer dreaded a weird suggestion that had once beset him, that his very soul was being moulded into the hills, and passing into the black mirror of still waterpools. He had taken refuge in the streets, in the harbor of a modern suburb, from the vague, dreaded magic that had charmed his life. Whenever he felt inclined to listen to the old wood-whisper or to the singing of the fauns he bent more earnestly to his work, turning a deaf ear to the incantations.

In the curious labour of the bureau he found refreshment that was continually renewed. He experienced again, and with a far more violent impulse, the enthusiasm that had attended the writing of his book a year or two before, and so, perhaps, passed from one drug to another. It was, indeed, with something of rapture that he imagined the great procession of years all to be devoted to the intimate analysis of words, to the construction of the sentence, as if it were a piece of jewelry or mosaic.

===Chinese===
但他觉得自己已经逃脱了。如今他能从外面俯瞰那些辉煌美妙的幻象，仿佛读着别人笔下的鸦片之梦；他不再畏惧曾经纠缠过他的那个怪异暗示——他自己的灵魂正被塑进群山之中，正没入静止水潭的黑色镜面。他已躲进街巷，躲进现代市郊的港湾，避开了那曾迷住他一生的、模糊而可畏的魔法。每当觉得想谛听古老的林语或法翁（fauns）的歌唱，他便更恳切地俯身于功课，对那些咒语充耳不闻。

在写字柜前这桩奇异的劳作中，他找到了不断更新的滋养。一两年前伴着他写书的那种热忱，他又一次体验到了，而且带着激烈得多的冲动——如此说来，他或许不过是换了一种药物。的确，怀着几分狂喜，他想象着岁月的浩荡行列将全部献于对词语的亲密分析，献于句子的构筑，仿佛那是一件首饰，或一方镶嵌画。

===Original===
Sometimes, in the pauses of the work, he would pace up and down his cell, looking out of the window now and again and gazing for an instant into the melancholy street. As the year advanced the days grew more and more misty, and he found himself the inhabitant of a little island wreathed about with the waves of a white and solemn sea. In the afternoon the fog would grow denser, shutting out not only sight but sound; the shriek of the garden gates, the jangling of the tram-bell echoed as if from a far way. Then there were days of heavy incessant rain; he could see a grey drifting sky and the drops plashing in the street, and the houses all dripping and saddened with wet.

===Chinese===
有时候，在功课的间歇，他会在他这间小室里踱来踱去，不时望一望窗外，对着那条忧郁的街市凝视片刻。随着年岁加深，日子一天天雾气愈重，他发现自己成了一座小岛的岛民，四面环绕着一片白色而庄严的海的波涛。午后，雾会愈来愈浓，不仅遮断望眼，也隔断声息；花园铁门的尖叫、电车的叮当，都仿佛从极远处传来回响。随后又有连绵苦雨的日子；他望得见灰蒙蒙流驶的天空，雨点啪嗒啪嗒落在街面上，房屋个个淋着水，湿得愁眉苦脸。

===Original===
He cured himself of one great aversion. He was no longer nauseated at the sight of a story begun and left unfinished. Formerly, even when an idea rose in his mind bright and wonderful, he had always approached the paper with a feeling of sickness and dislike, remembering all the hopeless beginnings he had made. But now he understood that to begin a romance was almost a separate and special art, a thing apart from the story, to be practiced with sedulous care. Whenever an opening scene occurred to him he noted it roughly in a book, and he devoted many long winter evenings to the elaboration of these beginnings. Sometimes the first impression would yield only a paragraph or a sentence, and once or twice but a splendid and sonorous word, which seemed to Lucian all dim and rich with unsurmised adventure. But often he was able to write three or four vivid pages, studying above all things the hint and significance of the words and actions, striving to work into the lines the atmosphere of expectation and promise, and the murmur of wonderful events to come.

===Chinese===
他治好了自己的一大痼疾。再也不会一见一篇开了头又半途而废的故事便恶心欲呕了。从前，哪怕一个念头在他心里升起来，明亮而奇妙，他也总是带着一阵厌恶与不适走近稿纸，记起自己作过的一切无望的开头。可如今他明白了：开一篇传奇，几乎是一门独立而专门的技艺，是故事之外的一件东西，须得孜孜不倦地用心修习。每当一个开场的景象在他脑中浮现，他就在一个本子里粗粗记下，又把许多漫长的冬夜用来雕琢这些开头。有时第一印象只能产出一个段落或一个句子，有一两次只有一个响亮堂皇的词——而在卢西安听来，它幽昧而丰饶，满载着未可逆料的奇遇。但常常他也能写出三四页鲜活的文章，首要钻研的是字句与行动的暗示与深意，竭力把期待与许诺的气氛、把未来奇事将至的低语，织进字里行间。

===Original===
In this one department of his task the labour seemed almost endless. He would finish a few pages and then rewrite them, using the same incident and nearly the same words, but altering that indefinite something which is scarcely so much style as manner, or atmosphere. He was astonished at the enormous change that was thus effected, and often, though he himself had done the work, he could scarcely describe in words how it was done. But it was clear that in this art of manner, or suggestion, lay all the chief secrets of literature, that by it all the great miracles were performed. Clearly it was not style, for style in itself was untranslatable, but it was that high theurgic magic that made the English *Don Quixote*, roughly traduced by some Jervas, perhaps the best of all English books. And it was the same element that made the journey of Roderick Random to London, so ostensibly a narrative of coarse jokes and common experiences and burlesque manners, told in no very choice diction, essentially a wonderful vision of the eighteenth century, carrying to one’s very nostrils the aroma of the Great North Road, iron-bound under black frost, darkened beneath shuddering woods, haunted by highwaymen, with an adventure waiting beyond every turn, and great old echoing inns in the midst of lonely winter lands.

===Chinese===
在他这项任务的这一个部门里，劳作似乎几乎永无止境。他会写完几页，然后重写一遍，用的是同一个事件、几乎同样的字句，只改动那难以界定的某样东西——那与其说是风格（style），不如说是风致（manner），或者说是气氛。他惊讶于如此施为竟能造成天翻地覆的变化，而且常常，虽是亲手做下的功夫，他也几乎无法用言语说清那是怎样做成的。但有一点很清楚：文学的诸般主要秘密，全在这门风致（或曰暗示）的技艺里，一切伟大的奇迹都由它成就。它分明不是风格，因为风格本身是不可移译的；它是那种高妙的神通魔法，使那部被某个贾维斯（Jervas）粗粗迻译的英文《堂吉诃德》（Don Quixote），或许成了全部英国书中最好的一部。也正是同一种元素，使罗德里克·兰登（Roderick Random）的伦敦之行——表面上不过是粗俗笑话、寻常阅历与滑稽做派的记叙，措辞也并不怎么考究——本质上成了十八世纪的一场奇妙幻象，把大北路（the Great North Road）的气息一直送到人的鼻端：那条路在黑色严霜下冻得铁硬，在战栗的林莽下暗下去，路上有剪径的大盗出没，每一个转弯之外都有奇遇在等待，还有一座座巨大而古老、回声隆隆的客栈，矗立在孤寂的冬日旷野中央。

===Original===
It was this magic that Lucian sought for his opening chapters; he tried to find that quality that gives to words something beyond their sound and beyond their meaning, that in the first lines of a book should whisper things unintelligible but all significant. Often he worked for many hours without success, and the grim wet dawn once found him still searching for hieroglyphic sentences, for words mystical, symbolic. On the shelves, in the upper part of his bureau, he had placed the books which, however various as to matter, seemed to have a part in this curious quality of suggestion, and in that sphere which might almost be called supernatural. To these books he often had recourse, when further effort appeared altogether hopeless, and certain pages in Coleridge and Edgar Allan Poe had the power of holding him in a trance of delight, subject to emotions and impressions which he knew to transcend altogether the realm of the formal understanding. Such lines as:

===Chinese===
卢西安为自己的开篇各章寻求的，正是这种魔法；他试图找到那种品质，它赋予词语某种超出其声音、超出其意义的东西，让一部书的开头几行便低语着不可解而全然意味深长的事物。他常常一连许多小时劳而无获；曾有一个阴冷潮湿的黎明，发现他还在搜寻象形文字般的句子，搜寻神秘而象征的词语。在写字柜上层的架子上，他安放了那些题材各异、却似乎都在这奇异的暗示品质里、在那几乎可称为超自然的领域里占有一席的书。每当继续努力显得全然无望，他便常常求助于这些书；柯勒律治（Coleridge）与埃德加·爱伦·坡（Edgar Allan Poe）的某些篇页，有一种力量，能把他持留在欢愉的迷离出神之中，使他领受种种情绪与印象——他知道，这些全然超出了形式知性的疆界。这样的诗行，如：

===Original===
> Bottomless vales and boundless floods,
 

 And chasms, and caves, and Titan woods,
 

 With forms that no man can discover
 

 For the dews that drip all over;

===Chinese===
> 无底的幽谷，无边的洪流，

> 还有峡谷、洞穴与泰坦的林莽，

> 其中的形貌无人能窥见，

> 只因露水淋漓，滴落周身；

===Original===
had for Lucian more than the potency of a drug, lulling him into a splendid waking-sleep, every word being a supreme incantation. And it was not only his mind that was charmed by such passages, for he felt at the same time a strange and delicious bodily languor that held him motionless, without the desire or power to stir from his seat. And there were certain phrases in “Kubla Khan” that had such a magic that he would sometimes wake up, as it were, to the consciousness that he had been lying on the bed or sitting in the chair by the bureau, repeating a single line over and over again for two or three hours. Yet he knew perfectly well that he had not been really asleep; a little effort recalled a constant impression of the wallpaper, with its pink flowers on a buff ground, and of the muslin-curtained window, letting in the grey winter light. He had been some seven months in London when this odd experience first occurred to him. The day opened dreary and cold and clear, with a gusty and restless wind whirling round the corner of the street, and lifting the dead leaves and scraps of paper that littered the roadway into eddying mounting circles, as if a storm of black rain were to come. Lucian had sat late the night before, and rose in the morning feeling weary and listless and heavy-headed. While he dressed, his legs dragged him as with weights, and he staggered and nearly fell in bending down to the mat outside for his tea-tray. He lit the spirit lamp on the hearth with shaking, unsteady hands, and could scarcely pour out the tea when it was ready. A delicate cup of tea was one of his few luxuries; he was fond of the strange flavor of the green leaf, and this morning he drank the straw-coloured liquid eagerly, hoping it would disperse the cloud of languor. He tried his best to coerce himself into the sense of vigor and enjoyment with which he usually began the day, walking briskly up and down and arranging his papers in order. But he could not free himself from depression; even as he opened the dear bureau a wave of melancholy came upon him, and he began to ask himself whether he were not pursuing a vain dream, searching for treasures that had no existence. He drew out his cousin’s letter and read it again, sadly enough. After all there was a good deal of truth in what she said; he had “overrated” his powers, he had no friends, no real education. He began to count up the months since he had come to London; he had received his two thousand pounds in March, and in May he had said goodbye to the woods and to the dear and friendly paths. May, June, July, August, September, October, November, and half of December had gone by; and what had he to show? Nothing but the experiment, the attempt, futile scribblings which had no end nor shining purpose. There was nothing in his desk that he could produce as evidence of his capacity, no fragment even of accomplishment. It was a thought of intense bitterness, but it seemed as if the barbarians were in the right﻿—a place in a house of business would have been more suitable. He leaned his head on his desk overwhelmed with the severity of his own judgment. He tried to comfort himself again by the thought of all the hours of happy enthusiasm he had spent amongst his papers, working for a great idea with infinite patience. He recalled to mind something that he had always tried to keep in the background of his hopes, the foundation-stone of his life, which he had hidden out of sight. Deep in his heart was the hope that he might one day write a valiant book; he scarcely dared to entertain the aspiration, he felt his incapacity too deeply, but yet this longing was the foundation of all his painful and patient effort. This he had proposed in secret to himself, that if he laboured without ceasing, without tiring, he might produce something which would at all events be art, which would stand wholly apart from the objects shaped like books, printed with printers’ ink, and called by the name of books that he had read. Giotto, he knew, was a painter, and the man who imitated walnut-wood on the deal doors opposite was a painter, and he had wished to be a very humble pupil in the class of the former. It was better, he thought, to fail in attempting exquisite things than to succeed in the department of the utterly contemptible; he had vowed he would be the dunce of Cervantes’s school rather than top-boy in the academy of *A Bad Un to Beat* and *Millicent’s Marriage*. And with this purpose he had devoted himself to laborious and joyous years, so that however mean his capacity, the pains should not be wanting. He tried now to rouse himself from a growing misery by the recollection of this high aim, but it all seemed hopeless vanity. He looked out into the grey street, and it stood a symbol of his life, chill and dreary and grey and vexed with a horrible wind. There were the dull inhabitants of the quarter going about their common business; a man was crying “mackerel” in a doleful voice, slowly passing up the street, and staring into the white-curtained “parlours,” searching for the face of a purchaser behind the India-rubber plants, stuffed birds, and piles of gaudy gilt books that adorned the windows. One of the blistered doors over the way banged, and a woman came scurrying out on some errand, and the garden gate shrieked two melancholy notes as she opened it and let it swing back after her. The little patches called gardens were mostly untilled, uncared for, squares of slimy moss, dotted with clumps of coarse ugly grass, but here and there were the blackened and rotting remains of sunflowers and marigolds. And beyond, he knew, stretched the labyrinth of streets more or less squalid, but all grey and dull, and behind were the mud pits and the steaming heaps of yellowish bricks, and to the north was a great wide cold waste, treeless, desolate, swept by bitter wind.
