It was all like his own life, he said again to himself, a maze of unprofitable dreariness and desolation, and his mind grew as black and hopeless as the winter sky. The morning went thus dismally till twelve o’clock, and he put on his hat and greatcoat. He always went out for an hour every day between twelve and one; the exercise was a necessity, and the landlady made his bed in the interval. The wind blew the smoke from the chimneys into his face as he shut the door, and with the acrid smoke came the prevailing odour of the street, a blend of cabbage-water and burnt bones and the faint sickly vapor from the brickfields. Lucian walked mechanically for the hour, going eastward, along the main road. The wind pierced him, and the dust was blinding, and the dreariness of the street increased his misery. The row of common shops, full of common things, the blatant public-houses, the Independent chapel, a horrible stucco parody of a Greek temple with a façade of hideous columns that was a nightmare, villas like smug Pharisees, shops again, a church in cheap Gothic, an old garden blasted and riven by the builder, these were the pictures of the way. When he got home again he flung himself on the bed, and lay there stupidly till sheer hunger roused him. He ate a hunch of bread and drank some water, and began to pace up and down the room, wondering whether there were no escape from despair. Writing seemed quite impossible, and hardly knowing what he did he opened his bureau and took out a book from the shelves. As his eyes fell on the page the air grew dark and heavy as night, and the wind wailed suddenly, loudly, terribly.

===Chinese===
对卢西安，这些诗行有逾于药物的效力，把他催眠进一场辉煌的白日之眠，每一个词都是一记至高的咒语。而且被这些段落魅住的还不只是他的心智；他同时还感到一种奇异而美妙的身体的倦怠，把他钉在原地不动，既无离开座位的意愿，也无离开的气力。《忽必烈汗》（Kubla Khan）里有几个短语具有如此的魔法，以致他有时会仿佛猛然醒转，意识到自己一直躺在床上，或坐在写字柜旁的椅子里，把单独一行诗翻来覆去念了两三个钟头。然而他清楚得很，自己并没有真的睡着；稍一凝神，便能忆起壁纸——浅黄底子上粉红色的花——和那扇挂平纹细布窗帘的窗，它们源源不断地向他送来灰色冬日天光的恒常印象，从未间断。这桩奇异的经验头一回降临到他身上时，他来伦敦已约有七个月了。那天破晓阴冷而晴朗，一阵阵狂躁不宁的风在街角打着旋，把路面上狼藉的枯叶与纸屑卷起，卷成一圈圈盘旋上升的涡流，仿佛一场黑雨的风暴将要来临。卢西安前一夜坐到很晚，早晨起来只觉疲惫、恹恹、头重脚轻。穿衣的当儿，双腿像坠着铅块似的拖着他，弯腰去门外垫子上取茶盘时，他一个趔趄，险些跌倒。他用颤抖不稳的手点着壁炉边的酒精灯，茶好了，却几乎倒不出来。一杯好茶是他仅有的几样享受之一；他喜欢绿叶那奇异的香味，这天早晨，他急急地喝下那稻草色的茶汤，指望它驱散倦怠的阴云。他竭力强迫自己打起精神，强扭出平日开始一天时那股劲头与兴味，快步来回踱着，把稿纸一一理顺。可他摆脱不掉消沉；甚至当他打开心爱的写字柜，一阵忧郁的浪潮便涌上心头，他开始问自己：自己追寻的莫不是一场虚妄的梦，寻觅的宝藏莫不是子虚乌有。他抽出表姐的来信，又读了一遍，心里悲凉。她的话毕竟有几分道理；他是「把自己估量高了」，他没有朋友，没有受过真正的教育。他开始细数来伦敦以后的月份；他三月里收到那两千镑，五月里告别了树林，告别了那些亲爱而友善的小径。五月、六月、七月、八月、九月、十月、十一月，外加半个十二月，都过去了；而他拿得出什么呢？什么也没有，只有那试验、那尝试，只有既无归宿亦无光辉目标的徒劳涂写。他的书桌里没有一件东西能拿出来证明他的才具，连一星半点的成果也没有。这是一个万分苦涩的念头，可野蛮人似乎倒是对的——当初若到一家商行里谋个位子，原会更合适些。他把头伏在书桌上，被自己判决的严酷压倒了。他又试图用那些在纸页间度过的、怀着欢欣热忱的时辰来宽慰自己——为了一个伟大的观念，他以无限的耐心辛勤劳作。他记起一件他始终竭力藏在希望深处的东西，他生命的基石，那被他藏得不见踪影的东西。在他心底，怀着这样的希望：有朝一日写出一部英勇的书；这抱负他几乎不敢去怀抱，他对自己的无力感得太深，可这渴望毕竟是一切苦痛而坚忍的努力的地基。他曾在心里秘密立约：若能不辍不倦地劳作，或可造出一件至少是艺术的东西，它将全然有别于那些做成书的形状、用印刷油墨印出来、顶着书名的东西——他读过、也被称作书的那些东西。他知道，乔托（Giotto）是画家，对面那扇杉木门上把胡桃木纹摹得惟妙惟肖的那个人也是画家，而他甘愿在前者的门下做一名至为卑微的学生。去尝试精美之事而失败，他想，总强过在十足可鄙的行当里成功；他立过誓：宁做塞万提斯（Cervantes）学堂里的笨伯，不做*《难缠的对手》*（A Bad Un to Beat）与*《米莉森特的婚姻》*（Millicent's Marriage）学院里的头名高材。怀着这个宗旨，他献身于劳苦而欢愉的岁月，如此，无论才具何等寒俭，苦功总不会欠缺。此刻他试图用这崇高目标的记忆，把自己从愈长愈大的苦楚中振拔出来，可这一切似乎都是无望的虚荣。他望向外面的灰色街道，它立在那里，成了他生命的象征，阴冷、凄凉、灰暗，被一阵可怕的风搅扰着。那一带迟钝的居民们在忙着各自的俗务；一个男人用哀伤的嗓音吆喝着「鲭鱼」，缓缓沿街走过，眼睛盯着那些垂白窗帘的「客厅」，在窗里印度橡胶树、鸟类标本和一摞摞俗艳烫金书的装点之间，搜寻一张买主的脸。对面一扇起泡掉漆的门砰地一响，一个女人小跑着出门办事去了，花园铁门随着她的推拉开阖，尖叫出两个忧郁的音符。那些唤作花园的小块地多一半无人耕种、无人照管，尽是一方方滑腻的苔藓，点缀着一丛丛粗陋难看的草，只是间或还有几株向日葵和金盏花发黑腐烂的残骸。再往外，他知道，蜿蜒伸展着迷宫般或多或少的邋遢街道，但一律灰暗沉闷；身后是泥坑和一堆堆冒着蒸汽的黄砖；北面是一片广阔寒冷的荒原，不见树木，满目荒凉，横扫着砭骨的寒风。这一切都像他自己的生命，他再次对自己说，一座无利可图的凄凉与荒芜的迷宫，而他的心境，也变得同冬日的天空一样黑、一样无望。那个上午就这样凄凄惨惨挨到了十二点，他戴上帽子，穿上大衣。每天十二点到一点之间他总出去走一个钟头；这运动是必需的，房东太太也好趁这个空当给他铺床。他关上门时，风正把烟囱里的煤烟吹到他脸上；随着呛人的煤烟一起来的，还有这条街经久不散的气味，一股白菜汤、煳骨头和砖场飘来的淡淡病腻蒸气的混合物。卢西安机械地走满一个钟头，一路往东，沿着大马路。风刺透了他，尘土迷了他的眼，街市的凄凉更添他的愁苦。一排售卖俗货的俗铺子，喧喧嚷嚷的小酒馆，独立派礼拜堂——一座用灰泥拙劣仿造希腊神庙的可怖建筑，正面一排丑恶的柱子，简直就是一场噩梦，一幢幢沾沾自喜、道貌岸然如法利赛人的别墅，又是一排铺子，一座廉价哥特式的教堂，一座被营造商摧残得枝残干裂的老园子——这些便是这一路的景致。等他再回到家，他一头扑倒在床上，呆呆地躺着，直到纯粹的饥饿把他唤起。他啃了一大块面包，喝了些水，开始在屋里踱来踱去，想着绝望可有出路。写作是全然不可能的了，他几乎不知道自己在做什么，打开写字柜，从架子上取下了一本书。他的目光落到书页上时，空气骤然变得沉黑如夜，风忽然哀号起来，响亮地、可怕地哀号起来。

===Original===
“By woman wailing for her Demon lover.” The words were on his lips when he raised his eyes again. A broad band of pale clear light was shining into the room, and when he looked out of the window he saw the road all brightened by glittering pools of water, and as the last drops of the rainstorm starred these mirrors the sun sank into the wrack. Lucian gazed about him, perplexed, till his eyes fell on the clock above his empty hearth. He had been sitting, motionless, for nearly two hours without any sense of the passage of time, and without ceasing he had murmured those words as he dreamed an endless wonderful story. He experienced somewhat the sensations of Coleridge himself; strange, amazing, ineffable things seemed to have been presented to him, not in the form of the idea, but actually and materially, but he was less fortunate than Coleridge in that he could not, even vaguely, image to himself what he had seen. Yet when he searched his mind he knew that the consciousness of the room in which he sat had never left him; he had seen the thick darkness gather, and had heard the whirl of rain hissing through the air. Windows had been shut down with a crash, he had noted the pattering footsteps of people running to shelter, the landlady’s voice crying to someone to look at the rain coming in under the door. It was like peering into some old bituminous picture, one could see at last that the mere blackness resolved itself into the likeness of trees and rocks and travelers. And against this background of his room, and the storm, and the noises of the street, his vision stood out illuminated, he felt he had descended to the very depths, into the caverns that are hollowed beneath the soul. He tried vainly to record the history of his impressions; the symbols remained in his memory, but the meaning was all conjecture.

===Chinese===
「有一个女人在哀哭，为她的魔鬼情人。」他再抬起眼时，这几个词正挂在他的唇边。一道宽阔的苍白清光正照进屋里；他望出窗外，看见街道被闪闪发亮的水潭映得一片明亮，暴雨最后的雨点在那一面面镜子上点出繁星，太阳便沉进了乱云。卢西安茫然地环顾四周，直到目光落在空荡荡的壁炉上方的时钟上。他一动不动坐了将近两个钟头，全然不觉得时光的流逝；他一直不停低念着那几个词，一边梦想着一个无尽而奇妙的故事。他体味到的，多少正是柯勒律治本人的那种感觉；奇异、惊人、无可名状的事物，似乎曾呈现在他面前，不是以观念的形式，而是实实在在地、物质地呈现；只是他不如柯勒律治幸运——他连自己曾看见过什么，都无法，哪怕依稀地——在心中成像。然而当他搜索自己的心，他知道，对他端坐其中的这间屋子的意识，从未离开过他；他看见浓黑聚拢，听见骤雨的旋流嘶嘶地划过空气。窗子砰地放了下来，他注意到人们奔向遮蔽处的嗒嗒脚步声，听见房东太太的嗓音，嚷着叫人看雨水从门底下漫进来。这就像凝望一幅古老的沥青色调的画：到头来你能看出，那片纯粹的黑，原来化作了树木、岩石与行旅的形影。而以他的房间、这场风暴、街上的种种声息为背景，他的幻象凸显出来，通体发光；他感到自己已降到了最深的深处，降到了在灵魂之下掏空的那些洞窟里。他徒劳地试图记录下他种种印象的历史；那些象征物还留存记忆之中，其意义却全出于悬测。

===Original===
The next morning, when he awoke, he could scarcely understand or realize the bitter depression of the preceding day. He found it had all vanished away and had been succeeded by an intense exaltation. Afterwards, when at rare intervals he experienced the same strange possession of the consciousness, he found this to be the invariable result, the hour of vision was always succeeded by a feeling of delight, by sensations of brightened and intensified powers. On that bright December day after the storm he rose joyously, and set about the labour of the bureau with the assurance of success, almost with the hope of formidable difficulties to be overcome. He had long busied himself with those curious researches which Poe had indicated in the *Philosophy of Composition*, and many hours had been spent in analyzing the singular effects which may be produced by the sound and resonance of words. But he had been struck by the thought that in the finest literature there were more subtle tones than the loud and insistent music of “never more,” and he endeavored to find the secret of those pages and sentences which spoke, less directly, and less obviously, to the soul rather than to the ear, being filled with a certain grave melody and the sensation of singing voices. It was admirable, no doubt, to write phrases that showed at a glance their designed rhythm, and rang with sonorous words, but he dreamed of a prose in which the music should be less explicit, of names rather than notes. He was astonished that morning at his own fortune and facility; he succeeded in covering a page of ruled paper wholly to his satisfaction, and the sentences, when he read them out, appeared to suggest a weird elusive chanting, exquisite but almost imperceptible, like the echo of the plainsong reverberated from the vault of a monastic church.

===Chinese===
第二天早晨醒来，他几乎理解不了、也体会不出前一天那种苦涩的消沉了。他发现它已消散净尽，接替它的是一阵强烈的昂奋。后来，每逢间隔许久，他再度经历这种意识被「附身」的怪异经验时，他发现结果总是如此：幻象的时刻过后，总跟着一阵欢愉之感，种种官能豁亮而倍增的感觉。风暴过后那个明亮的十二月之日，他欢欣地起身，以必成之势着手写字柜前的功课，几乎是怀着一种但望有强敌当前、得以克之的希望。许久以来，他一直忙着坡在《创作哲学》（Philosophy of Composition）里提示过的那些古怪研究，花过许多个钟头，分析词语的声音与共鸣所能产生的奇异效果。但他曾被一个念头打动：在最精美的文学里，还有比「永不复还」那响亮而执拗的音乐更微妙的音调；于是他努力去寻找那些篇页与句子的秘密——它们诉说的对象不是耳朵，而是灵魂，不那么直接，也不那么明显，盈满着某种庄重的旋律与歌唱着的声音的感觉。无疑，写出那种一望便见出匠心节奏、铿锵着响亮词语的句子，是令人赞叹的；可他梦想的，是一种音乐不那么外露的散文——其中回响的是名，而不是音符。那天早晨，他为自己的运气与娴熟感到惊异；他成功地写满了一页横格纸，完全称他的心意；那些句子读出声来，仿佛隐隐透出一种奇异而难以捉摸的吟诵，精美之极，却又几乎无从察觉，像素歌从修道院教堂的穹顶上回荡下来的回声。

===Original===
He thought that such happy mornings well repaid him for the anguish of depression which he sometimes had to suffer, and for the strange experience of “possession” recurring at rare intervals, and usually after many weeks of severe diet. His income, he found, amounted to sixty-five pounds a year, and he lived for weeks at a time on fifteen shillings a week. During these austere periods his only food was bread, at the rate of a loaf a day; but he drank huge draughts of green tea, and smoked a black tobacco, which seemed to him a more potent mother of thought than any drug from the scented East. “I hope you go to some nice place for dinner,” wrote his cousin; “there used to be some excellent eating-houses in London where one could get a good cut from the joint, *with plenty of gravy*, and a boiled potato, for a shilling. Aunt Mary writes that you should try Mr. Jones’s in Water Street, Islington, whose father came from near Caermaen, and was always most comfortable in her day. I daresay the walk there would do you good. It is such a pity you smoke that horrid tobacco. I had a letter from Mrs. Dolly (Jane Diggs, who married your cousin John Dolly) the other day, and she said they would have been delighted to take you for only twenty-five shillings a week for the sake of the family if you had not been a smoker. She told me to ask you if you had ever seen a horse or a dog smoking tobacco. They are such nice, comfortable people, and the children would have been company for you. Johnnie, who used to be such a dear little fellow, has just gone into an office in the City, and seems to have excellent prospects. How I wish, my dear Lucian, that you could do something in the same way. Don’t forget Mr. Jones’s in Water Street, and you might mention your name to him.”

===Chinese===
他想，这样一些幸福的早晨，很足以补偿他有时不得不忍受的消沉的苦痛，补偿那种隔许久才复发一次的「附身」怪经验——它通常在许多个星期严苛节食之后降临。他算出收入每年六十五镑，而他一连数周每周只靠十五先令过活。在这些清苦的时期，他唯一的食物是面包，每天一只的定量；但绿茶他大杯大杯地喝，还抽一种黑烟草——在他看来，比起芬芳东方运来的任何药物，它都是思想更强有力的一位母亲。「我希望你晚饭总到些体面的地方去吃，」表姐写道；「伦敦从前有几家顶好的小饭馆，一先令就能吃上一大片从整块烤肉上切下的好肉，*肉汁旺旺的*，外加一只煮土豆。玛丽姨妈（Aunt Mary）来信说，你该去试试伊斯灵顿水街的琼斯馆子（Mr. Jones's），店主的父亲就是凯尔梅恩附近人，在她那个年头，那店总是再舒服不过。我想走去那一趟对你也有好处。你抽那种可怕的烟，真是可惜。前几天我收到多利太太（Mrs. Dolly，即简·迪格斯，嫁给了你表哥约翰·多利）的信，她说若不是你抽烟，看在一家人的份上，他们本很乐意只收你每周二十五先令把你收留下。她让我问你：你可曾见过马或者狗抽烟？他们是那么和善、安逸的人家，孩子们也本可以给你作伴。约翰尼——从前是多可爱的一个小家伙——刚进了伦敦城（the City）一家商号做事，前程看起来好得很。我多么盼望，亲爱的卢西安，你也能照这条路做点出来。别忘了伊斯灵顿水街的琼斯馆子，你也可以向他提提你的名字。」

===Original===
Lucian never troubled Mr. Jones; but these letters of his cousin’s always refreshed him by the force of contrast. He tried to imagine himself a part of the Dolly family, going dutifully every morning to the City on the bus, and returning in the evening for high tea. He could conceive the fine odour of hot roast beef hanging about the decorous house on Sunday afternoons, papa asleep in the dining-room, mamma lying down, and the children quite good and happy with their “Sunday books.” In the evening, after supper, one read the *Quiver* till bedtime. Such pictures as these were to Lucian a comfort and a help, a remedy against despair. Often when he felt overwhelmed by the difficulty of the work he had undertaken, he thought of the alternative career, and was strengthened.

===Chinese===
卢西安从没有去麻烦过琼斯先生；但表姐的这些信，总凭着对照的力量使他精神一爽。他试着想象自己是多利家的一分子，每天早晨规规矩矩乘公共汽车进城，傍晚回来吃浓茶晚饭。他想象得出礼拜天下午那幢规规矩矩的宅子里，热烤牛肉的香气袅袅不散：爸爸在饭厅里睡着了，妈妈躺着歇息，孩子们乖乖的、欢欢喜喜地读着他们的「礼拜日图书」。晚间用过晚饭，一家人读着《羽箭》（the Quiver）直到上床。这样一些图景，对卢西安是一种安慰、一种扶助，是一剂对付绝望的良药。常常，当他被自己所承担工作的艰难压得喘不过气，他便想想那另一条人生道路，便又强健起来。

===Original===
He returned again and again to that desire of a prose which should sound faintly, not so much with an audible music, but with the memory and echo of it. In the night, when the last tram had gone jangling by, and he had looked out and seen the street all wrapped about in heavy folds of the mist, he conducted some of his most delicate experiments. In that white and solitary midnight of the suburban street he experienced the curious sense of being on a tower, remote and apart and high above all the troubles of the earth. The gas lamp, which was nearly opposite, shone in a pale halo of light, and the houses themselves were merely indistinct marks and shadows amidst that palpable whiteness, shutting out the world and its noises. The knowledge of the swarming life that was so still, though it surrounded him, made the silence seem deeper than that of the mountains before the dawn; it was as if he alone stirred and looked out amidst a host sleeping at his feet. The fog came in by the open window in freezing puffs, and as Lucian watched he noticed that it shook and wavered like the sea, tossing up wreaths and drifts across the pale halo of the lamp, and, these vanishing, others succeeded. It was as if the mist passed by from the river to the north, as if it still passed by in the silence.

===Chinese===
他一次又一次回到那个心愿：一种低低作响的散文，与其说是发出可闻的音乐，不如说是回荡着音乐的记忆与回声。夜里，当末班电车叮叮当当地驶过，他望出去，看见街道整个裹在浓重的雾褶里，他便做起他那些最为精微的实验。在市郊街道这白色而孤绝的午夜，他体验到一种奇异的感受，仿佛置身塔顶，遥远，孤绝，高踞于尘世一切烦嚣之上。正对面的那盏煤气灯，在一环苍白的光晕里发亮；房舍本身只是那可触知的白色之中模糊的斑痕与暗影，把世界及其喧嚣一并隔绝在外。明知那熙攘的生命尽管四面包围着他，却如此安静，这知识使寂静显得比黎明前群山的寂静更深；仿佛只有他独自一人，在卧睡于他脚下的众生中间悄然醒着，向外张望。雾从敞开的窗口一阵阵寒冷地涌入；卢西安望着，注意到它像海一样摇荡起伏，把一绺绺、一堆堆的雾卷抛过灯的苍白光晕，前一批方逝，后一批又继。仿佛雾正从北面的河上流过，仿佛它此刻还在寂静中流过。

===Original===
He would shut his window gently, and sit down in his lighted room with all the consciousness of the white advancing shroud upon him. It was then that he found himself in the mood for curious labours, and able to handle with some touch of confidence the more exquisite instruments of the craft. He sought for that magic by which all the glory and glamour of mystic chivalry were made to shine through the burlesque and gross adventures of Don Quixote, by which Hawthorne had lit his infernal Sabbath fires, and fashioned a burning aureole about the village tragedy of the *Scarlet Letter*. In Hawthorne the story and the suggestion, though quite distinct and of different worlds, were rather parallel than opposed to one another; but Cervantes had done a stranger thing. One read of Don Quixote, beaten, dirty, and ridiculous, mistaking windmills for giants, sheep for an army; but the impression was of the enchanted forest, of Avalon, of the San Graal, “far in the spiritual city.” And Rabelais showed him, beneath the letter, the Tourainian sun shining on the hot rock above Chinon, on the maze of narrow, climbing streets, on the high-pitched, gabled roofs, on the grey-blue tourelles, pricking upward from the fantastic labyrinth of walls. He heard the sound of sonorous plainsong from the monastic choir, of gross exuberant gaiety from the rich vineyards; he listened to the eternal mystic mirth of those that halted in the purple shadow of the *sorbier* by the white, steep road. The gracious and ornate châteaux on the Loire and the Vienne rose fair and shining to confront the incredible secrets of vast, dim, far-lifted Gothic naves, that seemed ready to take the great deep, and float away from the mist and dust of earthly streets to anchor in the haven of the clear city that hath foundations. The rank tale of the garderobe, of the farm-kitchen, mingled with the reasoned, endless legend of the schools, with luminous Platonic argument; the old pomp of the Middle Ages put on the robe of a fresh life. There was a smell of wine and of incense, of June meadows and of ancient books, and through it all he hearkened, intent, to the exultation of chiming bells ringing for a new feast in a new land. He would cover pages with the analysis of these marvels, tracking the suggestion concealed beneath the words, and yet glowing like the golden threads in a robe of samite, or like that device of the old binders by which a vivid picture appeared on the shut edges of a book. He tried to imitate this art, to summon even the faint shadow of the great effect, rewriting a page of Hawthorne, experimenting and changing an epithet here and there, noting how sometimes the alteration of a trifling word would plunge a whole scene into darkness, as if one of those bloodred fires had instantly been extinguished. Sometimes, for severe practice, he attempted to construct short tales in the manner of this or that master. He sighed over these desperate attempts, over the clattering pieces of mechanism which would not even simulate life; but he urged himself to an infinite perseverance. Through the white hours he worked on amidst the heap and litter of papers; books and manuscripts overflowed from the bureau to the floor; and if he looked out he saw the mist still pass by, still passing from the river to the north.

===Chinese===
他会轻轻关上窗，在亮着灯的房间里坐下，那白色殓衣层层进逼的意识整个罩在他身上。正是在这种时候，他发觉自己正处在做那些奇妙劳作的心境里，能够带着几分自信，去驾驭这门手艺中更为精微的器具。他寻求的，是那样一种魔法：凭着它，神秘骑士制度的全部荣光与迷魅（glamour），才得以透过《堂吉诃德》滑稽而粗鄙的种种奇遇熠熠放光；凭着它，霍桑（Hawthorne）才点燃了他那些地狱般的安息日之火，为《红字》（The Scarlet Letter）的村庄悲剧绕上一圈燃烧的光环。在霍桑那里，故事与暗示虽然判然有别、分属两个世界，与其说相互对立，不如说彼此平行；而塞万提斯做的事更为奇特。人们读到的是堂吉诃德，挨了打，一身泥，荒唐可笑，把风车认作巨人，把羊群认作大军；可得到的印象，却是中了魔法的森林，是阿瓦隆（Avalon），是圣格拉尔（the San Graal），「远在灵性之城中」。而拉伯雷（Rabelais）在字句之下给他看的是：都兰（Touraine）的太阳照在希农（Chinon）上方滚烫的岩石上，照在狭窄曲折、攀援而上的街巷迷宫上，照在高耸的山墙屋顶上，照在灰蓝色的小塔楼上——塔尖从墙垣奇幻的迷宫里刺向天空。他听见修道院唱诗班庄严宏亮的素歌，听见富饶葡萄园里粗豪奔放的欢愉；他听见那些在雪白陡峭的路旁、在花楸树（*sorbier*）紫色阴影里歇脚的人们那永恒而神秘的欢笑。卢瓦尔河（the Loire）与维埃纳河（the Vienne）畔那些典雅华美的城堡，明媚辉煌地耸起，迎向一座座巨大、幽暗、高不可攀的哥特式中殿那难以置信的秘密——那些中殿仿佛就要拔针出海，离开尘世街衢的雾与尘，驶去停泊在那有根基的澄澈之城的港湾。便所秽谈与农舍厨房的粗话，搀进了经院里那推理绵延的传说，搀进了澄明的柏拉图式论辩；中世纪的古老排场，披上了一场新鲜生命的袍子。有一种酒与熏香的气味，六月草甸与古书的气味；而在这一切之中，他凝神谛听着欢鸣的钟那激昂的庆祝，为一方新土地上的一个新节日而鸣。他会写满一页页对这些奇迹的分析，追踪藏在词语之下、却像白锦袍中缕缕金线一样灼灼发光的暗示，或者像旧时装订匠那门手艺——书页合拢的边上，会显现出一幅鲜亮的图画。他试图模仿这门艺术，试图唤起那伟大效果的哪怕一缕淡影：重写霍桑的一页，试着这里那里换一个形容语，注意有时一个无关紧要的小词一改，整幅场景便骤然沉入黑暗，仿佛那些血红的火堆里，有一堆霎时熄灭了。有时，为着严格的演练，他还试着照这一位或那一位大师的笔法，构筑几篇短篇故事。他对这些孤注一掷的尝试叹息，对着那些连生命的模拟都做不到的哐当作响的机械构件叹息；可他敦促自己拿出无限的坚忍。在这一个个白色的时辰里，他在纸堆的凌乱中间继续劳作；书籍和稿纸从写字柜漫溢到地板上；若是抬眼外望，他看见雾还在流过，还在从北面的河上流过。

===Original===
It was not till the winter was well advanced that he began at all to explore the region in which he lived. Soon after his arrival in the grey street he had taken one or two vague walks, hardly noticing where he went or what he saw; but for all the summer he had shut himself in his room, beholding nothing but the form and colour of words. For his morning walk he almost invariably chose the one direction, going along the Uxbridge Road towards Notting Hill, and returning by the same monotonous thoroughfare. Now, however, when the new year was beginning its dull days, he began to diverge occasionally to right and left, sometimes eating his luncheon in odd corners, in the bulging parlours of eighteenth-century taverns, that still fronted the surging sea of modern streets, or perhaps in brand new “publics” on the broken borders of the brickfields, smelling of the clay from which they had swollen. He found waste by-places behind railway embankments where he could smoke his pipe sheltered from the wind; sometimes there was a wooden fence by an old pear-orchard where he sat and gazed at the wet desolation of the market-gardens, munching a few currant biscuits by way of dinner. As he went farther afield a sense of immensity slowly grew upon him; it was as if, from the little island of his room, that one friendly place, he pushed out into the grey unknown, into a city that for him was uninhabited as the desert.

He came back to his cell after these purposeless wanderings always with a sense of relief, with the thought of taking refuge from grey. As he lit the gas and opened the desk of his bureau and saw the pile of papers awaiting him, it was as if he had passed from the black skies and the stinging wind and the dull maze of the suburb into all the warmth and sunlight and violent colour of the south.

===Chinese===
直到冬天已深，他才开始去探一探自己所居住的这一带地方。刚到这条灰色街道不久，他曾漫无目的地散过一两次步，几乎不曾留意自己去向何方、看见何物；可整整一个夏天，他把自己关在房间里，眼中只有词语的形与色。早晨散步，他几乎一成不变地选定一个方向：沿着阿克斯布里奇路（Uxbridge Road）朝诺丁山（Notting Hill）走去，再顺着同一条单调的大道走回来。然而如今，当新年开始它沉闷的日子，他偶尔也开始向左右岔开去，有时在古怪的角落里用午饭——在十八世纪小酒馆那凸窗的厅堂里，那些酒馆依然面朝现代街市的汹涌怒海；要不然就在砖场破败边缘全新开张的「酒馆」里，满屋一股子黏土味——它们正是靠这黏土发胀起家的。他在铁路路堤后面寻到些荒僻的角落，可以背风抽一斗烟；有时在一座老梨园旁有一道木栅栏，他就坐在那里，望着供应市场的菜园湿漉漉的荒凉，嚼几块醋栗饼干权当晚饭。随着越走越远，一种浩大之感在他心里慢慢生长；仿佛从他房间那座小岛——那唯一友好的地方——他一路驶进了灰色的未知，驶进一座对他而言如沙漠般无人居住的城。

这些漫无目的的游荡之后，他回到自己的小室，总带着一种解脱之感，怀着从灰色中避难归来的心情。当他点起煤气灯，拉开写字柜的桌面，看见等着他的那一摞纸页，便仿佛已从黑色的天空、砭人的寒风和市郊沉闷的迷宫，走进了南方全部的温暖、阳光和浓烈的色彩。

## VI / 第六章

===Original===

It was in this winter after his coming to the grey street that Lucian first experienced the pains of desolation. He had all his life known the delights of solitude, and had acquired that habit of mind which makes a man find rich company on the bare hillside and leads him into the heart of the wood to meditate by the dark waterpools. But now in the blank interval when he was forced to shut up his desk, the sense of loneliness overwhelmed him and filled him with unutterable melancholy. On such days he carried about with him an unceasing gnawing torment in his breast; the anguish of the empty page awaiting him in his bureau, and the knowledge that it was worse than useless to attempt the work. He had fallen into the habit of always using this phrase “the work” to denote the adventure of literature; it had grown in his mind to all the austere and grave significance of “the great work” on the lips of the alchemists; it included every trifling and laborious page and the vague magnificent fancies that sometimes hovered below him. All else had become mere byplay, unimportant, trivial; the work was the end, and the means and the food of his life﻿—it raised him up in the morning to renew the struggle, it was the symbol which charmed him as he lay down at night. All through the hours of toil at the bureau he was enchanted, and when he went out and explored the unknown coasts, the one thought allured him, and was the colored glass between his eyes and the world. Then as he drew nearer home his steps would quicken, and the more weary and grey the walk, the more he rejoiced as he thought of his hermitage and of the curious difficulties that awaited him there. But when, suddenly and without warning, the faculty disappeared, when his mind seemed a hopeless waste from which nothing could arise, then he became subject to a misery so piteous that the barbarians themselves would have been sorry for him. He had known some foretaste of these bitter and inexpressible griefs in the old country days, but then he had immediately taken refuge in the hills, he had rushed to the dark woods as to an anodyne, letting his heart drink in all the wonder and magic of the wild land. Now in these days of January, in the suburban street, there was no such refuge.

He had been working steadily for some weeks, well enough satisfied on the whole with the daily progress, glad to awake in the morning, and to read over what he had written on the night before. The new year opened with faint and heavy weather and a breathless silence in the air, but in a few days the great frost set in. Soon the streets began to suggest the appearance of a beleaguered city, the silence that had preceded the frost deepened, and the mist hung over the earth like a dense white smoke. Night after night the cold increased, and people seemed unwilling to go abroad, till even the main thoroughfares were empty and deserted, as if the inhabitants were lying close in hiding. It was at this dismal time that Lucian found himself reduced to impotence. There was a sudden break in his thought, and when he wrote on valiantly, hoping against hope, he only grew more aghast on the discovery of the imbecilities he had committed to paper. He ground his teeth together and persevered, sick at heart, feeling as if all the world were fallen from under his feet, driving his pen on mechanically, till he was overwhelmed. He saw the stuff he had done without veil or possible concealment, a lamentable and wretched sheaf of verbiage, worse, it seemed, than the efforts of his boyhood. He was no longer tautological, he avoided tautology with the internal art of a leader-writer, filling his wind bags and mincing his words as if he had been a trained journalist on the staff of the *Daily Post*. There seemed all the matter of an insufferable tragedy in these thoughts; that his patient and enduring toil was in vain, that practice went for nothing, and that he had wasted the labour of Milton to accomplish the tenth-rate. Unhappily he could not “give in”; the longing, the fury for the work burnt within him like a burning fire; he lifted up his eyes in despair.

It was then, while he knew that no one could help him, that he languished for help, and then, though he was aware that no comfort was possible, he fervently wished to be comforted. The only friend he had was his father, and he knew that his father would not even understand his distress. For him, always, the printed book was the beginning and end of literature; the agony of the maker, his despair and sickness, were as accursed as the pains of labour. He was ready to read and admire the work of the great Smith, but he did not wish to hear of the period when the great Smith had writhed and twisted like a scotched worm, only hoping to be put out of his misery, to go mad or die, to escape somehow from the bitter pains. And Lucian knew no one else. Now and then he read in the paper the fame of the great *litérateurs*; the Gypsies were entertaining the Prince of Wales, the Jolly Beggars were dining with the Lord Mayor, the Old Mumpers were mingling amicably and gorgeously with the leading members of the Stock Exchange. He was so unfortunate as to know none of these gentlemen, but it hardly seemed likely that they could have done much for him in any case. Indeed, in his heart, he was certain that help and comfort from without were in the nature of things utterly impossible, his ruin and grief were within, and only his own assistance could avail. He tried to reassure himself, to believe that his torments were a proof of his vocation, that the facility of the novelist who stood six years deep in contracts to produce romances was a thing wholly undesirable, but all the while he longed for but a drop of that inexhaustible fluency which he professed to despise.

===Chinese===

来到这条灰色街道之后的这个冬天，卢西安（Lucian）第一次尝到了凄凉的痛楚。他平生只识得孤独的欢愉，并且养成了那样一种心性：教人在光秃的山坡上也能觅得丰盛的伴侣，引人走进树林深处，在幽黑的水潭旁冥想。可如今，在他被迫合上写字台的空白间隙里，孤寂之感把他压倒，使他满怀难以言说的忧郁。这样的日子里，他胸中终日揣着一阵啮咬不休的煎熬：那是在写字台里等候他的空白书页的苦楚，是明知此时去碰「那部书」非但无益、反而更糟的了悟。他早已习惯，始终用「那部书」这个说法来指称文学这场历险；在他心里，这个词已长成炼金术士口中「大业」（the great work）所具有的那全部严峻而庄重的分量；它涵盖每一页琐碎劳苦的文字，也涵盖时而在他下方幽幽盘旋的那些恢弘而朦胧的奇想。其余一切都成了余兴，无关紧要，琐屑无聊；那部书是他的归宿，是他生命的凭借与食粮——清晨是它扶他起身，重整鏖战；夜里又是它化作咒符，安顿他入眠。在写字台前劳作的整段时光里，他如痴如醉；出门踏探未知的海岸时，也只有这一个念想引诱着他，像隔在他眼睛与世界之间的一块彩色玻璃。离家渐近，他的脚步便愈走愈快；归途愈是疲惫、愈是灰暗，他想到自己的隐庐、想到那里等候他的种种奇妙的难处，便愈是欢喜。可是一旦那官能毫无征兆地骤然消隐，一旦他的心智沦为一片无可救药的荒芜、再长不出任何东西，他便堕入一种凄恻入骨的悲苦，连野蛮人自己见了也要为他难过。在早年的乡间岁月里，他已尝过这些苦涩难宣的悲哀的些许滋味；但那时他随即便逃进山中，奔赴幽暗的树林如奔赴一剂止痛药，任他的心饮尽那片蛮荒之地的全部奇瑰与魔法。而如今，在这一月的天色里，在这条郊外的街上，再没有那样的避难所了。

此前几个星期他一直笔耕不辍，对每日的进展总体还算满意，清晨醒来也是欢喜的，乐意把前一晚写下的东西重读一遍。新年是在阴晦低沉的天气里开场的，空气里有一种屏息的寂静；不出几日，大冰冻降临了。很快，这些街道便显出一副被围困城池的模样，先于冰冻而至的寂静愈发深沉，雾霭如浓白的烟，笼罩着大地。一夜冷过一夜，人们似乎都不愿出门，到后来连通衢大道也空空荡荡、渺无人迹，仿佛居民们全都深藏匿伏。正是在这凄黯的时节，卢西安发觉自己陷于无力。他的思路猛然断裂；他硬着头皮写下去，怀着万一的希望，结果只越发惊骇地发现，自己落到纸上的尽是白痴般的文字。他咬紧牙关苦撑，心中作呕，只觉得整个世界都从脚底下塌陷了下去，机械地驱着笔往前赶，直到彻底被击溃。他把自己写出的东西看了个通透，无遮无掩、无从掩饰：一束可悲可悯的芜词冗语，看来比他少年时代的习作还要不如。他已不再颠三倒四地重复；他以社论作家内在的功夫回避重复，鼓起他的风袋，把字句剁成细末，活像《每日邮报》（*Daily Post*）编辑部里一名训练有素的记者。这些念头里简直含着一出不堪忍受的悲剧的全部材料：他坚忍持久的辛劳尽属徒然，磨练毫无所成，他枉费了弥尔顿（Milton）的劳作，只做出十流的货色。不幸的是，他不能「认输」；对那部书的渴念与狂热像一团烈火在他胸中燃烧；他绝望地举目仰望。

正是在那时，他明知无人能助，却因渴求援助而憔悴；也正是在那时，他明知慰藉无望，却热切地盼人安慰。他唯一的朋友是他的父亲，而他知道，父亲连他的苦楚都不会懂得。在父亲那里，印成的书从来就是文学的起点与终点；制作者的煎熬、他的绝望与沉疴，同分娩的阵痛一样被目为咒诅。他乐于阅读并叹赏大作家史密斯（the great Smith）的作品，却不愿听说大史密斯也曾像一条被剁伤未死的蛇般翻滚扭动，一心只求得以解脱，发疯也好，死了也好，好歹逃出那苦痛。而卢西安再不认识旁人。他偶尔在报上读到伟大*文豪*们的声名：「吉普赛人」在款待威尔士亲王，「快活乞丐」在与伦敦市长共餐，「老叫花子」正与证券交易所的头面人物融洽而豪奢地过从。他不曾有幸认识这些先生中的任何一位；不过无论如何，他们看来也帮不上他多少忙。的确，在他心底他认定：外来的援助与安慰，就事物的本性而言是全然不可能的；他的毁灭与悲哀都在内里，唯有自救一途可恃。他试着宽解自己，说服自己相信：他的磨难正是他天职的明证；那位深陷六年长约、专事炮制传奇的小说家的下笔万言，本是全然要不得的东西——可他自始至终渴念的，恰恰是他佯装鄙夷的那份永不枯竭的流畅，哪怕一滴也好。

===Original===

He drove himself out from that dreary contemplation of the white paper and the idle pen. He went into the frozen and deserted streets, hoping that he might pluck the burning coal from his heart, but the fire was not quenched. As he walked furiously along the grim iron roads he fancied that those persons who passed him cheerfully on their way to friends and friendly hearths shrank from him into the mists as they went by. Lucian imagined that the fire of his torment and anguish must in some way glow visibly about him; he moved, perhaps, in a nimbus that proclaimed the blackness and the flames within. He knew, of course, that in misery he had grown delirious, that the well-coated, smooth-hatted personages who loomed out of the fog upon him were in reality shuddering only with cold, but in spite of common sense he still conceived that he saw on their faces an evident horror and disgust, and something of the repugnance that one feels at the sight of a venomous snake, half-killed, trailing its bleeding vileness out of sight. By design Lucian tried to make for remote and desolate places, and yet when he had succeeded in touching on the open country, and knew that the icy shadow hovering through the mist was a field, he longed for some sound and murmur of life, and turned again to roads where pale lamps were glimmering, and the dancing flame of firelight shone across the frozen shrubs. And the sight of these homely fires, the thought of affection and consolation waiting by them, stung him the more sharply perhaps because of the contrast with his own chills and weariness and helpless sickness, and chiefly because he knew that he had long closed an everlasting door between his heart and such felicities. If those within had come out and had called him by his name to enter and be comforted, it would have been quite unavailing, since between them and him there was a great gulf fixed. Perhaps for the first time he realized that he had lost the art of humanity forever. He had thought when he closed his ears to the wood whisper and changed the fauns’ singing for the murmur of the streets, the black pools for the shadows and amber light of London, that he had put off the old life, and had turned his soul to healthy activities, but the truth was that he had merely exchanged one drug for another. He could not be human, and he wondered whether there were some drop of the fairy blood in his body that made him foreign and a stranger in the world.

He did not surrender to desolation without repeated struggles. He strove to allure himself to his desk by the promise of some easy task; he would not attempt invention, but he had memoranda and rough jottings of ideas in his notebooks, and he would merely amplify the suggestions ready to his hand. But it was hopeless, again and again it was hopeless. As he read over his notes, trusting that he would find some hint that might light up the dead fires, and kindle again that pure flame of enthusiasm, he found how desperately his fortune had fallen. He could see no light, no color in the lines he had scribbled with eager trembling fingers; he remembered how splendid all these things had been when he wrote them down, but now they were meaningless, faded into grey. The few words he had dashed on to the paper, enraptured at the thought of the happy hours they promised, had become mere jargon, and when he understood the idea it seemed foolish, dull, unoriginal. He discovered something at last that appeared to have a grain of promise, and determined to do his best to put it into shape, but the first paragraph appalled him; it might have been written by an unintelligent schoolboy. He tore the paper in pieces, and shut and locked his desk, heavy despair sinking like lead into his heart. For the rest of that day he lay motionless on the bed, smoking pipe after pipe in the hope of stupefying himself with tobacco fumes. The air in the room became blue and thick with smoke; it was bitterly cold, and he wrapped himself up in his greatcoat and drew the counterpane over him. The night came on and the window darkened, and at last he fell asleep.

He renewed the effort at intervals, only to plunge deeper into misery. He felt the approaches of madness, and knew that his only hope was to walk till he was physically exhausted, so that he might come home almost fainting with fatigue, but ready to fall asleep the moment he got into bed. He passed the mornings in a kind of torpor, endeavoring to avoid thought, to occupy his mind with the pattern of the paper, with the advertisements at the end of a book, with the curious greyness of the light that glimmered through the mist into his room, with the muffled voices that rumbled now and then from the street. He tried to make out the design that had once colored the faded carpet on the floor, and wondered about the dead artist in Japan, the adorner of his bureau. He speculated as to what his thoughts had been as he inserted the rainbow mother-of-pearl and made that great flight of shining birds, dipping their wings as they rose from the reeds, or how he had conceived the lacquer dragons in red gold, and the fantastic houses in the garden of peach-trees. But sooner or later the oppression of his grief returned, the loud shriek and clang of the garden-gate, the warning bell of some passing bicyclist steering through the fog, the noise of his pipe falling to the floor, would suddenly awaken him to the sense of misery. He knew that it was time to go out; he could not bear to sit still and suffer. Sometimes he cut a slice of bread and put it in his pocket, sometimes he trusted to the chance of finding a public-house, where he could have a sandwich and a glass of beer. He turned always from the main streets and lost himself in the intricate suburban byways, willing to be engulfed in the infinite whiteness of the mist.

===Chinese===

他把自己从白纸与闲笔那凄凉的凝望中驱赶出来，走进冰封空寂的街道，指望能从心里拔出那块燃烧的炭，火却没有熄。他狂乱地沿着铁硬狰狞的道路疾走，恍惚觉得那些迎面而过、欢欢喜喜赶去赴朋友与暖炉的人们，经过他时都畏缩着退进了雾里。卢西安想象，他那磨难与苦痛之火，必是以某种方式在他周身灼灼可见；他或许正移动在一圈光晕之中，昭示着内里的黑暗与火焰。他当然知道，自己是愁苦得神志昏乱了；那些从雾里朝他隐隐逼近的衣冠楚楚、礼帽光洁的人物，实际上只是冷得发抖；可尽管常识如此，他仍执意觉得自己在他们脸上看见了明白无误的恐怖与憎厌，还有几分人们看见一条毒蛇半死不活、拖着血污的可憎之躯爬出视野时的那种憎恶。卢西安存心往僻远荒凉的地方走；然而一旦当真挨到了旷野，知道了雾中逡巡的那片冰冷暗影是一块田地，他又渴念起人声与生活的低语，重新折回那些路上——苍白的灯微微闪烁，炉火的舞焰照过冻僵的灌木。这些家常炉火的光景，火边守候着的亲情与慰藉的念想，刺得他愈发锋痛：或许因为他自己的寒冷、疲惫与无助的病楚与之恰成对照，而更因为他知道，自己早已在心与这般幸福之间关上了一道永恒的门。倘若屋内的人走出来，唤着他的名字请他进去受一番安慰，也终归无用；因为他们与他之间，隔着一道命定的深渊。他大约是头一回意识到：他已经永远失去了人性的艺术。他原以为，当他塞住耳朵不再听林间的絮语，当他以街市的喧吟换掉法翁（faun）们的歌唱、以伦敦的暗影与琥珀色的灯火换掉黑色的水潭，他已然卸却旧日的生活，把灵魂转向了健全的营生；而真相是，他不过把一种毒品换成了另一种。他做不成人；他不免疑心，自己血液里掺着一滴精灵的血，才使他在世上成了一个异邦人、一个陌路客。

他并非不曾反复挣扎便向凄凉屈服。他设法以一桩轻省任务的许诺，诱自己回到书桌前；他不去尝试凭空创造，但笔记本里存着备忘和粗率的意想速记，他只须把现成在手的提示加以敷演。然而无望，一次又一次都是无望。他重读那些笔记，指望寻得一点暗示，能点亮死灰，重新燃起那纯净的热情之焰，却发现自己的运势坠落得何等凄惨。他以急切颤抖的手指匆匆涂下的那些字句里，他看不出一丝光、一毫色彩；他记得写下这些的时候它们何等辉煌，如今却全无意义，褪成了灰色。当初他满心欢喜地扑到纸上、以为它们许诺着欢乐时辰的那几行字，如今成了十足的呓语；即便懂得了其中的意思，那意思也显得愚蠢、乏味、平庸。他终于发现一样东西似乎还有一星指望，便打定主意尽力将它赋形，可第一段就叫他胆寒；那简直像出自一个愚钝学童的手笔。他把纸撕成碎片，关上书桌落了锁，沉重的绝望如铅沉入心底。那天余下的时光，他一动不动躺在床上，一斗接一斗地抽烟，指望用烟草的烟雾把自己熏得麻木。屋里的空气变得又蓝又浊；天寒彻骨，他裹上厚呢大衣，又拉过床罩盖在身上。夜色降临，窗户暗了下去，他终于睡着了。

他隔三差五重振旗鼓再试，结果只是更深地坠入悲苦。他觉出疯狂在步步逼近，知道自己唯一的指望，就是走到筋疲力尽，好回到家时几乎累得发晕，一上床便能睡去。他的上午都在一种昏沉里打发，竭力避开思想，让心神去忙活墙纸的花样、一本书末页的广告、透过雾霭渗进屋里来的那种古怪的灰色微光、街上不时隆隆传来的喑哑人语。他试着辨认地板上那张褪色地毯曾经有过的图样，悬想日本那位故去的艺人、他那写字台的装点者。他揣想那人嵌进虹彩的珠母、作出那一大群熠熠生辉的鸟儿——从芦苇间振翅而起时翼尖低回——时怀着怎样的心思；或者，他是怎样构想出红金色的髹漆蟠龙，和桃树园中那些奇幻的屋宇。可迟早他的悲愁总要重新压上心头；花园铁门刺耳的尖叫与哐啷、哪个骑车人在雾里穿行时的警铃、烟斗跌落地板的声响，都会猛然把他唤醒到苦楚的知觉里。他知道该出门了；他忍受不了干坐着受罪。有时他切一片面包揣进衣袋，有时就指望碰上一家酒馆，能吃一份三明治、喝一杯啤酒。他总是避开大街，把自己迷失在郊外错综的小巷里，甘愿被雾霭的无穷白色吞没。

===Original===

The roads had stiffened into iron ridges, the fences and trees were glittering with frost crystals, everything was of strange and altered aspect. Lucian walked on and on through the maze, now in a circle of shadowy villas, awful as the buried streets of Herculaneum, now in lanes dipping onto open country, that led him past great elm-trees whose white boughs were all still, and past the bitter lonely fields where the mist seemed to fade away into grey darkness. As he wandered along these unfamiliar and ghastly paths he became the more convinced of his utter remoteness from all humanity, he allowed that grotesque suggestion of there being something visibly amiss in his outward appearance to grow upon him, and often he looked with a horrible expectation into the faces of those who passed by, afraid lest his own senses gave him false intelligence, and that he had really assumed some frightful and revolting shape. It was curious that, partly by his own fault, and largely, no doubt, through the operation of mere coincidence, he was once or twice strongly confirmed in this fantastic delusion. He came one day into a lonely and unfrequented byway, a country lane falling into ruin, but still fringed with elms that had formed an avenue leading to the old manor-house. It was now the road of communication between two far outlying suburbs, and on these winter nights lay as black, dreary, and desolate as a mountain track. Soon after the frost began, a gentleman had been set upon in this lane as he picked his way between the corner where the bus had set him down, and his home where the fire was blazing, and his wife watched the clock. He was stumbling uncertainly through the gloom, growing a little nervous because the walk seemed so long, and peering anxiously for the lamp at the end of his street, when the two footpads rushed at him out of the fog. One caught him from behind, the other struck him with a heavy bludgeon, and as he lay senseless they robbed him of his watch and money, and vanished across the fields. The next morning all the suburb rang with the story; the unfortunate merchant had been grievously hurt, and wives watched their husbands go out in the morning with sickening apprehension, not knowing what might happen at night. Lucian of course was ignorant of all these rumors, and struck into the gloomy byroad without caring where he was or whither the way would lead him.

He had been driven out that day as with whips, another hopeless attempt to return to the work had agonised him, and existence seemed an intolerable pain. As he entered the deeper gloom, where the fog hung heavily, he began, half consciously, to gesticulate; he felt convulsed with torment and shame, and it was a sorry relief to clench his nails into his palm and strike the air as he stumbled heavily along, bruising his feet against the frozen ruts and ridges. His impotence was hideous, he said to himself, and he cursed himself and his life, breaking out into a loud oath, and stamping on the ground. Suddenly he was shocked at a scream of terror, it seemed in his very ear, and looking up he saw for a moment a woman gazing at him out of the mist, her features distorted and stiff with fear. A momentary convulsion twitched her arms into the ugly mimicry of a beckoning gesture, and she turned and ran for dear life, howling like a beast.

Lucian stood still in the road while the woman’s cries grew faint and died away. His heart was chilled within him as the significance of this strange incident became clear. He remembered nothing of his violent gestures; he had not known at the time that he had sworn out loud, or that he was grinding his teeth with impotent rage. He only thought of that ringing scream, of the horrible fear on the white face that had looked upon him, of the woman’s headlong flight from his presence. He stood trembling and shuddering, and in a little while he was feeling his face, searching for some loathsome mark, for the stigmata of evil branding his forehead. He staggered homewards like a drunken man, and when he came into the Uxbridge Road some children saw him and called after him as he swayed and caught at the lamppost. When he got to his room he sat down at first in the dark. He did not dare to light the gas. Everything in the room was indistinct, but he shut his eyes as he passed the dressing-table, and sat in a corner, his face turned to the wall. And when at last he gathered courage and the flame leapt hissing from the jet, he crept piteously towards the glass, and ducked his head, crouching miserably, and struggling with his terrors before he could look at his own image.

===Chinese===

道路冻成了铁硬的棱脊，栅栏和树木凝着霜晶闪闪发亮，一切都换了古怪而陌生的面目。卢西安在迷宫里走啊走，时而绕行在一圈影影绰绰的别墅之间，可怖如赫库兰尼姆（Herculaneum）被埋葬的街道；时而走进下倾通往旷野的小巷，被他引着经过一株株大榆树——洁白的枝桠纹丝不动——又经过那一片片苦寒孤寂的田野，雾霭在那里似乎渐渐淡入灰暗的冥色。他在这些陌生而阴森的路上游荡，愈发确信自己与整个人类相隔迢遥；他任凭那个怪诞的念头滋长——自己外表上有什么东西明明白白地不对劲；他时常带着骇人的预感去察看过路人的脸，唯恐自己的感官给了他虚假的消息，唯恐自己当真已变作一副狰狞可憎的形相。说来奇怪，一半由于他自己的过错，多半无疑只是巧合的作用，这荒诞的错觉竟有一两次得到了强烈的印证。有一天，他走进一条僻寂无人的小径，一条败落中的乡间小路，路边仍镶着榆树，从前原是通往古老庄园宅第的林荫道。如今它成了两个偏远郊区之间的通道，冬夜里横陈在那里，同山间小径一样黢黑、凄凉、荒僻。冰冻开始后不久，有位先生在这条巷子里遭到了袭击：公共汽车把他撂在路口，他从那里摸黑寻路回家——家里炉火正旺，妻子正看着钟等他。他深一脚浅一脚地在昏暗中踉跄，路显得那样长，他心里渐渐发慌，正焦灼地张望自家居前那盏街灯，两个拦路贼从雾里朝他扑来。一个从背后擒住他，另一个抡起沉重的短棍就打；趁他昏死过去，他们劫走了他的怀表和钱，窜过田野消失了。第二天早晨，整个郊区都传遍了这件事；那倒霉的商人伤得很重，主妇们清早望着丈夫出门，心里怀着令人作呕的忧惧，不知道夜里会出什么事。卢西安自然对这些风传一无所知，一头扎进那条阴郁的小路，也不管自己身在何处、这条路通向何方。

那一天，他是被鞭子似的赶出门的：重返那部书的又一次无望尝试把他折磨够了，生存本身似乎成了一份难忍的疼痛。走进雾霭沉沉的更深的幽暗里，他半自觉地比划起手势来；他被折磨与羞惭绞扭着，把指甲攥进掌心、踉跄前行时向空中挥击，倒也算一种可怜的宽解——他的脚在冻硬的车辙和棱脊上磕得青紫。他的无能是可憎的，他对自己说；他咒骂自己，咒骂自己的生命，迸出一声高声的咒誓，直跺脚。突然，一声恐怖的尖叫震得他一凛，仿佛就在他耳边；抬头的一瞬，他看见一个女人正从雾里凝视着他，五官因惊恐而扭曲僵硬。一阵痉挛把她的双臂抽搐成一个丑陋的、仿佛在招手的手势；她转身没命地奔逃，像一头野兽般嚎叫着。

卢西安呆立在路当中，女人的喊叫声渐渐微弱，终于消逝。这桩怪事的意义清晰起来时，他的心在胸腔里冷透了。他全然记不得自己那些激烈的比划；当时他并不知道自己出声咒骂过，也不知道自己正咬着牙、憋着无能为力的狂怒。他满脑子只有那声尖利的叫喊，只有那张望过他的白脸上的骇人恐惧，只有那女人从他面前不顾一切奔逃而去的背影。他站在那里发抖、战栗；不多时，他开始摸索自己的脸，搜寻某个可憎的印记，搜寻烙在他额头的邪恶圣痕（stigmata）。他像个醉汉似的踉跄着往家走；到了阿克斯布里奇路（Uxbridge Road），几个孩子看见他摇摇晃晃、一把抓住灯柱，便冲着他喊叫。回到自己的房间，他先是摸黑坐下。他不敢点煤气灯。屋里的一切都影影绰绰，可经过梳妆台时他还是闭上了眼睛，然后在一个角落里坐下，脸朝着墙。及至他终于鼓起勇气、火苗从喷嘴里嘶地蹿起，他才凄凄惶惶地朝镜子挪过去，垂着头，可怜地缩着身子，同自己的恐怖挣扎了半晌，才敢看自己的镜像。

===Original===

To the best of his power he tried to deliver himself from these more grotesque fantasies; he assured himself that there was nothing terrific in his countenance but sadness, that his face was like the face of other men. Yet he could not forget that reflection he had seen in the woman’s eyes, how the surest mirrors had shown him a horrible dread, her soul itself quailing and shuddering at an awful sight. Her scream rang and rang in his ears; she had fled away from him as if he offered some fate darker than death.

He looked again and again into the glass, tortured by a hideous uncertainty. His senses told him there was nothing amiss, yet he had had a proof, and yet, as he peered most earnestly, there was, it seemed, something strange and not altogether usual in the expression of the eyes. Perhaps it might be the unsteady flare of the gas, or perhaps a flaw in the cheap looking-glass, that gave some slight distortion to the image. He walked briskly up and down the room and tried to gaze steadily, indifferently, into his own face. He would not allow himself to be misguided by a word. When he had pronounced himself incapable of humanity, he had only meant that he could not enjoy the simple things of common life. A man was not necessarily monstrous, merely because he did not appreciate high tea, a quiet chat about the neighbors, and a happy noisy evening with the children. But with what message, then, did he appear charged that the woman’s mouth grew so stark? Her hands had jerked up as if they had been pulled with frantic wires; she seemed for the instant like a horrible puppet. Her scream was a thing from the nocturnal Sabbath.

He lit a candle and held it close up to the glass so that his own face glared white at him, and the reflection of the room became an indistinct darkness. He saw nothing but the candle flame and his own shining eyes, and surely they were not as the eyes of common men. As he put down the light, a sudden suggestion entered his mind, and he drew a quick breath, amazed at the thought. He hardly knew whether to rejoice or to shudder. For the thought he conceived was this: that he had mistaken all the circumstances of the adventure, and had perhaps repulsed a sister who would have welcomed him to the Sabbath.

===Chinese===

他竭尽全力想把自己从这些更荒诞的妄想里解脱出来；他宽慰自己，说他脸上除了悲哀并无可怕之物，说他的脸同旁人的脸并无不同。然而他忘不掉他在那女人眼睛里看见的映像：最确凿的镜子给他照出了一种骇人的惊惧，她的灵魂本身正为眼见的可怖之物瑟缩战栗。她的尖叫在他耳中萦回又萦回；她从他身边逃开，仿佛他携带着某种比死亡更黑暗的命运。

他一遍又一遍地照镜子，被一种可憎的悬疑难熬着。感官告诉他什么事也没有，可他毕竟得到过一个证据；而当他最恳切地细看时，那双眼睛的表情里，似乎又有某种陌生的、迥异寻常的东西。也许那是煤气灯不定的光焰，也许是廉价镜面上的一个疵点，给镜像添了一点轻微的歪扭。他在屋里轻快地踱来踱去，竭力要平稳地、漠然地凝视自己的脸。他不许自己被一个字眼引入歧途。他自认丧失了人性的时候，指的不过是他无法欣赏寻常生活里的简单事物。一个人未必就因此成了怪物，仅仅因为他不赏识傍晚那顿丰盛的茶点、一场关于左邻右舍的闲谈、一个同孩子们欢欢喜喜吵吵嚷嚷的夜晚。可是，他究竟携着怎样的讯息而来，那女人的嘴竟僵成那样？她的双手猛地扬起，仿佛被狂乱的提线拉扯；那一瞬她像一具可怕的木偶。她的尖叫，是夜半巫魔夜会（the Sabbath）里传来的东西。

他点起一支蜡烛，高高凑近镜前，好让自己的脸惨白地瞪视着自己，房间的映像则化作一片模糊的黑暗。他只看见烛焰和自己那双发亮的眼睛，而那双眼睛，确实不像常人的眼睛。放下烛台时，一个念头蓦地钻进心里，他猛吸一口气，被这想法惊住了。他几乎不知该欣喜还是该战栗。因为他生出的念头是这样的：他把这场奇遇的一切情形都弄错了，他推开的那一位，或许本是一位乐意迎他入巫魔夜会的姊妹。

===Original===

He lay awake all night, turning from one dreary and frightful thought to the other, scarcely dozing for a few hours when the dawn came. He tried for a moment to argue with himself when he got up; knowing that his true life was locked up in the bureau, he made a desperate attempt to drive the phantoms and hideous shapes from his mind. He was assured that his salvation was in the work, and he drew the key from his pocket, and made as if he would have opened the desk. But the nausea, the remembrances of repeated and utter failure, were too powerful. For many days he hung about the Manor Lane, half dreading, half desiring another meeting, and he swore he would not again mistake the cry of rapture, nor repulse the arms extended in a frenzy of delight. In those days he dreamed of some dark place where they might celebrate and make the marriage of the Sabbath, with such rites as he had dared to imagine.

It was perhaps only the shock of a letter from his father that rescued him from these evident approaches to madness. Mr. Taylor wrote how they had missed him at Christmas, how the farmers had inquired after him, of the homely familiar things that recalled his boyhood, his mother’s voice, the friendly fireside, and the good old fashions that had nurtured him. He remembered that he had once been a boy, loving the cake and puddings and the radiant holly, and all the seventeenth-century mirth that lingered on in the ancient farmhouses. And there came to him the more holy memory of Mass on Christmas morning. How sweet the dark and frosty earth had smelt as he walked beside his mother down the winding lane, and from the stile near the church they had seen the world glimmering to the dawn, and the wandering lanterns advancing across the fields. Then he had come into the church and seen it shining with candles and holly, and his father in pure vestments of white linen sang the longing music of the liturgy at the altar, and the people answered him, till the sun rose with the grave notes of the Paternoster, and a red beam stole through the chancel window.

The worst horror left him as he recalled the memory of these dear and holy things. He cast away the frightful fancy that the scream he had heard was a shriek of joy, that the arms, rigidly jerked out, invited him to an embrace. Indeed, the thought that he had longed for such an obscene illusion, that he had gloated over the recollection of that stark mouth, filled him with disgust. He resolved that his senses were deceived, that he had neither seen nor heard, but had for a moment externalized his own slumbering and morbid dreams. It was perhaps necessary that he should be wretched, that his efforts should be discouraged, but he would not yield utterly to madness.

===Chinese===

他整夜清醒地躺着，从这一个凄怖的念头翻到那一个，直到破晓才朦胧睡了几小时。起床后，他试着同自己辩驳了片刻；他知道自己的真正生命锁在写字台里，便发狠要把那些幻影和狰狞的形相从心里驱赶出去。他确信自己的救渡就在那部书里；他从口袋里掏出钥匙，作势要打开书桌。可那阵恶心，那一次次彻底惨败的记忆，实在太强大了。一连多日，他在庄园巷（the Manor Lane）一带徘徊，半是惧怕、半是渴盼另一场相遇；他发誓，这一回绝不会再错认那声狂喜的呼喊，绝不会再推开那双在欢悦的迷乱中伸展过来的臂膀。那些日子里，他梦想着某个幽暗的地方，他们可以在那里庆贺、缔结巫魔夜会的婚礼，行他敢于想象过的那些仪轨。

把他从这昭然逼近的疯狂里搭救出来的，或许只是一封父亲来信的震动。泰勒先生（Mr. Taylor）信里写道，圣诞时节他们如何惦念他，农夫们如何打听他的近况，还写到那些令他想起少年时代的家常旧物：母亲的声音，友爱的炉边，以及养育过他的那些善良的老风尚。他记起自己也曾是个孩子，爱着节庆的糕饼与布丁，爱着光艳夺目的冬青，爱着古旧农舍里绵延至今的十七世纪的欢愉。随后，一种更神圣的记忆降临到他身上：圣诞清晨的弥撒。他跟在母亲身旁走过蜿蜒的小巷时，那幽暗霜冻的大地闻起来何等甘甜；在教堂近旁的栅阶上，他们望见世界在曙光里微微生光，游动的灯笼正穿过田野迤逦而来。随后他走进教堂，看见满堂烛光与冬青交映生辉；父亲身着纯白的麻布祭衣，在圣坛上唱起礼仪经文渴慕的音乐，众人应和着，直到太阳伴着主祷文（Paternoster）庄严的诵声升起，一道红光悄悄潜过圣坛的窗。

当他忆起这些亲爱而神圣的事物，最深的恐怖便离开了他。他抛弃了那个骇人的妄想——以为自己听见的那声尖叫是狂喜的欢呼，以为那双僵直扬出的臂膀是在邀他入怀。的确，一想到自己竟渴念过这样一个淫邪的幻象、竟对那副僵张的嘴念念回味，他便满心憎恶。他断定是自己的感官受了骗：他既没有看见什么，也没有听见什么，只是一时间把自己沉睡的病梦外化成了形。或许他命该受苦，命该处处碰壁，但他不肯全然向疯狂屈膝。
===Original===

Yet when he went abroad with such good resolutions, it was hard to resist an influence that seemed to come from without and within. He did not know it, but people were everywhere talking of the great frost, of the fog that lay heavy on London, making the streets dark and terrible, of strange birds that came fluttering about the windows in the silent squares. The Thames rolled out duskily, bearing down the jarring ice-blocks, and as one looked on the black water from the bridges it was like a river in a northern tale. To Lucian it all seemed mythical, of the same substance as his own fantastic thoughts. He rarely saw a newspaper, and did not follow from day to day the systematic readings of the thermometer, the reports of ice-fairs, of coaches driven across the river at Hampton, of the skating on the fens; and hence the iron roads, the beleaguered silence and the heavy folds of mist appeared as amazing as a picture, significant, appalling. He could not look out and see a common suburban street foggy and dull, nor think of the inhabitants as at work or sitting cheerfully eating nuts about their fires; he saw a vision of a grey road vanishing, of dim houses all empty and deserted, and the silence seemed eternal. And when he went out and passed through street after street, all void, by the vague shapes of houses that appeared for a moment and were then instantly swallowed up, it seemed to him as if he had strayed into a city that had suffered some inconceivable doom, that he alone wandered where myriads had once dwelt. It was a town as great as Babylon, terrible as Rome, marvelous as Lost Atlantis, set in the midst of a white wilderness surrounded by waste places. It was impossible to escape from it; if he skulked between hedges, and crept away beyond the frozen pools, presently the serried stony lines confronted him like an army, and far and far they swept away into the night, as some fabled wall that guards an empire in the vast dim east. Or in that distorting medium of the mist, changing all things, he imagined that he trod an infinite desolate plain, abandoned from ages, but circled and encircled with dolmen and menhir that loomed out at him, gigantic, terrible. All London was one grey temple of an awful rite, ring within ring of wizard stones circled about some central place, every circle was an initiation, every initiation eternal loss. Or perhaps he was astray forever in a land of grey rocks. He had seen the light of home, the flicker of the fire on the walls; close at hand, it seemed, was the open door, and he had heard dear voices calling to him across the gloom, but he had just missed the path. The lamps vanished, the voices sounded thin and died away, and yet he knew that those within were waiting, that they could not bear to close the door, but waited, calling his name, while he had missed the way, and wandered in the pathless desert of the grey rocks. Fantastic, hideous, they beset him wherever he turned, piled up into strange shapes, pricked with sharp peaks, assuming the appearance of goblin towers, swelling into a vague dome like a fairy rath, huge and terrible. And as one dream faded into another, so these last fancies were perhaps the most tormenting and persistent; the rocky avenues became the camp and fortalice of some half-human, malignant race who swarmed in hiding, ready to bear him away into the heart of their horrible hills. It was awful to think that all his goings were surrounded, that in the darkness he was watched and surveyed, that every step but led him deeper and deeper into the labyrinth.

===Chinese===

然而，他怀着这样良好的决心出了门，却难于抵挡一种仿佛自外而来、又自内而生的力量。他并不知道，人们到处都在谈论这场大冰冻，谈论沉沉压住伦敦、把街道变得阴森可怖的浓雾，谈论寂静的广场上绕着窗棂扑打的怪鸟。泰晤士河幽暗地翻滚着，冲挟下哐哐作响的冰块；从桥上俯瞰那黑沉沉的水，它就像北方传说里的一条河。在卢西安眼里，这一切都像是神话，同他自己那些诡奇的念头是同一种质料。他难得见到报纸，也不逐日追随那些按部就班的气温度数记录、冰上集市的报道、马车在汉普顿碾冰渡河的消息、沼泽地带的滑冰盛况；于是，铁硬的道路、遭围困的寂静、雾霭沉重的褶皱，便如一幅画般令人惊异，意味深长，骇人心目。他无法望出去看见一条寻常郊区街道的阴沉晦暗，也无法想象居民们正在劳作、或正围炉欢欢喜喜嗑着坚果；他看见的是一个幻象：灰色的道路渐渐消隐，幽暗的房舍尽皆空寂废弃，而那寂静似乎是永恒的。他走出去，穿过一条又一条空无一人的街道，经过那些乍一显现、随即被吞没的房屋的模糊轮廓，恍惚间他像是误入了一座遭受过某种不可思议的厄运的城池，在曾经住着万千生灵的地方，只有他一人独自徘徊。这是一座与巴比伦同样伟大的城，与罗马同样可怖，与沉没的亚特兰蒂斯同样奇瑰，坐落在白色荒漠的正中，四围皆是旷废之地。要想逃出它是不可能的；他若在树篱间潜行，悄悄爬过冰封的水潭，转眼间那一列列密匝匝的石砌街巷便像一支大军迎面逼来，逶迤着扫进夜色深处，宛如传说中拱卫帝国、绵亘于苍茫辽远的东方的那道城墙。或者，在那改变万物的雾霭这面畸变的透镜里，他想象自己正踏着一片无边荒凉的原野，亘古以来便遭废弃，却环着重重圈围的史前巨石——石棚（dolmen）与门希尔竖石（menhir）森森然朝他逼视，庞大，可怖。整个伦敦是一座灰色神庙，供奉着一场可畏的秘仪；一环套一环的巫石，圈围着某个中心之地；每一道环都是一次入仪，每一次入仪都是永恒的沉沦。又或者，他永远迷失在一片灰色岩石之地。他曾见过家的灯火，见过墙上跳动的炉焰；敞开的门似乎近在咫尺，他也曾听见亲爱的话语穿过幽暗向他召唤——可他偏偏错过了那条小路。灯灭了，人语细弱下去、消散了；然而他知道屋里的人还在等候，不忍关上那扇门，只是一边等，一边唤着他的名字；而他已错过了路，正在灰色岩石的无径荒漠里流浪。那些岩石奇幻而狰狞，他转到哪里便围困到哪里：堆叠成种种怪状，刺起尖峰，扮作妖精的塔楼，又隆起为模糊的穹丘，像一座精灵古冢（fairy rath），庞大而可怖。一个梦淡入另一个梦，这最后的几段幻想，或许也正是最折磨人、最执拗的：岩石的巷道变成了某个半人的、恶毒种族的营盘与堡寨，那族人潜藏蜂聚，随时准备把他掳进他们可怖群山的腹地。一想到他的一切行踪尽遭围困，一想到黑暗里他正被人窥望、侦伺，每一步都只是把他越引越深地领进迷宫——这念头实在可怕。

===Original===

When, of an evening, he was secure in his room, the blind drawn down and the gas flaring, he made vigorous efforts toward sanity. It was not of his free will that he allowed terror to overmaster him, and he desired nothing better than a placid and harmless life, full of work and clear thinking. He knew that he deluded himself with imagination, that he had been walking through London suburbs and not through Pandemonium, and that if he could but unlock his bureau all those ugly forms would be resolved into the mist. But it was hard to say if he consoled himself effectually with such reflections, for the return to common sense meant also the return to the sharp pangs of defeat. It recalled him to the bitter theme of his own inefficiency, to the thought that he only desired one thing of life, and that this was denied him. He was willing to endure the austerities of a monk in a severe cloister, to suffer cold, to be hungry, to be lonely and friendless, to forbear all the consolation of friendly speech, and to be glad of all these things, if only he might be allowed to illuminate the manuscript in quietness. It seemed a hideous insufferable cruelty, that he should so fervently desire that which he could never gain.

He was led back to the old conclusion; he had lost the sense of humanity, he was wretched because he was an alien and a stranger amongst citizens. It seemed probable that the enthusiasm of literature, as he understood it, the fervent desire for the fine art, had in it something of the inhuman, and dissevered the enthusiast from his fellow-creatures. It was possible that the barbarian suspected as much, that by some slow process of rumination he had arrived at his fixed and inveterate impression, by no means a clear reasoned conviction; the average Philistine, if pressed for the reasons of his dislike, would either become inarticulate, ejaculating “faugh” and “pah” like an old-fashioned Scots Magazine, or else he would give some imaginary and absurd reason, alleging that all “littery men” were poor, that composers never cut their hair, that painters were rarely public-school men, that sculptors couldn’t ride straight to hounds to save their lives, but clearly these imbecilities were mere afterthoughts; the average man hated the artist from a deep instinctive dread of all that was strange, uncanny, alien to his nature; he gibbered, uttered his harsh, semi-bestial “faugh,” and dismissed Keats to his gallipots from much the same motives as usually impelled the black savages to dismiss the white man on an even longer journey.

Lucian was not especially interested in this hatred of the barbarian for the maker, except from this point, that it confirmed him in his belief that the love of art dissociated the man from the race. One touch of art made the whole world alien, but surely miseries of the civilized man cast amongst savages were not so much caused by dread of their ferocity as by the terror of his own thoughts; he would perhaps in his last despair leave his retreat and go forth to perish at their hands, so that he might at least die in company, and hear the sound of speech before death. And Lucian felt most keenly that in his case there was a double curse; he was as isolated as Keats, and as inarticulate as his reviewers. The consolation of the work had failed him, and he was suspended in the void between two worlds.

===Chinese===

到了晚间，他安然守在自己房间里，百叶窗放下，煤气灯烧得旺，便发狠朝着清明的神志努力。他听任恐怖把自己压倒，并非出于自愿；他最渴望的不过是一种平安无害的生活，充满劳作与澄明的思想。他知道自己是在用想象自欺，知道自己走过的是伦敦的郊区而不是万魔殿（Pandemonium），也知道只要能打开那架写字台，所有那些丑陋的形相便会消解于雾中。可是这类思量能否真正安慰他，却难说得很；因为回到常识，也意味着回到惨败的锐痛。它把他重新召回到自己无能这个苦涩的题目上，召回到那个念头上：他对生命只求一件事，而这件事偏被剥夺了。他甘愿忍受严苛修道院里修士的苦行，甘愿受冻、挨饿、孤寂无友，戒绝一切友好言谈的慰藉，并且甘之如饴——只要容他安安静静地为手稿做泥金饰彩。他如此炽烈地渴求那永不可得之物，这看来是一种可憎的、难堪的残忍。

他又被领回那个古老的结论：他丧失了人性的知觉；他痛苦，因为他在公民们中间是个异类、是个陌路客。看来很可能，文学的狂热——照他所理解的那种——对精美艺术的炽烈渴慕，本身就含着几分非人的东西，把狂热者从他的同类中间割裂了出去。野蛮人未尝不隐约察觉到这一层；经过某种缓慢的反刍，他得出了那个固定而顽强的印象——那绝说不上是一种清晰的、推理出来的确信。寻常的非利士人，倘若被逼问不喜欢的理由，要么就变得语无伦次，像一份老派的《苏格兰杂志》（Scots Magazine）只会迸出「呸」和「啐」；要么就给出某种凭空捏造的荒唐理由，说「舞文弄墨的家伙」个个穷酸，说作曲家从来不肯剪头发，说画家难得是公立学校出身，说雕塑家就是拼了命也骑不稳猎犬——可这些蠢话分明都是事后补造的说辞；寻常人憎恨艺术家，是出于对一切陌生的、诡异的、异于他天性之物的深深的、本能的恐惧；他咿呀乱语，发出刺耳的、半兽性的「呸」，把济慈（Keats）打发回他的药膏罐去——其所由来，同通常驱使黑色蛮人打发白人踏上更漫长旅程的动机，几乎如出一辙。

卢西安对野蛮人憎恨创造者这件事并不特别在意，只有一点除外：它印证了他的信念——对艺术的爱使人脱离了他的种族。艺术沾上一分，全世界便成了异邦；不过，沦落在野蛮人中间的文明人，其痛苦与其说出于对他们凶残的畏惧，不如说出于对自己念头的恐惧；在最后的绝望里，他或许会走出藏身之所，送上门去死在他们手里，好歹死得有个伴，死前还能听见人语的声音。而卢西安最痛切地感到，他的处境是一种双重的咒诅：他像济慈一样孤立，又像他的书评家一样拙于言辞。那部书的慰藉也已弃他而去；他悬在两个世界之间的虚空里。

===Original===

It was no doubt the composite effect of his failures, his loneliness of soul, and solitude of life, that had made him invest those common streets with such grim and persistent terrors. He had perhaps yielded to a temptation without knowing that he had been tempted, and, in the manner of De Quincey, had chosen the subtle in exchange for the more tangible pains. Unconsciously, but still of free will, he had preferred the splendor and the gloom of a malignant vision before his corporal pains, before the hard reality of his own impotence. It was better to dwell in vague melancholy, to stray in the forsaken streets of a city doomed from ages, to wander amidst forlorn and desperate rocks than to awake to a gnawing and ignoble torment, to confess that a house of business would have been more suitable and more practical, that he had promised what he could never perform. Even as he struggled to beat back the phantasmagoria of the mist, and resolved that he would no longer make all the streets a stage of apparitions, he hardly realized what he had done, or that the ghosts he had called might depart and return again.

He continued his long walks, always with the object of producing a physical weariness and exhaustion that would enable him to sleep of nights. But even when he saw the foggy and deserted avenues in their proper shape, and allowed his eyes to catch the pale glimmer of the lamps, and the dancing flame of the firelight, he could not rid himself of the impression that he stood afar off, that between those hearths and himself there was a great gulf fixed. As he paced down the footpath he could often see plainly across the frozen shrubs into the homely and cheerful rooms. Sometimes, late in the evening, he caught a passing glimpse of the family at tea, father, mother, and children laughing and talking together, well pleased with each other’s company. Sometimes a wife or a child was standing by the garden gate peering anxiously through the fog, and the sight of it all, all the little details, the hideous but comfortable armchairs turned ready to the fire, maroon-red curtains being drawn close to shut out the ugly night, the sudden blaze and illumination as the fire was poked up so that it might be cheerful for father; these trivial and common things were acutely significant. They brought back to him the image of a dead boy﻿—himself. They recalled the shabby old “parlour” in the country, with its shabby old furniture and fading carpet, and renewed a whole atmosphere of affection and homely comfort. His mother would walk to the end of the drive and look out for him when he was late (wandering then about the dark woodlands); on winter evenings she would make the fire blaze, and have his slippers warming by the hearth, and there was probably buttered toast “as a treat.” He dwelt on all these insignificant petty circumstances, on the genial glow and light after the muddy winter lanes, on the relish of the buttered toast and the smell of the hot tea, on the two old cats curled fast asleep before the fender, and made them instruments of exquisite pain and regret. Each of these strange houses that he passed was identified in his mind with his own vanished home; all was prepared and ready as in the old days, but he was shut out, judged and condemned to wander in the frozen mist, with weary feet, anguished and forlorn, and they that would pass from within to help him could not, neither could he pass to them. Again, for the hundredth time, he came back to the sentence: he could not gain the art of letters and he had lost the art of humanity. He saw the vanity of all his thoughts; he was an ascetic caring nothing for warmth and cheerfulness and the small comforts of life, and yet he allowed his mind to dwell on such things. If one of those passersby, who walked briskly, eager for home, should have pitied him by some miracle and asked him to come in, it would have been worse than useless, yet he longed for pleasures that he could not have enjoyed. It was as if he were come to a place of torment, where they who could not drink longed for water, where they who could feel no warmth shuddered in the eternal cold. He was oppressed by the grim conceit that he himself still slept within the matted thicket, imprisoned by the green bastions of the Roman fort. He had never come out, but a changeling had gone down the hill, and now stirred about the earth.

===Chinese===

毫无疑问，正是他的种种惨败、灵魂的孤独与生活的孤寂合在一起，才使他给这些寻常的街道披上了如此阴森而执拗的恐怖。他或许是在不知受诱的情况下屈从了一个诱惑，并且效法德·昆西（De Quincey）的故技，选取了幽微的痛楚，来替换那些更切身的疼痛。他不自觉地、却又是出于自由意志地，宁取那凶邪幻象的辉煌与幽暗，而不取他身体的疼痛，不取他自己无能那铁硬的现实。与其醒来承受一种啮人的、卑琐的煎熬，与其承认一间商行本来更适合他、也更实际，承认他许诺了他永远兑现不了的东西，不如栖居在朦胧的忧郁里，流浪在一座亘古注定废弃的城池那遭人遗弃的街道上，徘徊在凄绝无望的岩石之间。即便当他奋力击退雾霭的幻灯戏景，决意不再把所有街道变成群鬼登台的场所时，他也几乎不曾意识到自己做了什么，不曾意识到他召来的那些鬼魂或许会离去，还会再回来。

他继续着他的长途漫步，始终抱着一个目的：制造出一种身体上的困乏与耗竭，好让他夜里睡得着。可即便当他看清雾中空寂的林荫道的本来形状，让眼睛去捕捉灯的微光与炉火的舞焰时，他也摆脱不掉那个印象：他远远地站着，那些炉边与他之间隔着一道命定的深渊。他沿着人行道踱下去，常常能越过冻僵的灌木，清清楚楚望进那些家常而欢快的房间。有时入夜已深，他瞥见一家人正在用茶，父亲、母亲和孩子们又说又笑，彼此作伴其乐融融。有时一个妻子或孩子立在花园门口，隔着雾焦灼地张望。这全部的光景，全部的细枝末节——一张张难看却舒适的扶手椅，早已朝火炉摆好；栗红色的窗帘拉得严严实实，把丑陋的夜关在外面；炉火被捅旺时骤然亮起的一片辉光，好叫父亲回来觉得暖意——这些琐屑寻常之物，都尖锐地显出意义。它们把一个死去的孩子带回他眼前——那孩子就是他自己。它们唤回乡下那间寒酸的旧「客厅」，连同寒酸的旧家具和褪色的地毯，重温了一整套亲情与家常温暖的气氛。他晚归时（那时他正在幽暗的林间游荡），母亲总会走到车道尽头去望他；冬天的傍晚，她会把炉火拨得旺旺的，把他的拖鞋搁在壁炉边烘着，多半还有黄油烤面包片，算作「特别的款待」。他流连于所有这些细碎不足道的情境：泥泞的冬巷之后那融融的炉光与暖意，黄油烤面包的滋味与热茶的香气，炉档前蜷作一团酣睡的两只老猫——他把它们统统变成了精微的痛楚与悔恨的刑具。他经过的每一座陌生的房子，在他心里都与他那消逝的家合而为一；一切都像旧日那样备办停当，唯独他被关在门外，被判了罪，被判在冰冻的雾里流浪，双足疲惫，凄惶无告；屋里的人想出来救助他，却不能；他也不能进去。于是，第一百次，他又回到那句话上：他赢不得文字的艺术，也早失去了人性的艺术。他看得出自己一切念头的虚妄：他是个苦行者，于温暖、欢悦与生活的些微舒适本无所求，却偏又容许自己的心在这些东西上流连。倘若那些步履轻快、归心似箭的过路人中，有谁出于奇迹怜悯他、请他进屋，那也只会更糟；然而他渴念着他自己无从享受的欢愉。这就仿佛他来到了一个受苦刑的地方：不能喝水的人渴念着水，感觉不到温暖的人在永恒的寒冷里瑟瑟发抖。他被一个阴森的玄想压住：他自己仍旧睡在那片缠结的灌木丛里，被那座罗马堡垒的绿色壁垒囚禁着。出来的从来不是他；下山去、如今在世上走动的，不过是一个精灵换下的替身（changeling）。

===Original===

Beset by such ingenious terrors, it was not wonderful that outward events and common incidents should abet his fancies. He had succeeded one day in escaping from the mesh of the streets, and fell on a rough and narrow lane that stole into a little valley. For the moment he was in a somewhat happier mood; the afternoon sun glowed through the rolling mist, and the air grew clearer. He saw quiet and peaceful fields, and a wood descending in a gentle slope from an old farmstead of warm red brick. The farmer was driving the slow cattle home from the hill, and his loud halloo to his dog came across the land a cheerful mellow note. From another side a cart was approaching the clustered barns, hesitating, pausing while the great horses rested, and then starting again into lazy motion. In the well of the valley a wandering line of bushes showed where a brook crept in and out amongst the meadows, and, as Lucian stood, lingering, on the bridge, a soft and idle breath ruffled through the boughs of a great elm. He felt soothed, as by calm music, and wondered whether it would not be better for him to live in some such quiet place, within reach of the streets and yet remote from them. It seemed a refuge for still thoughts; he could imagine himself sitting at rest beneath the black yew tree in the farm garden, at the close of a summer day. He had almost determined that he would knock at the door and ask if they would take him as a lodger, when he saw a child running towards him down the lane. It was a little girl, with bright curls tossing about her head, and, as she came on, the sunlight glowed upon her, illuminating her brick-red frock and the yellow kingcups in her hat. She had run with her eyes on the ground, chirping and laughing to herself, and did not see Lucian till she was quite near him. She started and glanced into his eyes for a moment, and began to cry; he stretched out his hand, and she ran from him screaming, frightened no doubt by what was to her a sudden and strange apparition. He turned back towards London, and the mist folded him in its thick darkness, for on that evening it was tinged with black.

It was only by the intensest strain of resolution that he did not yield utterly to the poisonous anodyne which was always at hand. It had been a difficult struggle to escape from the mesh of the hills, from the music of the fauns, and even now he was drawn by the memory of these old allurements. But he felt that here, in his loneliness, he was in greater danger, and beset by a blacker magic. Horrible fancies rushed wantonly into his mind; he was not only ready to believe that something in his soul sent a shudder through all that was simple and innocent, but he came trembling home one Saturday night, believing, or half-believing, that he was in communion with evil. He had passed through the clamorous and blatant crowd of the “high street,” where, as one climbed the hill, the shops seemed all aflame, and the black night air glowed with the flaring gas-jets and the naphtha-lamps, hissing and wavering before the February wind. Voices, raucous, clamant, abominable, were belched out of the blazing public-houses as the doors swung to and fro, and above these doors were hideous brassy lamps, very slowly swinging in a violent blast of air, so that they might have been infernal thuribles, censing the people. Some man was calling his wares in one long continuous shriek that never stopped or paused, and, as a respond, a deeper, louder voice roared to him from across the road. An Italian whirled the handle of his piano-organ in a fury, and a ring of imps danced mad figures around him, danced and flung up their legs till the rags dropped from some of them, and they still danced on. A flare of naphtha, burning with a rushing noise, threw a light on one point of the circle, and Lucian watched a lank girl of fifteen as she came round and round to the flash. She was quite drunk, and had kicked her petticoats away, and the crowd howled laughter and applause at her. Her black hair poured down and leapt on her scarlet bodice; she sprang and leapt round the ring, laughing in Bacchic frenzy, and led the orgy to triumph. People were crossing to and fro, jostling against each other, swarming about certain shops and stalls in a dense dark mass that quivered and sent out feelers as if it were one writhing organism. A little farther a group of young men, arm in arm, were marching down the roadway chanting some music-hall verse in full chorus, so that it sounded like plainsong. An impossible hubbub, a hum of voices angry as swarming bees, the squeals of five or six girls who ran in and out, and dived up dark passages and darted back into the crowd; all these mingled together till his ears quivered. A young fellow was playing the concertina, and he touched the keys with such slow fingers that the tune wailed solemn into a dirge; but there was nothing so strange as the burst of sound that swelled out when the public-house doors were opened.

===Chinese===

被这般精巧的恐怖围困着，外物与寻常小事竟也来助长他的幻想，也就不足为奇了。有一天，他总算从街衢的罗网里脱身出来，撞见一条粗砺窄小的巷子，悄悄潜入一道小小的山谷。那一刻他的心情竟轻快了些；午后的太阳透过翻涌的雾霭灼灼放光，空气渐渐清朗。他望见安宁平静的田野，望见一片树林从一座暖红色砖墙的老农舍旁徐徐缓降。农夫正把迟缓的牛群从山上赶回家，他朝狗的大声呼喝越过田野传来，是一声欢快醇厚的调子。另一边，一辆大车正向成群的谷仓挨近，踌躇着，停下来让高大的马匹歇息，随即又懒懒地重新走动。谷底一线蜿蜒的灌木，指示着一条小溪在草甸间出没的踪迹；卢西安在桥上流连伫立时，一阵轻软懒散的微风拂过一株大榆树的枝头。他感到被抚慰了，像听了一曲平静的音乐；他思忖，自己若住在这样一处僻静地方，与街市相去不远而又迥然相隔，岂不更好。这里似乎是静思的庇护所；他能想象夏日的黄昏，自己安坐在农园那株黑黢黢的紫杉树下。他几乎已经打定主意，要上去叩那扇门，问他们可愿收他做个房客——就在这时，他看见一个孩子顺着巷子朝他跑来。是个小姑娘，一头明亮的鬈发在头上颠动；她跑近时，阳光照在她身上，照亮了她砖红色的衣裙和帽子上黄色的驴蹄草。她一路眼睛盯着地面，自顾自叽叽喳喳地笑着，直到离他极近才看见卢西安。她一惊，朝他眼睛里望了一瞬，就哭了起来；他伸出手，她尖叫着从他身边跑开——无疑是被这个在她看来突如其来、怪异如鬼魅的形相吓着了。他转身回伦敦去，雾用它浓重的黑暗把他裹住，因为那天傍晚，雾里洇着黑色。

全凭最紧绷的一股决绝，他才没有全然屈从于那随手可得的毒质止痛剂。当初逃离群山的罗网、逃离法翁们的音乐，已是一场艰难的挣扎；即便此刻，那些旧诱惑的记忆仍在引拽着他。但他感到，在这里，在他的孤独里，他处在更大的危险中，被一种更黑的魔法围困。骇人的幻想恣意涌进他的头脑；他不仅渐渐相信，他灵魂里的某种东西能使一切单纯无辜之物为之一颤；而且有一个星期六的夜晚，他浑身发抖地回到家里，相信——或者半相信——自己正与邪恶相通。他刚穿过「大街」上那喧嚣刺耳的人潮：沿着上山的坡路，店铺似乎尽数着火，黑色的夜空被灼灼的煤气灯和石脑油灯映得通红，在二月的风里嘶响、摇曳。酒馆的门来回开阖，喷出一阵阵嘶哑、急切、可憎的人声；门楣上方挂着黄铜的丑怪灯盏，在猛烈的疾风里极缓地摇摆，俨然地狱的提炉，向人群熏着香。有个汉子在叫卖他的货色，一声长嚎连绵不断、无休无歇；作为应答，马路对面一个更低沉、更响的嗓子朝他吼了回去。一个意大利人狂怒地摇着手摇风琴的柄，一圈小鬼围着他狂舞，跳着，蹬着腿，直到有人身上的破布片都跳落了，还在跳。一股石脑油的火焰嗖嗖地燃着，照亮了圆圈的一角；卢西安望着一个十五岁的瘦长姑娘，一圈又一圈地转到光亮里来。她醉得厉害，把裙子都踢脱了，人群冲她嚎出哄笑与喝彩。她的黑发倾泻下来，在她猩红的胸衣上跳跃；她绕着圈又蹦又跳，带着巴克斯式的癫狂大笑，把这场狂欢引向凯旋。人们来来往往穿行着，彼此推搡，密密匝匝地蜂拥在几家店铺和货摊四周，结成一块黑压压的、颤动并伸出触须的团块，仿佛一个盘扭的整体生物。稍远一点，一群年轻人挽着臂，沿车行道齐步而下，齐声唱着杂耍剧场的小调，那唱法听来竟像素歌（plainsong）。一片难以想象的喧嚣：一片人声的嗡营，愤怒如蜂群；五六个女孩的尖叫，钻进黑洞洞的巷口又箭一般窜回人群；这一切混作一团，直到他的耳膜发颤。一个小伙子在拉六角手风琴，指头按得那样迟缓，曲调哀哀地庄严成了一支哀歌；然而最古怪的，还是酒馆的门一开时轰然涌出的那一阵声浪。
===Original===

He walked amongst these people, looked at their faces, and looked at the children amongst them. He had come out thinking that he would see the English working class, “the best-behaved and the best-tempered crowd in the world,” enjoying the simple pleasure of the Saturday night’s shopping. Mother bought the joint for Sunday’s dinner, and perhaps a pair of boots for father; father had an honest glass of beer, and the children were given bags of sweets, and then all these worthy people went decently home to their well-earned rest. De Quincey had enjoyed the sight in his day, and had studied the rise and fall of onions and potatoes. Lucian, indeed, had desired to take these simple emotions as an opiate, to forget the fine fret and fantastic trouble of his own existence in plain things and the palpable joy of rest after labour. He was only afraid lest he should be too sharply reproached by the sight of these men who fought bravely year after year against starvation, who knew nothing of intricate and imagined grief, but only the weariness of relentless labour, of the long battle for their wives and children. It would be pathetic, he thought, to see them content with so little, brightened by the expectation of a day’s rest and a good dinner, forced, even then, to reckon every penny, and to make their children laugh with halfpence. Either he would be ashamed before so much content, or else he would be again touched by the sense of his inhumanity which could take no interest in the common things of life. But still he went to be at least taken out of himself, to be forced to look at another side of the world, so that he might perhaps forget a little while his own sorrows.

He was fascinated by what he saw and heard. He wondered whether De Quincey also had seen the same spectacle, and had concealed his impressions out of reverence for the average reader. Here there were no simple joys of honest toilers, but wonderful orgies, that drew out his heart to horrible music. At first the violence of sound and sight had overwhelmed him; the lights flaring in the night wind, the array of naphtha lamps, the black shadows, the roar of voices. The dance about the piano-organ had been the first sign of an inner meaning, and the face of the dark girl as she came round and round to the flame had been amazing in its utter furious abandon. And what songs they were singing all around him, and what terrible words rang out, only to excite peals of laughter. In the public-houses the workmen’s wives, the wives of small tradesmen, decently dressed in black, were drinking their faces to a flaming red, and urging their husbands to drink more. Beautiful young women, flushed and laughing, put their arms round the men’s necks and kissed them, and then held up the glass to their lips. In the dark corners, at the openings of side streets, the children were talking together, instructing each other, whispering what they had seen; a boy of fifteen was plying a girl of twelve with whisky, and presently they crept away. Lucian passed them as they turned to go, and both looked at him. The boy laughed, and the girl smiled quietly. It was above all in the faces around him that he saw the most astounding things, the Bacchic fury unveiled and unashamed. To his eyes it seemed as if these revelers recognized him as a fellow, and smiled up in his face, aware that he was in the secret. Every instinct of religion, of civilization even, was swept away; they gazed at one another and at him, absolved of all scruples, children of the earth and nothing more. Now and then a couple detached themselves from the swarm, and went away into the darkness, answering the jeers and laughter of their friends as they vanished.

===Chinese===

他走进这些人中间，看他们的脸，看他们中间的孩子。他出门时想的是，他将看到英国的劳动阶级——「世界上最守规矩、最好脾气的群氓」——享受着星期六夜里购物的简单快乐。母亲买下礼拜天正餐的大块肉，或许还给父亲捎一双靴子；父亲喝上一杯老实的啤酒，孩子们得到几袋糖果；随后这些善良的人们便体体面面地回家，去享他们挣来的安息。德·昆西当年也观赏过这景象，还研究过洋葱与土豆的涨落。卢西安原本也正想拿这些简单的情绪当一剂鸦片，好把自己生存里那精细的焦灼与想入非非的烦恼，忘在平平常常的事物里，忘在劳作之后那触手可及的安息之乐里。他只是害怕，眼前这些人的光景会给他太尖锐的责难：这些人年复一年英勇地与饥饿搏斗，不识繁复错综、凭空虚构的悲愁，只认得无情劳苦的疲惫，只认得为妻儿打的那场漫长的仗。他想，看他们满足于这么一点点，被一天休息和一顿好饭的指望照亮，却即便在那时也不得不一个便士一个便士地算计，用半便士的铜子儿逗孩子发笑——这光景定然令人心酸。他要不在这样多的知足面前羞愧无地，要不就再一次被自己的非人之感所刺痛——他对生活的寻常之物竟无从起一点兴味。可他还是去了，为的是至少暂时忘掉自己，强迫自己看一看世界的另一面，好或许把他自己的悲哀忘却片刻。

他为所见所闻迷住了。他不免疑惑，德·昆西当年见到的可也是这同一番景象，只是出于对普通读者的敬畏，才把印象隐去了。这里没有诚实劳动者们的简单欢愉，有的是一场场奇妙的纵欲狂欢，把他的心弦拨向可怕的音乐。起初，声与光的暴力把他压倒了：夜风里灼灼的灯焰，成排的石脑油灯，黑色的影子，人声的咆哮。围着手摇风琴的那场舞，是内里意义露出的第一个征兆；那黑发姑娘一圈圈转到火光前来的那张脸，那副全然狂乱的放纵，惊心动魄。而他四周唱的是些什么歌啊，响起的又是些什么可怕的字句啊——响起，只为激起阵阵哄笑。酒馆里，做工人的妻子、小店主的妻子们，一身体面的黑衣，把脸喝得通红，还怂恿丈夫们再多喝。年轻貌美的女人们红着脸、笑着，把胳膊搂上男人的脖颈亲吻他们，再把酒杯举到他们唇边。幽暗的角落里，岔路的巷口，孩子们聚在一起交谈，彼此传授，低声交换他们的所见；一个十五岁的男孩正拿威士忌劝一个十二岁的女孩，不多时两人便悄悄溜走了。两人转身要走时，卢西安从他们旁边经过；两个都看了看他。男孩笑了笑，女孩静静地微笑了。尤其在他四周的那些脸上，他看见了最骇人的东西：巴克斯式的狂怒，毫无遮掩，全无愧色。在他眼里，这些狂欢者仿佛认出他是同道，仰起脸朝他微笑，知道他识得其中的秘密。宗教的、乃至文明的一切本能都被荡涤一空；他们彼此对望，也望着他，解脱了一切顾忌，只是大地的儿女，仅此而已。不时有一对男女从人堆里脱身出来，走进黑暗里去，一面消隐，一面回应着朋友们的讪笑与哄笑。

===Original===

On the edge of the pavement, not far from where he was standing, Lucian noticed a tall and lovely young woman who seemed to be alone. She was in the full light of a naphtha flame, and her bronze hair and flushed cheeks shone illuminate as she viewed the orgy. She had dark brown eyes, and a strange look as of an old picture in her face; and her eyes brightened with an urgent gleam. He saw the revelers nudging each other and glancing at her, and two or three young men went up and asked her to come for a walk. She shook her head and said “No thank you” again and again, and seemed as if she were looking for somebody in the crowd.

“I’m expecting a friend,” she said at last to a man who proposed a drink and a walk afterwards; and Lucian wondered what kind of friend would ultimately appear. Suddenly she turned to him as he was about to pass on, and said in a low voice:

“I’ll go for a walk with you if you like; you just go on, and I’ll follow in a minute.”

For a moment he looked steadily at her. He saw that the first glance had misled him; her face was not flushed with drink as he had supposed, but it was radiant with the most exquisite color, a red flame glowed and died on her cheek, and seemed to palpitate as she spoke. The head was set on the neck nobly, as in a statue, and about the ears the bronze hair strayed into little curls. She was smiling and waiting for his answer.

He muttered something about being very sorry, and fled down the hill out of the orgy, from the noise of roaring voices and the glitter of the great lamps very slowly swinging in the blast of wind. He knew that he had touched the brink of utter desolation; there was death in the woman’s face, and she had indeed summoned him to the Sabbath. Somehow he had been able to refuse on the instant, but if he had delayed he knew he would have abandoned himself to her, body and soul. He locked himself in his room and lay trembling on the bed, wondering if some subtle sympathy had shown the woman her perfect companion. He looked in the glass, not expecting now to see certain visible and outward signs, but searching for the meaning of that strange glance that lit up his eyes. He had grown even thinner than before in the last few months, and his cheeks were wasted with hunger and sorrow, but there were still about his features the suggestion of a curious classic grace, and the look as of a faun who has strayed from the vineyards and olive gardens. He had broken away, but now he felt the mesh of her net about him, a desire for her that was a madness, as if she held every nerve in his body and drew him to her, to her mystic world, to the rosebush where every flower was a flame.

===Chinese===

在人行道边上，离他站的地方不远，卢西安注意到一个高挑而可爱的年轻女子，似乎是独自一人。她正站在一盏石脑油灯的全部光焰里，青铜色的头发和红晕的双颊在观览这场狂欢时灼灼生辉。她有一双深棕色的眼睛，脸上带着一种古画般的奇异神情；她的眼睛亮着一种急切的光。他看见狂欢者们彼此用胳膊肘轻捣、朝她张望，有两三个年轻人走上前邀她去散步。她摇着头，一遍又一遍地说「不，谢谢你」，那神气像是在人群里寻找什么人。

「我在等一位朋友，」她终于对一个提议先喝一杯、再散一会儿步的男人说道；卢西安不免好奇，最终会出现的是怎样一位朋友。忽然，正当他就要走过去时，她转向他，低声说：

「你若愿意，我就同你去散一回步；你只管先走，我过一分钟就跟上来。」

他定定地看了她片刻。他看出第一眼欺骗了他；她的脸并非像他料想的那样因酒而红，而是焕发着最精致的颜色：一朵红焰在她颊上亮起又隐去，说话时仿佛还在悸动。她的头颅端凝地安在颈项上，宛如一尊雕像；耳畔的青铜色头发散作小小的鬈圈。她微笑着，等候他的回答。

他咕哝了一句什么抱歉的话，便顺着山坡逃下了那场狂欢，逃开嘶吼人声的喧嚣，逃开疾风里极缓摇摆的巨灯的辉光。他知道他已经触到了彻底荒凉的边缘；那女人的脸上有死亡，她也确确实实在召唤他去赴巫魔夜会。不知怎的，他竟能当场回绝；可他知道，只要迟疑一下，他便会把自己交付给她，身与魂一并交付。他把自己锁进房间，躺在床上发抖，心里疑惑：莫非某种幽微的共感，向那女人显示出了她完美的同伴？他照了照镜子，如今已不指望看见什么确凿可见的外在表征，而是搜寻那点亮他眼睛的奇异眼色的含义。近几个月来他比先前更瘦了，双颊为饥饿与忧愁所消损；但他的眉目间仍留着一种古怪的古典风韵的意味，还有一种神色，像一头从葡萄园与橄榄园里走失的法翁。他已经挣脱开了，可此刻他感到她的罗网仍罩着他：对她的欲望是一种癫狂，仿佛她攥着他身体里的每一根神经，把他拖向她，拖向她那神秘的世界，拖向那一丛玫瑰——那里每一朵花都是一团火焰。

===Original===

He dreamed all night of the perilous things he had refused, and it was loss to awake in the morning, pain to return to the world. The frost had broken and the fog had rolled away, and the grey street was filled with a clear grey light. Again he looked out on the long dull sweep of the monotonous houses, hidden for the past weeks by a curtain of mist. Heavy rain had fallen in the night, and the garden rails were still dripping, the roofs still dark with wet, all down the line the dingy white blinds were drawn in the upper windows. Not a soul walked the street; everyone was asleep after the exertions of the night before; even on the main road it was only at intervals that some straggler paddled by. Presently a woman in a brown ulster shuffled off on some errand, then a man in shirtsleeves poked out his head, holding the door half-open, and stared up at a window opposite. After a few minutes he slunk in again, and three loafers came slouching down the street, eager for mischief or beastliness of some sort.
